```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

/-!
# Segment tests, their completion, and coordinate functionals
-/



open scoped BigOperators
open Finset

namespace SegmentLenses

abbrev InfiniteVertex := List ℕ

def ForestVertex := {p : ℕ × List ℕ // 1 ≤ p.1 ∧ p.2.length ≤ p.1}

def ForestAncestor (a b : ForestVertex) : Prop :=
  a.val.1 = b.val.1 ∧ a.val.2.IsPrefix b.val.2

def IsInterval {V : Type*} (ancestor : V → V → Prop) (S : Finset V) : Prop :=
  ∃ a b, ancestor a b ∧ ∀ v, v ∈ S ↔ ancestor a v ∧ ancestor v b

def Ancestral {V : Type*} (ancestor : V → V → Prop) (H : Finset V) : Prop :=
  ∀ ⦃a b⦄, b ∈ H → ancestor a b → a ∈ H

class SegmentSystem (V : Type*) where
  isSegment : Finset V → Prop
  singleton : ∀ v, isSegment {v}

structure SegmentFamily (V : Type*) [SegmentSystem V] where
  sets : Finset (Finset V)
  segment : ∀ S ∈ sets, SegmentSystem.isSegment S
  disjoint : (sets : Set (Finset V)).PairwiseDisjoint id

namespace SegmentFamily

variable {V : Type*} [SegmentSystem V]

def column (F : SegmentFamily V) (v : V) : EuclideanSpace ℝ F.sets :=
  by classical exact WithLp.toLp 2 (fun S => if v ∈ S.val then 1 else 0)

def test (F : SegmentFamily V) : (V →₀ ℝ) →ₗ[ℝ] EuclideanSpace ℝ F.sets :=
  Finsupp.linearCombination ℝ F.column

def seminorm (F : SegmentFamily V) : Seminorm ℝ (V →₀ ℝ) :=
  (normSeminorm ℝ (EuclideanSpace ℝ F.sets)).comp F.test

def singleton (v : V) : SegmentFamily V where
  sets := {{v}}
  segment := by
    intro S h
    have he : S = {v} := Finset.mem_singleton.mp h
    rw [he]
    exact SegmentSystem.singleton v
  disjoint := by
    intro S hS T hT hne
    simp only [Finset.mem_coe, Finset.mem_singleton] at hS hT
    exact False.elim (hne (hS.trans hT.symm))

end SegmentFamily

def segmentSeminorm (V : Type*) [SegmentSystem V] : Seminorm ℝ (V →₀ ℝ) :=
  ⨆ F : SegmentFamily V, F.seminorm

def Test (V : Type*) := V →₀ ℝ

namespace Test

variable {V : Type*}
instance : AddCommGroup (Test V) := inferInstanceAs (AddCommGroup (V →₀ ℝ))
instance : Module ℝ (Test V) := inferInstanceAs (Module ℝ (V →₀ ℝ))

def coeff (u : Test V) : V →₀ ℝ := u

def ofFinsupp (u : V →₀ ℝ) : Test V := u

variable [SegmentSystem V]
instance : Norm (Test V) where norm u := segmentSeminorm V u.coeff

instance : NormedAddCommGroup (Test V) := by
  have norm_column_le {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (F : SegmentFamily V) (v : V) : ‖F.column v‖ ≤ 1 := by
    classical
    by_cases hv : ∃ S ∈ F.sets, v ∈ S
    · obtain ⟨S, hS, hvS⟩ := hv
      have he : F.column v = PiLp.single 2 (⟨S, hS⟩ : F.sets) (1 : ℝ) := by
        ext T
        change (if v ∈ T.val then (1 : ℝ) else 0) = _
        by_cases hTS : T = ⟨S, hS⟩
        · subst T
          simp [hvS]
        · have hn : v ∉ T.val := by
            intro hvT
            have hne : T.val ≠ S := fun h => hTS (Subtype.ext h)
            exact Finset.disjoint_left.mp (F.disjoint T.property hS hne) hvT hvS
          simp [hTS, hn]
      rw [he, PiLp.norm_single]
      norm_num
    · have he : F.column v = 0 := by
        ext S
        have hn : v ∉ S.val := fun h => hv ⟨S.val, S.property, h⟩
        simp [SegmentFamily.column, hn]
      rw [he, norm_zero]
      norm_num
  have test_apply {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (F : SegmentFamily V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) (S : F.sets) :
      F.test u S = ∑ v ∈ S.val, u v := by
    classical
    simp only [SegmentFamily.test, Finsupp.linearCombination_apply, Finsupp.sum,
      WithLp.ofLp_sum, Finset.sum_apply, PiLp.smul_apply, SegmentFamily.column,
      smul_eq_mul]
    simp only [mul_ite, mul_one, mul_zero]
    rw [← Finset.sum_filter]
    apply Finset.sum_subset
    · intro v hv
      exact (Finset.mem_filter.mp hv).2
    · intro v hv hnv
      have hnot : v ∉ u.support := by
        intro hvs
        exact hnv (Finset.mem_filter.mpr ⟨hvs, hv⟩)
      exact Finsupp.notMem_support_iff.mp hnot
  have norm_test_le {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (F : SegmentFamily V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) :
      ‖F.test u‖ ≤ ∑ v ∈ u.support, |u v| := by
    classical
    rw [SegmentFamily.test, Finsupp.linearCombination_apply, Finsupp.sum]
    calc
      ‖∑ v ∈ u.support, u v • F.column v‖ ≤
          ∑ v ∈ u.support, ‖u v • F.column v‖ := norm_sum_le _ _
      _ ≤ ∑ v ∈ u.support, |u v| := by
        apply Finset.sum_le_sum
        intro v hv
        rw [norm_smul, Real.norm_eq_abs]
        exact mul_le_of_le_one_right (abs_nonneg _) (norm_column_le F v)
  have seminorm_sq {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (F : SegmentFamily V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) :
      F.seminorm u ^ 2 = ∑ S : F.sets, (∑ v ∈ S.val, u v) ^ 2 := by
    change ‖F.test u‖ ^ 2 = _
    rw [EuclideanSpace.real_norm_sq_eq]
    apply Finset.sum_congr rfl
    intro S _
    rw [test_apply]
  have singleton_seminorm {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (v : V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) :
      (SegmentFamily.singleton v).seminorm u = |u v| := by
    have h := seminorm_sq (SegmentFamily.singleton v) u
    have hh : (∑ S : (SegmentFamily.singleton v).sets, (∑ w ∈ S.val, u w) ^ 2) = u v ^ 2 := by
      exact (Finset.sum_coe_sort (SegmentFamily.singleton v).sets
        (fun S : Finset V => (∑ w ∈ S, u w) ^ 2)).trans (by simp [SegmentFamily.singleton])
    rw [hh] at h
    have hn := apply_nonneg (SegmentFamily.singleton v).seminorm u
    nlinarith [sq_abs (u v), abs_nonneg (u v)]
  have segmentTests_bdd (V : Type _) [SegmentSystem V] :
      BddAbove (Set.range (SegmentFamily.seminorm (V := V))) := by
    rw [Seminorm.bddAbove_iff]
    refine ⟨fun u => ∑ v ∈ u.support, |u v|, ?_⟩
    rintro _ ⟨p, ⟨F, rfl⟩, rfl⟩ u
    exact norm_test_le F u
  have le_segmentSeminorm {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V]
      (F : SegmentFamily V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) : F.seminorm u ≤ segmentSeminorm V u := by
    have h := le_ciSup (f := fun G : SegmentFamily V => G.seminorm) (segmentTests_bdd V) F
    exact Seminorm.le_def.mp h u
  have abs_coord_le {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (u : V →₀ ℝ) (v : V) :
      |u v| ≤ segmentSeminorm V u := by
    rw [← singleton_seminorm v u]
    exact le_segmentSeminorm _ _
  have segmentSeminorm_eq_zero {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (u : V →₀ ℝ) :
      segmentSeminorm V u = 0 ↔ u = 0 := by
    constructor
    · intro h
      ext v
      have hh := abs_coord_le u v
      rw [h] at hh
      simpa using hh
    · rintro rfl
      exact map_zero _
  exact NormedAddCommGroup.ofCore (𝕜 := ℝ) {
    norm_nonneg u := apply_nonneg (segmentSeminorm V) u.coeff
    norm_smul c u := map_smul_eq_mul (segmentSeminorm V) c u.coeff
    norm_triangle u v := map_add_le_add (segmentSeminorm V) u.coeff v.coeff
    norm_eq_zero_iff u := segmentSeminorm_eq_zero u.coeff }
instance : NormedSpace ℝ (Test V) where
  norm_smul_le c u := le_of_eq (map_smul_eq_mul (segmentSeminorm V) c u.coeff)

def evaluation (v : V) : Test V →L[ℝ] ℝ := by
  have norm_column_le {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (F : SegmentFamily V) (v : V) : ‖F.column v‖ ≤ 1 := by
    classical
    by_cases hv : ∃ S ∈ F.sets, v ∈ S
    · obtain ⟨S, hS, hvS⟩ := hv
      have he : F.column v = PiLp.single 2 (⟨S, hS⟩ : F.sets) (1 : ℝ) := by
        ext T
        change (if v ∈ T.val then (1 : ℝ) else 0) = _
        by_cases hTS : T = ⟨S, hS⟩
        · subst T
          simp [hvS]
        · have hn : v ∉ T.val := by
            intro hvT
            have hne : T.val ≠ S := fun h => hTS (Subtype.ext h)
            exact Finset.disjoint_left.mp (F.disjoint T.property hS hne) hvT hvS
          simp [hTS, hn]
      rw [he, PiLp.norm_single]
      norm_num
    · have he : F.column v = 0 := by
        ext S
        have hn : v ∉ S.val := fun h => hv ⟨S.val, S.property, h⟩
        simp [SegmentFamily.column, hn]
      rw [he, norm_zero]
      norm_num
  have test_apply {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (F : SegmentFamily V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) (S : F.sets) :
      F.test u S = ∑ v ∈ S.val, u v := by
    classical
    simp only [SegmentFamily.test, Finsupp.linearCombination_apply, Finsupp.sum,
      WithLp.ofLp_sum, Finset.sum_apply, PiLp.smul_apply, SegmentFamily.column,
      smul_eq_mul]
    simp only [mul_ite, mul_one, mul_zero]
    rw [← Finset.sum_filter]
    apply Finset.sum_subset
    · intro v hv
      exact (Finset.mem_filter.mp hv).2
    · intro v hv hnv
      have hnot : v ∉ u.support := by
        intro hvs
        exact hnv (Finset.mem_filter.mpr ⟨hvs, hv⟩)
      exact Finsupp.notMem_support_iff.mp hnot
  have norm_test_le {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (F : SegmentFamily V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) :
      ‖F.test u‖ ≤ ∑ v ∈ u.support, |u v| := by
    classical
    rw [SegmentFamily.test, Finsupp.linearCombination_apply, Finsupp.sum]
    calc
      ‖∑ v ∈ u.support, u v • F.column v‖ ≤
          ∑ v ∈ u.support, ‖u v • F.column v‖ := norm_sum_le _ _
      _ ≤ ∑ v ∈ u.support, |u v| := by
        apply Finset.sum_le_sum
        intro v hv
        rw [norm_smul, Real.norm_eq_abs]
        exact mul_le_of_le_one_right (abs_nonneg _) (norm_column_le F v)
  have seminorm_sq {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (F : SegmentFamily V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) :
      F.seminorm u ^ 2 = ∑ S : F.sets, (∑ v ∈ S.val, u v) ^ 2 := by
    change ‖F.test u‖ ^ 2 = _
    rw [EuclideanSpace.real_norm_sq_eq]
    apply Finset.sum_congr rfl
    intro S _
    rw [test_apply]
  have singleton_seminorm {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (v : V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) :
      (SegmentFamily.singleton v).seminorm u = |u v| := by
    have h := seminorm_sq (SegmentFamily.singleton v) u
    have hh : (∑ S : (SegmentFamily.singleton v).sets, (∑ w ∈ S.val, u w) ^ 2) = u v ^ 2 := by
      exact (Finset.sum_coe_sort (SegmentFamily.singleton v).sets
        (fun S : Finset V => (∑ w ∈ S, u w) ^ 2)).trans (by simp [SegmentFamily.singleton])
    rw [hh] at h
    have hn := apply_nonneg (SegmentFamily.singleton v).seminorm u
    nlinarith [sq_abs (u v), abs_nonneg (u v)]
  have segmentTests_bdd (V : Type _) [SegmentSystem V] :
      BddAbove (Set.range (SegmentFamily.seminorm (V := V))) := by
    rw [Seminorm.bddAbove_iff]
    refine ⟨fun u => ∑ v ∈ u.support, |u v|, ?_⟩
    rintro _ ⟨p, ⟨F, rfl⟩, rfl⟩ u
    exact norm_test_le F u
  have le_segmentSeminorm {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V]
      (F : SegmentFamily V) (u : V →₀ ℝ) : F.seminorm u ≤ segmentSeminorm V u := by
    have h := le_ciSup (f := fun G : SegmentFamily V => G.seminorm) (segmentTests_bdd V) F
    exact Seminorm.le_def.mp h u
  have abs_coord_le {V : Type _} [SegmentSystem V] (u : V →₀ ℝ) (v : V) :
      |u v| ≤ segmentSeminorm V u := by
    rw [← singleton_seminorm v u]
    exact le_segmentSeminorm _ _
  exact let f : Test V →ₗ[ℝ] ℝ :=
      { toFun := fun u => u.coeff v
        map_add' := fun _ _ => rfl
        map_smul' := fun _ _ => rfl }
    f.mkContinuous 1 (fun u => by
      change |u.coeff v| ≤ 1 * segmentSeminorm V u.coeff
      simpa only [one_mul] using abs_coord_le u.coeff v)

end Test

abbrev Primal (V : Type*) [SegmentSystem V] := UniformSpace.Completion (Test V)

abbrev FullDual (V : Type*) [SegmentSystem V] := StrongDual ℝ (Primal V)

def unitVector {V : Type*} [SegmentSystem V] (v : V) : Primal V :=
  (Test.ofFinsupp (Finsupp.single v 1) : Test V)

def coordinate {V : Type*} [SegmentSystem V] (v : V) : FullDual V :=
  (Test.evaluation v).extend UniformSpace.Completion.toComplL

def coordinateSpan (V : Type*) [SegmentSystem V] : Submodule ℝ (FullDual V) :=
  (Submodule.span ℝ (Set.range (coordinate (V := V)))).topologicalClosure

abbrev CoordinatePredual (V : Type*) [SegmentSystem V] : Type _ := ↥(coordinateSpan V)

def head {V : Type*} [SegmentSystem V] (H : Finset V) (x : FullDual V) : FullDual V :=
  ∑ v ∈ H, x (unitVector v) • coordinate v

def tail {V : Type*} [SegmentSystem V] (H : Finset V) (x : FullDual V) : FullDual V :=
  x - head H x

instance infiniteSegments : SegmentSystem InfiniteVertex where
  isSegment := IsInterval List.IsPrefix
  singleton v := by
    refine ⟨v, v, List.prefix_rfl, ?_⟩
    intro w
    simp only [Finset.mem_singleton]
    constructor
    · rintro rfl; exact ⟨List.prefix_rfl, List.prefix_rfl⟩
    · rintro ⟨hvw, hwv⟩
      exact hwv.eq_of_length (Nat.le_antisymm hwv.length_le hvw.length_le)

instance forestSegments : SegmentSystem ForestVertex where
  isSegment := IsInterval ForestAncestor
  singleton v := by
    refine ⟨v, v, ⟨rfl, List.prefix_rfl⟩, ?_⟩
    intro w
    simp only [Finset.mem_singleton]
    constructor
    · rintro rfl; exact ⟨⟨rfl, List.prefix_rfl⟩, ⟨rfl, List.prefix_rfl⟩⟩
    · rintro ⟨hvw, hwv⟩
      exact Subtype.ext (Prod.ext hwv.1 (hwv.2.eq_of_length (Nat.le_antisymm hwv.2.length_le hvw.2.length_le)))

abbrev J := Primal ForestVertex

abbrev X := FullDual ForestVertex

abbrev JInfinite := Primal InfiniteVertex

abbrev BInfinite := CoordinatePredual InfiniteVertex

theorem midpoint_lens_main :
    (∀ (H : Finset ForestVertex) (_ : Ancestral ForestAncestor H)
      (x y : X) (_ : x = head H x) (R : ℝ) (_ : 0 ≤ R)
      (_ : ‖x + y‖ ≤ R) (_ : ‖x - y‖ ≤ R),
      ‖tail H y‖ ≤ 2 * Real.sqrt (R ^ 2 - ‖x‖ ^ 2)) ∧
    (∀ (H : Finset InfiniteVertex) (_ : Ancestral List.IsPrefix H)
      (x y : BInfinite) (_ : x.val = head H x.val) (R : ℝ) (_ : 0 ≤ R)
      (_ : ‖x + y‖ ≤ R) (_ : ‖x - y‖ ≤ R),
      ‖tail H y.val‖ ≤ 2 * Real.sqrt (R ^ 2 - ‖x‖ ^ 2)) :=
  sorry

end SegmentLenses

end

end OAI
```

```json
    "family": "331",
    "text": "The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):\n\n- [Asymptotic midpoint uniform convexity and unbounded diamond distortion in a reflexive tree space](../../preprints/Asymptotic-midpoint-uniform-convexity-and-unbounded-diamond-distortion-in-a-reflexive-tree-space-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Midpoint lenses in segment spaces](../../preprints/Midpoint-lenses-in-segment-spaces-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Distortion of countably branching diamonds from midpoint and tree energies](../../preprints/Diamond-distortion-from-midpoint-and-tree-energies-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Exact asymptotic moduli in a Daugavet subspace of $L_1$](../../preprints/Exact-asymptotic-moduli-in-a-Daugavet-subspace-of-L1-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Midpoint convexity from bounded tree potentials and path costs](../../preprints/Midpoint-convexity-from-bounded-tree-potentials-and-path-costs-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Independent products in real $L_1$: asymptotic midpoint convexity without AUC renormings](../../preprints/Independent-products-in-real-L1-asymptotic-midpoint-convexity-without-AUC-renormings-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n- [Midpoint convexity from two recursive potentials](../../preprints/Midpoint-convexity-from-two-recursive-potentials-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)\n\n## Scope\n\nThe paper constructs a Banach space whose midpoint geometry is asymptotically uniformly convex even though no equivalent norm is asymptotically uniformly convex in the usual one-sided sense. The formalization proves that the full dual of the specified segment-norm completion is infinite-dimensional, separable, and reflexive; its averaged midpoint modulus is at least $\\sqrt{1+t^2/12}-1$ for every $t>0$, while every equivalent norm fails asymptotic uniform convexity. It also proves that an embedding of a depth-$k$ countably branching diamond has distortion at least $\\sqrt{1+k/12}$.\n\nThe general-forest and word-forest results use the closed span of the coordinate functionals. Their conclusions retain the finite-ancestor and equivalent-norm hypotheses, including the scale $\\alpha/(2\\beta)$ when $0<\\alpha\\le\\beta$ and the new norm lies between $\\alpha$ and $\\beta$ times the original norm; they do not identify that span with the full dual or assert reflexivity for every forest.\n\nThe paper controls midpoint lenses in Banach spaces defined by tree-segment norms. The formalization proves the following estimate in both the full dual of the finite-height forest space and the coordinate predual of the infinite-tree space. If $x$ is supported on a finite ancestral set $H$, $R\\ge0$, and $\\|x+y\\|,\\|x-y\\|\\le R$, then the part of $y$ outside $H$ has norm at most $2\\sqrt{R^2-\\|x\\|^2}$.\n\nThe formalization also covers selected consequences for averaged midpoint moduli and separated families of vectors, together with reflexivity and renorming obstructions. This scope concerns the segment-space results; the companion results on diamond energies remain separate.\n\nThe paper studies embeddings of countably branching diamond graphs into Banach spaces built from tree segments. The formalization proves that every embedding of the depth-$k$ diamond with distortion $D$, at any positive scale, satisfies $1+k/4\\le D^2$ in either of two spaces: the full dual of the completed finite-height forest space and the closed coordinate span in the dual of the infinite-tree space. The bound applies to all pairs of vertices and is unconditional.\n\nThe formalization also contains selected graph deductions under explicit companion assumptions. Twenty-two of those companion inputs are assumed in these deductions; the diamond distortion bound above does not require them.\n\nThe paper exhibits a real $L_1$ subspace with positive averaged midpoint convexity but no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. For every closed infinite-dimensional subspace whose unit ball is precompact in measure and which has the Daugavet property, the formalization computes the moduli at every unit vector: the averaged midpoint modulus is $\\max(t/2,t-1)$ and the usual one-sided asymptotic modulus is $\\max(0,t-2)$ for every $t>0$. The same formulas hold after taking the infimum over unit vectors, and every equivalent norm fails asymptotic uniform convexity.\n\nThe formalization also constructs such a subspace on a countable product of unit intervals and proves that it has the stated measure-precompactness and Daugavet properties, so the modulus formulas apply to an actual example.\n\nThe paper constructs Banach spaces from bounded tree potentials and path costs to separate averaged midpoint convexity from asymptotic uniform convexity. For the specified tree-potential completions, the formalization proves completeness, separability, infinite dimension, and an averaged midpoint modulus of at least $\\sqrt{1+t^2/4}-1$ for $0<t<1$. No space linearly isomorphic to one of these completions is asymptotically uniformly convex.\n\nThe formalization also covers selected path-cost duality and comparison results, clipping and energy estimates, and further renorming obstructions. These retain their stated support, head, and tail hypotheses; the remaining auxiliary assertions of the paper are outside this scope.\n\nThe paper forms a real $L_1$ space from the closed span of products of independent multipliers along tree paths. For a positive, nonconstant multiplier of mean one and finite second moment, the formalization proves that this space is infinite-dimensional and has no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm.\n\nFor every $t>0$, the averaged midpoint modulus nevertheless has explicit positive lower bounds. If $G$ is a standard real Gaussian, the exponential-multiplier bound is $\\frac{7t}{480}\\mathbb E[(|G|-20/t)_+]$, where $u_+=\\max(u,0)$. For squared-Gaussian multipliers, the bounds are $\\frac18\\Pr(|G|\\ge4\\sqrt2(2+\\sqrt2)/t)$ and $\\frac14\\Pr(|G|\\ge8\\sqrt6/t)$.\n\nThe paper constructs three Banach spaces from recursive tree potentials: a root-sum space, a zero-root space, and a joined space. The formalization proves that all three are infinite-dimensional and have no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. The root-sum and joined spaces have averaged midpoint modulus at least $t^3/128$ for $0<t<1$, and the root-sum modulus is positive for every $t>0$. The zero-root estimate retains its finite ancestral support and tail hypotheses, and the zero-root and joined spaces are reflexive.\n\nThe formalization also covers a separated-midpoint result for the joined space and a variable-exponent model with its recursive norm, duality, projections, renorming obstruction, and sixth- and third-order stability estimates. The separation, sequence, support, and head assumptions in these results are essential parts of their statements."
  },

}
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For a real segment-forest dual with unbounded finite component heights and the real infinite-height coordinate predual, we bound the tail of an arbitrary displacement in a symmetric lens by $`2\sqrt{R^2-\|x\|^2}`$, where <i>R</i> is the lens radius and <i>x</i> is its finitely supported center. Both given norms are asymptotically midpoint uniformly convex, although neither space admits an equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm. The finite-height forest dual is reflexive.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For a normed space $Y$, a vector $x\in Y$, and $R>0$, consider the symmetric lens of displacements $$\mathcal L(x,R)=\{y\in Y:\|x+y\|\le R,\ \|x-y\|\le R\}.$$ When $\|x\|$ is close to $R$, convexity restricts this set. In an infinite-dimensional space one can ask for smallness after projecting away finitely many coordinates. We prove such an estimate for a particular tree norm, with a bound determined by the squared-radius deficit $R^2-\|x\|^2$ of the center.

Approximate-midpoint lenses are central to asymptotic midpoint uniform convexity: Dilworth, Kutzarova, Randrianarivony, Revalski, and Zhivkov characterized that property in terms of the measure of noncompactness of suitable lenses (Dilworth et al. 2016, Theorem 2.1 and Definition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)]. Their framework concerns finite-codimensional control of symmetric displacements. The estimate here gives explicit control outside each finite ancestral set for the specified norm. Its proof uses disjoint tree segments, following the norm construction introduced by James (James 1974, 739) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[James1974](#ref-James1974)].

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The space and the tail estimate

<a id="eq:segment-norm"></a>
For each integer $h\ge1$, let $T_h=\mathbb N^{\le h}$ be the rooted tree of words of length at most $h$, ordered by extension of words. Its root is the empty word. Keep separate copies of these trees in the forest $$\mathcal F=\coprod_{h\ge1}(\{h\}\times T_h).$$ Write $a\preceq b$ if $a$ is an ancestor of $b$ in the same component. A *segment* is a nonempty interval $[a,b]=\{v:a\preceq v\preceq b\}$ in an ancestral chain. For a finitely supported real function $u$ on $\mathcal F$, set $u(S)=\sum_{v\in S}u(v)$ and define $$\label{eq:segment-norm}
 \rho(u)=\sup\left\{\left(\sum_{i=1}^m u(S_i)^2\right)^{1/2}:
  m\ge1,\ S_1,\ldots,S_m\text{ pairwise disjoint segments}\right\}.$$ The Euclidean triangle inequality and singleton segments show that $\rho$ is a norm. Let $J$ be its completion and let $X=J^*$, with the dual norm denoted by $\|\cdot\|$. We write $e_v\in J$ for a coordinate unit vector. Singleton tests give $|u(v)|\le\rho(u)$, so coordinate evaluation extends to an element $e_v^*\in X$. The construction in <a href="#eq:segment-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:segment-norm">[eq:segment-norm]</a> uses James’s square-sum norm on disjoint segments (James 1974, 739) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[James1974](#ref-James1974)]; the branching and component heights here differ from the classical binary tree.

The second model uses the infinite tree $T_\infty=\mathbb N^{<\omega}$, including its root. Let $J_\infty$ be the completion for the same disjoint-segment norm, and let $$B_\infty=\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{e_v^*:v\in T_\infty\}
 \subset J_\infty^*.$$ This closure uses the dual norm. It is the coordinate predual in the Ghoussoub–Maurey–Schachermayer construction presented by Banakh (Banakh 2000, sec. 2.II) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Banakh2000](#ref-Banakh2000)]. Section <a href="#binf:definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:definition">3</a> proves its finite-coordinate and atomic descriptions separately. Perreau asked whether the canonical predual of the countably branching James tree space is asymptotically midpoint uniformly convex (Perreau 2021, Question 6, p. 23 of arXiv v1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Perreau2021](#ref-Perreau2021)]. This question motivates our study of the infinite-height coordinate predual $B_\infty$ with the real norm specified here; the finite-height forest is a separate model.

A finite set $H$ is *ancestral* if it contains every ancestor of its vertices. On either coordinate space write $$P_Hx=\sum_{v\in H}x(e_v)e_v^*,\qquad Q_Hx=x-P_Hx.$$ Both projections are contractive. The displacement below may have a nonzero head.

<a id="stmt-mol-prop-joint-budget"></a>
<div id="mol:prop:joint-budget" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *<span id="thm:main-tail" label="thm:main-tail"></span>Let $Y$ be either the finite-height forest dual $X=J^*$ or the infinite-height coordinate predual $B_\infty$. For every finite ancestral $H$, every $x,y\in Y$ with $x=P_Hx$, and every $R\ge0$ with $\|x+y\|,\|x-y\|\le R$, <a id="eq:main-squared-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:main-squared-tail}
 \|Q_Hy\|\le2\sqrt{R^2-\|x\|^2}.$$*

</div>

The same head works for every displacement in the lens. This is the feature needed when a single center has infinitely many possible midpoints. Corollary <a href="#mol:cor:all-radius-averaged" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mol:cor:all-radius-averaged">10</a> gives an explicit averaged asymptotic midpoint modulus for both models.

For comparison with the tree-space history, Girardi proved asymptotic uniform convexity (AUC) of the canonical predual and full dual of the classical binary James tree space (Girardi 2001, Theorems 3 and 5 of arXiv v1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Girardi2001](#ref-Girardi2001)]. Our finite-height model uses countably branching trees with finite, unbounded heights. The local cancellation estimate in this paper is proved for those specified segment tests; it is not imported from the binary-tree result.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The mechanism of the proof

Section <a href="#sec:forest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:forest">2</a> proves the coordinate structure locally, so no companion theorem is needed to read the argument. The tail splits into an $\ell_2$ sum over the descendant subtrees starting just outside a finite ancestral set.

In Section <a href="#sec:atoms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:atoms">4</a>, we represent points of each finite head unit ball by convex combinations of disjoint segment functionals. A functional nearly norming the center makes the atom coefficients close to its sums over the same segments. The difficulty is cancellation: two represented atoms can have opposite sums when they leave the head at the same vertex. Their head segments are nested. Splitting the longer one improves its squared segment test, so the opposite-sign product is bounded by the amount that the two tests fall short of one. Section <a href="#sec:cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cancellation">5</a> proves this crossing comparison.

The two endpoint distributions can be combined with a random sign. Keeping the coefficient error and the unused head-test energy in one budget gives Theorem <a href="#thm:main-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-tail">[thm:main-tail]</a>, proved in Section <a href="#mol:sec:joint-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mol:sec:joint-budget">6</a>. The same head controls every displacement, so finite-coordinate approximation yields an asymptotic midpoint modulus uniformly over all centers.

The obstruction to an equivalent AUC norm uses a different feature of the spaces. Every root-path functional has norm one, while its child increments are unit coordinate vectors and are weakly null. A positive one-sided asymptotic modulus would permit successive children whose path norms grow by a fixed factor. This contradicts the uniform bound on path norms. The forest supplies arbitrarily long finite paths; the infinite tree also permits an argument using the supremum of all path norms. Sections <a href="#sec:coordinate-variants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coordinate-variants">8</a> and <a href="#sec:infinite-obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:infinite-obstruction">9</a> prove these assertions for the two completions separately.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The other quantities measured by the methods

A second proof method works directly with primal tests. Clipping their cumulative sums along each exterior path produces two perturbations with a quadratic norm cost. Testing the two endpoints on these perturbations controls their average. Corollary <a href="#clip:paired-modulus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:paired-modulus">21</a> gives the resulting uniform gain for perturbations in a suitable finite-codimensional subspace. The construction and its root-shift variants occupy Sections <a href="#clip:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:section">10</a>–<a href="#sec:primal-additions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:primal-additions">11</a>.

The later atomic arguments compare what survives when one changes the normalization or retains more of the original representation. A common head test can control several endpoint distributions with unequal weights or a nonzero center tail. A bound on their mixture does not automatically give the same bound on each conditional law. Proposition <a href="#prop:endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:endpoint">29</a>, for example, records separate coefficient errors and boundary masses. Some alternative calculations give weaker geometric constants: the radial bound $(\sqrt2+\sqrt6)\sqrt{R(R-\|x\|)}$ of Proposition <a href="#thm:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tail">27</a> estimates the two errors separately, while the original-product route incurs a fourth-root loss. These calculations record the losses of their respective methods. Sign selection and maximal-gain trimming instead isolate which crossing contributions can be retained with a common sign.

These estimates have metric consequences. The companion *Distortion of countably branching diamonds from midpoint and tree energies* (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DiamondEnergies](#ref-DiamondEnergies)] proves that Theorem <a href="#thm:main-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-tail">[thm:main-tail]</a> forces $D^2\ge1+k/4$ for embeddings of the depth-$k$ countably branching normalized diamond. That graph starts from an edge of length one and replaces each edge by countably many two-edge paths of the same total length; distortion means a common ratio between upper and lower all-pairs Lipschitz factors. The companion proves all infinite-family selections and graph recurrences, and also preserves the independent radial-deficit route and direct energy methods.

The broader question whether asymptotic midpoint uniform convexity implies an equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm already has a negative answer. Baudier proved midpoint convexity for the Kadets–Werner modification of the Bourgain–Rosenthal space, which has no equivalent AUC norm (Baudier 2026, Theorem 1 and Corollary 1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Baudier2026](#ref-Baudier2026)]. Here the finite-height forest is reflexive, and the infinite-height coordinate predual is a different completion. Their exact identification statements and local estimates are part of the proofs below. The numerical bounds are not claims of optimality or priority.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Reading guide.

Sections <a href="#sec:forest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:forest">2</a>–<a href="#mol:sec:joint-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mol:sec:joint-budget">6</a> establish the coordinate foundations and the main lens theorem. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">7</a> derives the midpoint modulus; the bounded-path arguments in Sections <a href="#sec:coordinate-variants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coordinate-variants">8</a>– <a href="#sec:infinite-obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:infinite-obstruction">9</a> complete the principal geometric conclusions. Sections <a href="#clip:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:section">10</a>–<a href="#sec:primal-additions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:primal-additions">11</a> then develop the independent primal method, from cumulative clipping to root shifts and bounded separated families.

The remaining sections study atomic representations in more detail. Sections <a href="#sec:radial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:radial">12</a>–<a href="#sec:joint-consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:joint-consequences">13</a> retain radial and conditional estimates. Sections <a href="#presentation:constructions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="presentation:constructions">14</a>– <a href="#stab:toolkit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:toolkit">15</a> give the gauge and polar descriptions and the common finite-test estimates. Sections <a href="#stab:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:section">16</a>–<a href="#stab:trim:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:trim:section">22</a> use them to compare weighted endpoint laws, original coefficients and sign choices, with the model specified in each statement. Sections <a href="#sec:sequential-forest" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sequential-forest">23</a>–<a href="#sec:qualitative-infinite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:qualitative-infinite">24</a> finish with qualitative arguments and their distinct normalization procedures.

<a id="sec:forest"></a>
### The coordinate structure of the forest

We first establish the structural facts needed to separate a vector into finitely many coordinates and its complement. The finite heights give reflexivity and coordinate density; the ancestral order gives contractive projections and an exact norm formula on the complementary subtrees.

For a finite set $H$, the coordinate projections are $P_Hu=\sum_{v\in H}u(v)e_v$ on $J$ and $P_Hx=\sum_{v\in H}x(e_v)e_v^*$ on $X$; write $Q_H=I-P_H$.

<a id="stmt-lem-forest-structure"></a>
<div id="lem:forest-structure" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *The space $X$ defined by <a href="#eq:segment-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:segment-norm">[eq:segment-norm]</a> is infinite dimensional, separable, and reflexive, and the coordinate functionals have dense span in $X$. Let $H\subset\mathcal F$ be finite and *ancestral*, meaning that it contains every ancestor of each of its vertices. The coordinate projections $P_H$ and $Q_H$ are contractive on both $J$ and $X$. Moreover, $P_Hx\to x$ along any increasing ancestral exhaustion of $\mathcal F$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-forest-structure"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-forest-structure</summary>

*Proof.* Singleton tests give $\|u\|_2\le\rho(u)$. On a component of height $h$, each segment has at most $h+1$ vertices. Thus Cauchy–Schwarz and disjointness give $$\|u\|_2\le\rho(u)\le\sqrt{h+1}\|u\|_2.$$ Write $J_h$ for the completion on that component. Segment families from different components combine freely, so $$J=\left(\bigoplus_{h\ge1}J_h\right)_{\ell_2}.$$ Each $J_h$ is isomorphic to Hilbert space. The dual of an $\ell_2$ sum is isometrically the $\ell_2$ sum of the duals: Cauchy–Schwarz proves one inequality, and testing on almost norming unit vectors in finitely many components, with proportional weights, proves the other. Applying this identity twice proves reflexivity. One may also pass from reflexivity of $J$ to that of its dual through the canonical maps. Write $\iota_J:J\to J^{**}$ and $\iota_X:X\to X^{**}$. For $\Phi\in X^{**}$, define $x=\Phi\circ\iota_J\in J^*=X$. Every $f\in X^*=J^{**}$ is $\iota_J(p)$ for some $p\in J$, so $$(\iota_Xx)(f)=f(x)=x(p)=\Phi(\iota_J(p))=\Phi(f).$$ Thus $\iota_X$ is onto. In particular $X^*=J$ canonically. If the coordinate functionals did not have dense span in $X$, Hahn–Banach would give a nonzero element of $X^*=J$ annihilating them. All its coordinates would vanish, a contradiction. Countability of $\mathcal F$ now proves separability; the independent coordinates prove infinite dimensionality.

A segment meets $H$ in a prefix and its complement in a suffix. Restricting disjoint segment tests to either set therefore proves contractivity on $J$. The adjoints give precisely the coordinate projections on $X$, and remain contractive. Finally, approximate $x$ by a finite coordinate combination. Once $H$ contains its support, contractivity bounds the discarded tail of $x$ by that approximation error. This proves convergence along an ancestral exhaustion. ◻

</details>

Let $G_H$ be the set of minimal vertices outside $H$. We call these vertices *gates*. They include roots of components disjoint from $H$. Distinct gates are incomparable, and the descendant sets $U_g=\{v:g\preceq v\}$ partition $\mathcal F\setminus H$. Each segment crossing from $H$ to its complement passes through one gate. Let $J(U_g)$ denote the coordinate subspace of $J$ supported in $U_g$, with its inherited norm.

<a id="stmt-lem-cones"></a>
<div id="lem:cones" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cone-decomposition"></a>
**Lemma 3**. *For every finite ancestral $H$, $$\label{eq:cone-decomposition}
 Q_HJ=\left(\bigoplus_{g\in G_H}J(U_g)\right)_{\ell_2},
 \qquad
 \|Q_Hx\|^2=\sum_{g\in G_H}\|x|_{J(U_g)}\|^2\quad(x\in X).$$ The gate-coordinate map $x\mapsto(x(e_g))_{g\in G_H}$ is a contraction from $X$ to $\ell_2(G_H)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cones"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cones</summary>

*Proof.* On a vector supported outside $H$, every segment test reduces to a segment in one cone. Grouping a disjoint family by its gates gives the upper estimate for the first identity; combining almost maximizing families from finitely many distinct cones gives the reverse estimate. Pass to the completion. A functional supported outside $H$ has exactly the norm of its restriction to $Q_HJ$, because $Q_H$ is contractive. Duality for the $\ell_2$ sum therefore proves the second identity.

A segment contains at most one gate, so $\rho(u)=\|u\|_2$ for vectors supported on $G_H$. Testing $x$ on such finite vectors proves the gate-coordinate contraction. ◻

</details>

<a id="binf:definition"></a>
### The infinite-height coordinate predual

The finite-height forest and the infinite tree have the same finite segment tests, but their completions must be specified separately. Here we give the infinite-tree construction and the precise finite-head facts needed by the subsequent arguments.

<a id="binf:polar"></a>
Let $T_\infty=\mathbb N^{<\omega}$, with root $o$, ordered by extension of finite words. On $c_{00}(T_\infty)$ use the real segment norm $$\rho(u)=\sup_{\mathcal S}
 \left(\sum_{S\in\mathcal S}u(S)^2\right)^{1/2},
 \qquad u(S)=\sum_{v\in S}u(v),$$ where $\mathcal S$ ranges over finite families of pairwise disjoint nonempty segments. Singleton tests and the Euclidean triangle inequality show that this is a norm, with $\|u\|_2\le\rho(u)\le\|u\|_1$. Let $J_\infty$ be its completion and define $$B_\infty=\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{e_v^*:v\in T_\infty\}
 \subset J_\infty^*.$$ The closure and norm in this definition are those of $J_\infty^*$. This is the coordinate predual described in (Banakh 2000, sec. 2.II) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Banakh2000](#ref-Banakh2000)]. On finite coefficient vectors its norm is $$\label{binf:polar}
 \left\|\sum_v a_v e_v^*\right\|
 =\sup\left\{\left|\sum_v a_v u(v)\right|:
                 u\in c_{00}(T_\infty),\ \rho(u)\le1\right\}.$$ Indeed finite tests are dense in $J_\infty$, with rescaling permitting approximation within its unit ball. Consequently completing the finite coefficient vectors in <a href="#binf:polar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="binf:polar">[binf:polar]</a> gives exactly $B_\infty$. In particular $\|a\|_\infty\le\|a\|\le\|a\|_2$ for finite coefficient arrays, and every coordinate functional has norm one, by testing on the corresponding primal coordinate vector. For any $x\in B_\infty$, the same supremum computes $\|x\|$: each test is continuous, and the supremum is a $1$-Lipschitz function of $x$ agreeing with its norm on the dense coordinate span. We use only this closed coordinate span, without identifying it with the full dual $J_\infty^*$.

<a id="stmt-binf-projections"></a>
<div id="binf:projections" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Finite heads and their complements). *Let $H\subset T_\infty$ be finite and ancestral. The coordinate projections $P_H$ and $Q_H=I-P_H$ are contractive on $J_\infty$ and $B_\infty$. The range of $P_H$ is finite dimensional, and finite coefficient vectors supported outside $H$ are dense in $\ker P_H$. Along every increasing ancestral exhaustion, $P_Hx\to x$ in $B_\infty$. A nonzero vector $x=P_Hx$ has a norming test $f=P_Hf$ with $\rho(f)=1$ and $x(f)=\|x\|$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-binf-projections"><summary>Proof of stmt-binf-projections</summary>

*Proof.* A segment intersects $H$ in a prefix and its complement in a suffix. Restricting every member of a disjoint testing family proves the primal contractions. Their adjoints preserve the coordinate span, hence its closure, and give the asserted dual contractions. Applying $Q_H$ to finite approximants proves density in its range. An exhaustion eventually fixes each finite coefficient vector; contractivity extends convergence to the completion. Finally the norm of $x=P_Hx$ can be computed using only $P_HJ_\infty$, whose unit ball is compact. A maximizer is a unit test when $x\ne0$. ◻

</details>

For a segment $S\subset T_\infty$ write $d_S=\sum_{v\in S}e_v^*$. A represented atom is a finite sum $\sum_i a_i d_{S_i}$ with disjoint segments $S_i$ and $\sum_i a_i^2\le1$. Cauchy–Schwarz gives its norm at most one. The representation is part of the data when coefficients or crossings are subsequently examined.

<a id="stmt-binf-finite-hull"></a>
<div id="binf:finite-hull" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Finite polar hull and completed atomic ball). *For finite ancestral $H$, the unit ball of $P_HB_\infty$ is the convex hull of represented atoms with all segments in $H$. Each point is a convex combination of at most $|H|+1$ such atoms. The full unit ball of $B_\infty$ is the norm-closed convex hull of finite atoms.*

*On $P_HB_\infty$ the norm also equals the gauge $$\inf\left\{\sum_j\lambda_j:
   x=\sum_j\lambda_j A_j,\quad \lambda_j\ge0,\quad
   A_j\text{ atoms supported in }H\right\}.$$ In particular, a vector of norm strictly below one has such a finite representation with $\sum_j\lambda_j<1$. The gauge defined on $c_{00}(T_\infty)$ by allowing all finite atoms has the same value. Its completion is therefore isometric to $B_\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-binf-finite-hull"><summary>Proof of stmt-binf-finite-hull</summary>

*Proof.* There are finitely many disjoint segment families in $H$. For each family, its atoms form the continuous image of a Euclidean unit ball. Their union is compact, as is its finite-dimensional convex hull $C$. For a test $f=P_Hf$, the support function of $C$ is precisely $\rho(f)$, by optimizing the coefficients in each family. Finite-dimensional separation identifies $C$ with the dual unit ball of $P_HJ_\infty$; contractivity identifies this with the inherited ball of $P_HB_\infty$. Carathéodory’s theorem gives $|H|+1$ atoms. Equivalently, its elementary proof repeatedly removes a term from any longer convex combination using an affine dependence among its points, keeping the coefficients nonnegative and their sum one.

For the gauge assertion, every displayed representation bounds the norm by $\sum_j\lambda_j$. Conversely, write $x=r(x/r)$, where $r=\|x\|>0$, and represent $x/r\in C$; $x=0$ is immediate. For the global gauge, choose an ancestral $H$ containing the support of $x$. Projection $P_H$ sends every global atom to an atom in $H$: intersect its disjoint segments with $H$ and discard empty intersections. Thus every global representation projects to a local representation with no larger total weight. Local representations are already global ones, so the two gauges agree on every finite coefficient vector. The completion identification follows from <a href="#binf:polar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="binf:polar">[binf:polar]</a>.

For a completed unit vector, Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a> gives $P_Hx\to x$ with each $P_Hx$ in the finite atomic ball. Thus the norm-closed convex hull of all finite atoms is the completed unit ball. This last assertion is an approximation statement; the finite exact representations are used only after a finite projection. ◻

</details>

Let $G_H$ be the minimal vertices outside $H$, and let $U_g=\{v:g\preceq v\}$ for $g\in G_H$. When $H$ is empty the root is the sole gate.

<a id="stmt-binf-cones"></a>
<div id="binf:cones" class="lemma">

<a id="binf:primal-cones"></a>
<a id="binf:dual-cones"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Exterior square sums). *For a finite test $u=Q_Hu$, $$\label{binf:primal-cones}
 \rho(u)^2=\sum_{g\in G_H}\rho(u\mathbf1_{U_g})^2.$$ After completion, $Q_HJ_\infty=(\bigoplus_{g\in G_H}J_\infty(U_g))_{\ell_2}$. For $x\in B_\infty$, $$\label{binf:dual-cones}
 \|Q_Hx\|^2=\sum_{g\in G_H}\|x|_{J_\infty(U_g)}\|^2.$$ The map $x\mapsto(x(e_g))_{g\in G_H}$ is a contraction to $\ell_2(G_H)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-binf-cones"><summary>Proof of stmt-binf-cones</summary>

*Proof.* Each exterior part of a segment lies in one cone. Grouping tests by cones gives one inequality in <a href="#binf:primal-cones" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="binf:primal-cones">[binf:primal-cones]</a>; joining nearly maximizing disjoint families from the finitely many nonzero cones gives the other. Completion gives the displayed primal sum. The norm of $Q_Hx$ is exactly that of its restriction to $Q_HJ_\infty$, by contractivity. The dual norm of an $\ell_2$ sum is the $\ell_2$ sum of the restriction norms: Cauchy–Schwarz proves the upper bound, and finitely many almost norming tests with proportional weights give the lower bound. This proves <a href="#binf:dual-cones" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="binf:dual-cones">[binf:dual-cones]</a> without any assertion that $B_\infty$ is the full dual. Finally a segment meets the antichain $G_H$ in at most one point, so tests supported there have precisely their Euclidean norm. Testing $x$ on them gives the contraction. The same argument shows that $x\mapsto(x(e_v))_{v\in A}$ is a contraction to $\ell_2(A)$ for every antichain $A$, not only for a gate set. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:atoms"></a>
### Finite convex representations

We next describe the finite-dimensional dual balls. Exact finite representations will let us perform the cancellation calculation with finite probability spaces, postponing the only limit until a coordinate exhaustion at the end.

<a id="eq:atom-coefficients"></a>
For a segment $S$, write $d_S=\sum_{v\in S}e_v^*$. By <a href="#eq:segment-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:segment-norm">[eq:segment-norm]</a>, $$\label{eq:atom-coefficients}
 \left\|\sum_i a_i d_{S_i}\right\|
 \le\left(\sum_i a_i^2\right)^{1/2}
 \qquad(S_i\text{ pairwise disjoint}).$$ Indeed, evaluate on a vector $u$ with $\rho(u)\le1$ and apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the coefficient vector and the segment sums. Call such a represented functional an *atom* when $\sum_i a_i^2\le1$. The representation, including the segment family, is part of the data.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-hull"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-hull" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *For a finite ancestral set $K$, the unit ball of $P_KX$ is the convex hull of the atoms whose segments lie in $K$. Each member is a convex combination of at most $|K|+1$ such atoms. If $0\ne x=P_Hx$ for a finite ancestral $H$, there is $p=P_Hp\in J$ with $\rho(p)=1$ and $x(p)=\|x\|$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-hull"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-hull</summary>

*Proof.* There are finitely many disjoint segment families in $K$. For each, its atoms are the continuous image of a Euclidean unit ball, so the union is compact. Its convex hull is compact in the finite-dimensional space $P_KX$. For a test $p$ supported in $K$, optimizing the atom coefficients shows that the support function of this convex hull is $\rho(p)$. In computing $\rho(p)$, all segment families can be restricted to $K$. Finite-dimensional separation therefore identifies the hull with the dual unit ball on that coordinate space. By contractivity of $P_K$, this is precisely the inherited unit ball of $P_KX$.

To obtain the stated bound on the number of atoms, consider a convex representation with more than $|K|+1$ positive weights. Its atoms are affinely dependent, so there are real numbers $\theta_i$, not all zero, with $\sum_i\theta_i=0$ and $\sum_i\theta_i A_i=0$. Vary the weights along this direction until the first weight reaches zero; all weights remain nonnegative, and both their sum and their represented vector are unchanged. Repeating leaves at most $|K|+1$ terms.

Likewise, for $x=P_Hx$ the dual pairing may be restricted to $P_HJ$ without altering its norm. That space is finite dimensional, so its compact unit ball contains a norming test, of norm one when $x\ne0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-closed-atomic-ball"></a>
<div id="cor:closed-atomic-ball" class="corollary">

**Corollary 8** (The completed atomic ball). *Let $\mathcal A$ be the set of all represented atoms in $X$. Then $$B_X=\overline{\operatorname{conv}\mathcal A}^{\,\|\cdot\|}.$$ Every segment functional $d_S$ has norm one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-closed-atomic-ball"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-closed-atomic-ball</summary>

*Proof.* The coefficient estimate puts every atom in $B_X$. Conversely, for $x\in B_X$, choose an increasing finite ancestral exhaustion $(K_n)$. Contractivity gives $P_{K_n}x\in B_X$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a> puts each $P_{K_n}x$ in $\operatorname{conv}\mathcal A$. The convergence $P_{K_n}x\to x$ proves the reverse inclusion. For a nonempty segment $S$, its functional has norm at most one by the coefficient estimate, and evaluation on $e_v$, for any $v\in S$, gives equality: the singleton vector $e_v$ has $\rho(e_v)=1$.

There is also a direct separation proof of the completed hull identity. Optimizing the atom coefficients gives $\sup_{A\in\mathcal A}A(p)=\rho(p)$ for every $p\in J$. Since $X^*=J$, separation of any point of $B_X$ from the norm-closed convex hull would give $x(p)>\rho(p)$, contradicting $\|x\|\le1$. This second proof uses reflexivity; the finite-projection proof only uses the coordinate completion and its contractive exhaustion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:cancellation"></a>
### Cancellation at the boundary

We can now compare the segment coefficients of two endpoint laws. The first step depends only on the head test, not on the original atom coefficients. It explains why opposite crossing sums cannot be large when both segment tests are nearly maximal.

For a finite ancestral $H$, a segment *crosses at $g\in G_H$* if it meets $H$ and its first vertex outside $H$ is $g$. For a disjoint segment family $\mathcal S$ and a test $p=P_Hp$ with $\rho(p)\le1$, put $$L_{\mathcal S}=1-\sum_{S\in\mathcal S}p(S)^2,\qquad
 b_{\mathcal S}(g)=
 \begin{cases}
 p(S),&S\in\mathcal S\text{ crosses at }g,\\
 0,&\text{no segment of }\mathcal S\text{ crosses at }g.
 \end{cases}$$ At most one segment of a disjoint family crosses at each gate, so this definition is unambiguous. Also $L_{\mathcal S}\ge0$.

<a id="stmt-lem-opposite-crossing"></a>
<div id="lem:opposite-crossing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:opposite-crossing"></a>
**Lemma 9**. *With the preceding notation, two disjoint segment families $\mathcal S,\mathcal S'$ satisfy $$\label{eq:opposite-crossing}
 2\sum_{g:\,b_{\mathcal S}(g)b_{\mathcal S'}(g)<0}
   |b_{\mathcal S}(g)b_{\mathcal S'}(g)|
 \le L_{\mathcal S}+L_{\mathcal S'}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-opposite-crossing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-opposite-crossing</summary>

*Proof.* At a gate with opposite nonzero sums $b,b'$, the two heads end at the same parent $v$. They are therefore nested and cannot coincide. Suppose the unprimed head $[a,v]$ is longer than the primed head $[a',v]$. Split the unprimed segment immediately before $a'$, retaining its own endpoint in the second piece. Since $p$ vanishes outside $H$, the two new sums are $b-b'$ and $b'$. The gain in its squared test is $$(b-b')^2+(b')^2-b^2=2(b')^2-2bb'\ge2|bb'|.$$ Figure <a href="#fig:opposite-crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:opposite-crossing">1</a> distinguishes the two original segments from the two pieces created by the split.

<figure id="fig:opposite-crossing">

<figcaption>Opposite $p$-sums at a gate $g$, with $p$ supported in $H$. The two heads on the left belong to different segment families; their traces are displaced to show their nesting on the same ancestral chain. Their tails are schematic and need not coincide. On the right, the longer segment is split before $a′$; the lower piece retains that segment’s original tail and has $p$-sum $b′$. The squared segment test increases by at least $2|bb′|$.</figcaption>
</figure>

Do this at every opposite-sign gate, in the family with the longer head. Within each family each original segment is cut at most once, because it crosses only one gate. Disjointness is preserved, so the total gain in each family is at most its deficit $L$. Summing proves <a href="#eq:opposite-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:opposite-crossing">[eq:opposite-crossing]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="mol:sec:joint-budget"></a>
### Keeping the coefficient and slack budgets together

We will replace each atom coefficient by a multiple of the head test’s sum on that segment. This produces two nonnegative errors: the squared change of coefficients and the unused squared head-test energy. Their sum is controlled by the squared-radius deficit of the center. Keeping this joint budget proves the main estimate for the part of an arbitrary displacement outside the head.

We use the segment atoms, ancestral projections, and cone decomposition already established above. The argument applies both to the coordinate completion on the infinite tree and to the finite-height forest, after their respective atomic-ball identifications. It does not identify those two spaces.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-opposite-crossing-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-opposite-crossing</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main-tail">[thm:main-tail]</a>.* Put $r=\|x\|$. If $r=0$, contractivity gives $\|Q_Hy\|\le\|y\|\le R$, which proves the assertion. Assume $r>0$. First suppose that $y$ has finite support, and enlarge $H$ to a finite ancestral set $K$ containing that support. Choose a head test $p$ of norm one such that $x(p)=r$. By the finite atomic-ball formula, choose finite laws of represented vectors $$A=\sum_i a_i d_{S_i},\qquad \sum_i a_i^2\le R^2,$$ with means $x+y$ and $x-y$. Choose a sign $\sigma$ with equal probabilities and then the corresponding law. Thus $\mathbb EA=x$ and $\mathbb E(\sigma A)=y$.

<a id="mol:eq:joint-identity"></a>
For each outcome put $$b_i=r p(S_i),\quad
 A^\circ=\sum_i b_i d_{S_i},\quad
 V=\sum_i(a_i-b_i)^2,\quad
 L=r^2-\sum_i b_i^2.$$ Both $V$ and $L$ are nonnegative. Since $\mathbb E\sum_i a_i b_i=r\mathbb EA(p)=r^2$, expansion gives $$\label{mol:eq:joint-identity}
 \eta+\theta
 :=\mathbb EV+\mathbb EL
 =\mathbb E\sum_i a_i^2-r^2
 \le R^2-r^2.$$ The atom coefficient estimate also gives $\|Q_H(A-A^\circ)\|\le\sqrt V$.

<a id="mol:eq:joint-signed-mass"></a>
Let $G$ be the gates outside $H$ which occur in the finite representations. At a gate $g$, write $b(g)$ for the coefficient of the crossing segment in $A^\circ$, and put it equal to zero when there is no crossing segment. Every segment wholly outside $H$ has replaced coefficient zero. If $W=Q_H(A-A^\circ)$, then $$\sum_{g\in G}|W(e_g)|^2\le V.$$ Indeed, the gates are incomparable, so each segment contains at most one gate, and disjointness assigns different gates to different coefficients. Since $\mathbb EA(e_g)=x(e_g)=0$, $$\label{mol:eq:joint-signed-mass}
 \sum_g(\mathbb Eb(g))^2
 =\sum_g(\mathbb EW(e_g))^2\le\mathbb EV=\eta.$$

<a id="mol:eq:joint-absolute-mass"></a>
The simultaneous opposite-head splitting argument, applied to the test $rp$, gives for two independent outcomes $$2\sum_{g:b(g)b'(g)<0}|b(g)b'(g)|\le L+L'.$$ Here the available squared-test budget is $r^2$; this is the only change from the norm-one version. Consequently, with $m(g)=\mathbb E|b(g)|$, $$\label{mol:eq:joint-absolute-mass}
 \|m\|_2^2-\|\mathbb Eb\|_2^2
 =\mathbb E\sum_g\bigl(|b(g)b'(g)|-b(g)b'(g)\bigr)
 \le2\theta.$$ Equations <a href="#mol:eq:joint-signed-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:joint-signed-mass">[mol:eq:joint-signed-mass]</a> and <a href="#mol:eq:joint-absolute-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:joint-absolute-mass">[mol:eq:joint-absolute-mass]</a> therefore give $\|m\|_2\le\sqrt{\eta+2\theta}$.

On the cone rooted at $g$, each replaced outcome is $b(g)d_{J(g)}$, where $J(g)$ is its crossing tail starting at $g$. The term is zero when there is no crossing segment. Thus the signed mean decomposes as <a id="mol:eq:signed-product-recovery"></a>$$\label{mol:eq:signed-product-recovery}
 Q_H\mathbb E(\sigma A^\circ)
 =\sum_g\mathbb E\bigl[\sigma b(g)d_{J(g)}\bigr].$$ Each cone term has norm at most $\mathbb E|b(g)|=m(g)$, since $|\sigma|=1$ and a segment indicator has norm one. The cone identity therefore gives $$\|Q_H\mathbb E(\sigma A^\circ)\|
 \le\|m\|_2\le\sqrt{\eta+2\theta}.$$ Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:tail-assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:tail-assembly">[stab:coef:tail-assembly]</a> gives a direct convex-product proof of the same reconstruction, allowing any multiplier of absolute value at most one in place of $\sigma$.

The signed mean of the original atoms is $y$, so its tail satisfies $$\|Q_Hy\|\le\sqrt\eta+\sqrt{\eta+2\theta}
 \le2\sqrt{\eta+\theta}.$$ For the last inequality, square both sides and use $\sqrt{\eta(\eta+2\theta)}\le\eta+\theta$. Now apply <a href="#mol:eq:joint-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:joint-identity">[mol:eq:joint-identity]</a>.

For an arbitrary completed vector $y$, take an increasing finite ancestral exhaustion $K_n\supset H$. Contractivity gives $\|x\pm P_{K_n}y\|\le R$, and $P_{K_n}y\to y$ in norm. The finite estimate passes to the limit. No bound on the height or the number of children entered the calculation. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### Asymptotic midpoint growth

The main tail estimate gives a uniform increase in the endpoint norms on a finite-codimensional subspace. We specify the two standard midpoint moduli before deriving this consequence for both models.

<a id="eq:max-modulus"></a>
For an infinite-dimensional real Banach space $Y$ and $t>0$, define the maximum-based asymptotic midpoint modulus by $$\label{eq:max-modulus}
 \overline\theta_Y(t)=\inf_{\|x\|=1}
 \sup_{F\in\operatorname{cof}(Y)}
 \inf_{y\in F,\,\|y\|=1}
 \left(\max\{\|x+ty\|,\|x-ty\|\}-1\right),$$ where $\operatorname{cof}(Y)$ denotes the closed finite-codimensional subspaces. This is the convention of (Dilworth et al. 2016, Definition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[DKRRZ2016](#ref-DKRRZ2016)]. A norm is AMUC when this modulus is positive for every $t>0$.

<a id="eq:averaged-modulus"></a>
We shall also use the averaged convention $$\label{eq:averaged-modulus}
 \widehat\delta_Y(t)=\inf_{\|x\|=1}\sup_{F\in\operatorname{cof}(Y)}
 \inf_{y\in F,\,\|y\|=1}
 \left(\frac{\|x+ty\|+\|x-ty\|}{2}-1\right).$$ For every infinite-dimensional real Banach space, <a id="eq:modulus-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:modulus-comparison}
 \tfrac12\overline\theta_Y(t)\le\widehat\delta_Y(t)
 \le\overline\theta_Y(t).$$ The second inequality is pointwise. For the first, fix a unit center $x$ and a norming functional $f$. Intersect a finite-codimensional subspace used for the maximum with $\ker f$. Both signed norms are then at least one, and an excess $a$ for their maximum gives an excess at least $a/2$ for their average. Arbitrary approximations to the supremum over subspaces, followed by the infimum over centers, prove the first inequality. The two conventions therefore agree as qualitative properties, but their numerical bounds must be distinguished.

For either symmetric expression, allowing $\|y\|\ge1$ in place of $\|y\|=1$ does not change the inner infimum. Indeed, the maximum and the average are even convex functions of the scalar multiplying a fixed direction, and are nondecreasing on the nonnegative half-line. This also shows that both moduli are nondecreasing in $t$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Averaged midpoint growth at every radius

Write $\widehat\delta(t)$ for the averaged modulus of the chosen space, defined in <a href="#eq:averaged-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-modulus">[eq:averaged-modulus]</a>.

<a id="stmt-mol-cor-all-radius-averaged"></a>
<div id="mol:cor:all-radius-averaged" class="corollary">

<a id="mol:eq:all-radius-averaged"></a>
**Corollary 10**. *In either of the segment-atomic completions considered above, $$\label{mol:eq:all-radius-averaged}
 \widehat\delta(t)\ge
 \frac{\sqrt{1+t^2/4}-1}{2}\qquad(t>0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mol-cor-all-radius-averaged"><summary>Proof of stmt-mol-cor-all-radius-averaged</summary>

*Proof.* For a unit $x=P_Hx$ and $y\in\ker P_H$ with $\|y\|\ge1$, Theorem <a href="#mol:prop:joint-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mol:prop:joint-budget">1</a> applied to $ty$ gives $$\max\{\|x+ty\|,\|x-ty\|\}\ge\sqrt{1+t^2/4}.$$ Both endpoint norms are at least one, by applying the contractive projection $P_H$. Their average therefore exceeds one by at least the right side of <a href="#mol:eq:all-radius-averaged" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:all-radius-averaged">[mol:eq:all-radius-averaged]</a>.

For an arbitrary unit center $x$, choose a finitely supported unit vector $x_0$ with $\|x-x_0\|<\varepsilon$, and let $H$ contain its ancestral support. The estimate for $x_0$ applies to every $y\in\ker P_H$ of norm at least one. Replacing $x_0$ by $x$ loses at most $\varepsilon$ in the average. Take the infimum over these $y$, then the supremum over finite-codimensional subspaces, and finally let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. The resulting bound is uniform over the original unit center. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:coordinate-variants"></a>
### Coordinate variants and bounded paths

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Projections and height conventions

The coordinate arguments also apply to restrictions of the forest and to other choices of finite component heights. We first record these variants, then use bounded root paths to rule out an equivalent AUC norm.

<a id="stmt-foundation-order-convex"></a>
<div id="foundation:order-convex" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11**. *Let $E$ be a set of vertices whose intersection with every segment is empty or a segment. The coordinate projection $P_E$ is contractive on both $J$ and $X=J^*$. Its range on $J$ is the completion of $c_{00}(E)$ under the segment norm restricted to $E$, and $$\|P_Ex\|=\|x|_{P_EJ}\| \qquad (x\in X).$$ In particular these assertions apply to an ancestral set, its complement, a difference of nested ancestral sets, a descendant subtree, and an antichain. On an antichain the coordinate norm on either space is exactly the usual $\ell_2$ norm.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-foundation-order-convex"><summary>Proof of stmt-foundation-order-convex</summary>

*Proof.* For a finite vector $u$, each segment sum of $P_Eu$ is the sum of $u$ over the intersection of that segment with $E$. Nonempty intersections are disjoint segments when the original segments are disjoint. Taking the supremum proves $\rho(P_Eu)\le\rho(u)$. Extend by density and take the adjoint to obtain the assertion on $X$. Contractivity and density identify $P_EJ$ with the indicated completion. Since $P_E$ maps the unit ball of $J$ onto the unit ball of its range, restriction has exactly the asserted dual norm. A segment meets an antichain in at most one vertex; singleton tests then give the $\ell_2$ norm on its primal range. The restriction identity and $\ell_2$ duality give the dual assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-foundation-height-variants"></a>
<div id="foundation:height-variants" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12**. *Let $I$ be countable and let $(h_i)_{i\in I}$ be nonnegative integers. On the disjoint forest $$\mathcal F_{\boldsymbol h}
   =\coprod_{i\in I}(\{i\}\times\mathbb N^{\le h_i})$$ define $J_{\boldsymbol h}$ by the same disjoint-segment norm and set $X_{\boldsymbol h}=J_{\boldsymbol h}^*$. If $J(h)$ denotes the single-tree completion on $\mathbb N^{\le h}$, then $$J_{\boldsymbol h}
   =\Big(\bigoplus_{i\in I}J(h_i)\Big)_{\ell_2},
 \qquad
 X_{\boldsymbol h}
   =\Big(\bigoplus_{i\in I}J(h_i)^*\Big)_{\ell_2}$$ isometrically under coordinate restriction. Both spaces are reflexive, and finite coordinate vectors are dense in each. The projection and complementary-subtree formulas remain valid for this forest.*

*The convention of one tree with $n$ levels, including the root, for every $n\ge1$ gives exactly $$J_{\mathrm{levels}}=\mathbb R\oplus_2 J,
 \qquad X_{\mathrm{levels}}=\mathbb R\oplus_2 X.$$ Its component estimate is $\|u\|_2\le\rho(u)\le\sqrt n\,\|u\|_2$, and a root-to-leaf path has $n-1$ extension steps.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-foundation-height-variants"><summary>Proof of stmt-foundation-height-variants</summary>

*Proof.* Every segment belongs to one component. Grouping a test family by component gives one inequality in the primal sum formula. For the other, choose nearly maximizing finite families on each of the finitely many components meeting the support and combine them. Completion gives the displayed primal identity, and duality for $\ell_2$ sums gives the dual identity. On component $i$, $$\|u\|_2\le\rho(u)\le\sqrt{h_i+1}\,\|u\|_2.$$ Thus each component and its dual are reflexive Hilbert space renormings with dense coordinates. The sum identities give reflexivity and coordinate density on the entire forest. No bound uniform in $i$ on the Hilbert space comparison is needed. Projection contractivity uses only segment intersection, as in Lemma <a href="#foundation:order-convex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="foundation:order-convex">11</a>; the complementary-subtree identity uses independent segment tests in its incomparable subtrees.

For the last assertion, put $h=n-1$. The component $n=1$ is the one-dimensional singleton tree with the absolute-value norm. The remaining components have heights $1,2,\ldots$, and the coordinate map $(n,\sigma)\mapsto(n-1,\sigma)$ preserves their segments and all their tests. Splitting off the singleton proves both isometries. The component bound and the number of extension steps follow from the maximal chain cardinality $n$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Bounded paths and equivalent norms

A single segment functional has norm one even when the segment is very long. Its successive coordinate increments therefore give bounded paths. The following argument explains why weakly null choices of the next increment obstruct an asymptotically uniformly convex equivalent norm. It also retains the precise radius when increments are normalized before being approximated in a finite-codimensional subspace.

<a id="eq:one-sided-modulus"></a>
For a norm $N$ on an infinite-dimensional real Banach space $Z$, write $S_N=\{z:N(z)=1\}$ and let $\operatorname{cof}(Z)$ denote the closed finite-codimensional linear subspaces. Define $$\label{eq:one-sided-modulus}
 \overline\delta_N(t)=\inf_{x\in S_N}
   \sup_{F\in\operatorname{cof}(Z)}
   \inf_{y\in F\cap S_N}\bigl(N(x+ty)-1\bigr),\qquad t>0.$$ Positivity at every $t>0$ is asymptotic uniform convexity. The spheres $F\cap S_N$ are nonempty. A norming functional for $x$, with $F$ contained in its kernel, gives the lower bound in $0\le\overline\delta_N(t)\le t$; the upper bound is the triangle inequality.

<a id="stmt-foundation-weak-null-growth"></a>
<div id="foundation:weak-null-growth" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13**. *Let $x\in S_N$, let $(z_j)$ be weakly null, and suppose $N(z_j)\ge t>0$ for every $j$. For every $0<\gamma<\overline\delta_N(t)$, all sufficiently large $j$ satisfy $$N(x+z_j)>1+\gamma.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-foundation-weak-null-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-foundation-weak-null-growth</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $\eta$ with $\gamma<\eta<\overline\delta_N(t)$ and then $F\in\operatorname{cof}(Z)$ such that $N(x+tv)>1+\eta$ for every $v\in F\cap S_N$. Put $u_j=z_j/N(z_j)$. These vectors are weakly null because $1/N(z_j)\le1/t$. Their images in $Z/F$ converge to zero weakly, hence in norm, since the quotient is finite dimensional. Choose $v_j\in F$ with $N(v_j-u_j)\to0$. Then $N(v_j)\to1$, so $w_j=v_j/N(v_j)$ is defined for large $j$, belongs to $F\cap S_N$, and satisfies $N(w_j-u_j)\to0$. Consequently $$N(x+t u_j)>1+\eta-tN(w_j-u_j)>1+\gamma$$ for large $j$. For $r\ge t$, convexity gives $$N(x+r u_j)-1\ge\frac rt\bigl(N(x+t u_j)-1\bigr).$$ Apply this at $r=N(z_j)$ to finish the proof. ◻

</details>

There is a second useful form of the approximation step. If $N(z_j)\ge2t$ and $N(x+tv)>1+\eta$ on $F\cap S_N$, approximate $z_j$ directly by $v_j\in F$ with $N(z_j-v_j)\to0$. Eventually $N(v_j)\ge t$, so the same ray-convexity inequality gives $N(x+v_j)>1+\eta$. For every $0<\gamma<\eta$, choosing $N(z_j-v_j)<\eta-\gamma$ gives $N(x+z_j)>1+\gamma$. This form uses the spare factor two in the radius and does not require approximating the normalized sequence.

<a id="stmt-foundation-bounded-tree"></a>
<div id="foundation:bounded-tree" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14**. *Suppose an infinite-dimensional real Banach space $(Z,\|\cdot\|)$ contains, for every positive integer $k$, vectors $(u_s)_{s\in\mathbb N^{\le k}}$ such that $$0<m\le\|u_s\|\le M,
 \qquad \|u_{s^\frown j}-u_s\|\ge c>0,$$ where $m,M,c$ are independent of $k$, and for each nonterminal $s$ the sequence $(u_{s^\frown j}-u_s)_j$ is weakly null against the whole continuous dual of $Z$. If $0<a\le b<\infty$ and $a\|z\|\le N(z)\le b\|z\|$, then $$\overline\delta_N\!\left(\frac{ac}{bM}\right)=0.$$ In particular the same conclusion holds at $ac/(2bM)$, and $Z$ admits no equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-foundation-bounded-tree"><summary>Proof of stmt-foundation-bounded-tree</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose the modulus at $t=ac/(bM)$ is positive and choose $0<\gamma<\overline\delta_N(t)$. At a nonterminal $s$, put $$x=\frac{u_s}{N(u_s)},\qquad
 z_j=\frac{u_{s^\frown j}-u_s}{N(u_s)}.$$ The equivalent norms have the same continuous linear functionals, so $(z_j)$ is weakly null, and $N(z_j)\ge ac/(bM)=t$. Lemma <a href="#foundation:weak-null-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="foundation:weak-null-growth">13</a> gives a child for which $N(u_{s^\frown j})>(1+\gamma)N(u_s)$. Starting at the root and repeating $k$ times gives $am(1+\gamma)^k<bM$, which is impossible for sufficiently large $k$. Thus the modulus at $t$ is zero. Applying the same argument at $t/2$, using either the lemma or the direct approximation form above, proves the stated smaller-radius conclusion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-foundation-forest-obstruction"></a>
<div id="foundation:forest-obstruction" class="corollary">

**Corollary 15**. *If the finite component heights $h_i$ in Proposition <a href="#foundation:height-variants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="foundation:height-variants">12</a> are unbounded, then every equivalent norm $N$ on $X_{\boldsymbol h}$ with $a\|x\|\le N(x)\le b\|x\|$ satisfies $$\overline\delta_N(a/b)=\overline\delta_N(a/(2b))=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-foundation-forest-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-foundation-forest-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* For a vertex $s$ in a component with root $o$, put $u_s=\sum_{o\preceq v\preceq s}e_v^*$. A single segment test gives $\|u_s\|\le1$, while evaluation on $e_s$ gives equality. Likewise $\|e_s^*\|=1$, and $u_{s^\frown j}-u_s=e_{s^\frown j}^*$. These increments are weakly null: under the canonical identification $X_{\boldsymbol h}^*=J_{\boldsymbol h}$, their evaluations on $g$ are the coordinates $g(s^\frown j)$, which tend to zero since $\|g\|_2\le\rho(g)$. Equivalently, the coordinate map on this fixed component is a Hilbert space isomorphism, so every continuous functional on the full space restricts to a continuous Hilbert space functional there. Components of unbounded finite height give the trees required by Proposition <a href="#foundation:bounded-tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="foundation:bounded-tree">14</a>, with $m=M=c=1$. For the convention of $n$ levels the same iteration makes $n-1$ steps. Both step counts tend to infinity. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:infinite-obstruction"></a>
### The infinite-height renorming obstruction

<a id="stmt-binf-obstruction"></a>
<div id="binf:obstruction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (The bounded-path obstruction). *The unit root-path vectors in $B_\infty$ have unit child increments which are weakly null among the children of each fixed vertex. Every sequence of distinct coordinate vectors is in fact weakly null. If $a\|\cdot\|\le N\le b\|\cdot\|$, with $0<a\le b<\infty$, then the one-sided asymptotic modulus of $N$ vanishes at $a/(2b)$ and at $a/b$. In particular, $B_\infty$ has no equivalent AUC norm. The same obstruction holds for the finite-height forest, using trees of arbitrarily large finite height.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-binf-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-binf-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* Singleton tests and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\left\|\sum_v c_v e_v^*\right\|\le
 \left(\sum_v c_v^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ For coefficients supported on an antichain, equality holds: the test with proportional coefficients on that antichain has its Euclidean norm, since a segment meets it in at most one point. In particular sibling coordinate vectors span an isometric $\ell_2$. Thus the map sending the unit vectors of $\ell_2(T_\infty)$ to $e_v^*$ extends boundedly into $B_\infty$. For any $L\in B_\infty^*$, finite Euclidean duality gives $\sum_v|L(e_v^*)|^2\le\|L\|^2$. Hence distinct coordinates are weakly null against the whole continuous dual. A root-path functional $z_v=d_{[o,v]}$ has norm one, by the single segment upper bound and any coordinate test on that path. Its child is $z_{v^\frown n}=z_v+e_{v^\frown n}^*$.

Use the one-sided modulus defined in <a href="#eq:one-sided-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:one-sided-modulus">[eq:one-sided-modulus]</a>. It is nonnegative: for each $x$, the kernel of a norming functional makes every displayed norm at least one. Suppose its value is positive at a radius $r_0\le a/b$. Choose $0<d<\overline\delta_N(r_0)$. For a path $z$, put $x=z/N(z)$ and choose $F$ so that $N(x+r_0w)\ge1+d$ on the $N$-unit sphere of $F$. The normalized child directions $v_n=e_{v^\frown n}^*/N(e_{v^\frown n}^*)$ are weakly null. Their images in the finite-dimensional quotient by $F$ converge to zero in norm. Approximate them in $F$ and normalize those approximants to obtain $N$-unit vectors $w_n\in F$ with $N(v_n-w_n)\to0$. Choose $N(v_n-w_n)<d/(2r_0)$. Then $N(x+r_0v_n)>1+d/2$ for large $n$. The actual child step has radius $$r_n=\frac{N(e_{v^\frown n}^*)}{N(z)}\in[a/b,b/a].$$ Convexity along this ray, including the case $r_n=r_0$, gives <a id="binf:radial-growth"></a>$$\label{binf:radial-growth}
 N(x+r_nv_n)>1+\frac{r_n}{r_0}\frac d2\ge1+\frac d2.$$ Choosing a child repeatedly contradicts $a\le N(z_v)\le b$. For the forest, choose the component height in advance large enough that $a(1+d/2)^h>b$ and carry out only $h$ such selections. ◻

</details>

<a id="binf:transfer-comparisons"></a>
#### Transferring the cofinite gain to a child

The following procedures keep track of the radius and approximation error in the bounded-path argument. Fix the equivalent norm $N$ and constants $a,b$ from Proposition <a href="#binf:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:obstruction">16</a>. For a root path $z=d_{[o,v]}$, put $x=z/N(z)$ and write $e_n=e_{v^\frown n}^*$ for its child increments. Then $$a\le N(z)\le b,\qquad a\le N(e_n)\le b.$$ At each stated radius $r_0$, suppose a finite-codimensional subspace $F$ gives $N(x+r_0w)\ge1+d$ for every $N$-unit $w\in F$, with $d>0$ chosen below the putative positive modulus. Raw, path-normalized, and unit child sequences are weakly null, so all three can be approximated in $F$. Each resulting uniform child gain contradicts bounded path norms by the iteration just proved.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### Raw children at the safety radius.

<a id="binf:raw-error"></a>
At the safety radius $r_0=a/(2b)$, one can approximate the child without first normalizing it. Given a cofinite gain $d>0$ at $z/N(z)$, choose a raw child $e$ and $q\in F$ with $$\label{binf:raw-error}
 N(e-q)<\min\{a/2,dN(z)/2\}.$$ Then $N(q)\ge a/2\ge r_0N(z)$, and ray convexity yields $N(z+q)\ge(1+d)N(z)$. Consequently $N(z+e)\ge(1+d/2)N(z)$. The stronger restriction $N(e-q)<\min\{a/2,ad/2\}$ suffices uniformly over all paths. If the cofinite gain is $2d$, the error bound $dN(z)$ gives factor $1+d$; if that gain is half a named modulus $\delta$, error less than $\delta N(z)/4$ gives factor $1+\delta/4$.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### Repair at the endpoint radius.

Normalize a raw child by the path norm, setting $h_n=e_{v^\frown n}^*/N(z)$. Its norm is at least $a/b$. At the radius $r_0=a/b$, take $q_n\in F$ with $N(q_n-h_n)\to0$. These approximants may have norm slightly less than $r_0$. For large $n$, set <a id="binf:radial-repair"></a>$$\label{binf:radial-repair}
 q_n'=\max\{1,r_0/N(q_n)\}\,q_n.$$ Since $N(h_n)\ge r_0$, the enlargement costs at most $N(q_n-h_n)$, so $N(q_n'-h_n)\le2N(q_n-h_n)\to0$. The cofinite gain applied to $q_n'$ therefore gives a strict child factor greater than $1+d/2$ once the repaired error $N(q_n'-h_n)$ is less than $d/2$ (for example, when the original error is less than $d/4$). At the safety radius $r_0=a/(2b)$ no enlargement is needed: $N(h_n)\ge2r_0$, and choosing $N(q_n-h_n)<\min\{r_0,\gamma/2\}$ transfers a cofinite gain $\gamma$ to a strict child factor greater than $1+\gamma/2$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### Unit directions at the variable radius.

For $0<r_0\le a/b$, the unit-child procedure from the proof inserts the approximating unit direction at the actual variable radius $r_n$, instead of first transferring at $r_0$. Ray convexity gives $N(x+r_nw_n)\ge1+d$. As $r_n\le b/a$, <a id="binf:variable-radius-transfer"></a>$$\label{binf:variable-radius-transfer}
 N(x+r_nv_n)\ge1+d-r_nN(v_n-w_n)
              \ge1+d-(b/a)N(v_n-w_n)>1+d/2$$ for all sufficiently close unit approximants. This proves the variable-scale transfer with its uniform error factor and also covers the endpoint $r_n=r_0$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Additive growth.

At the safety radius $r_0=a/(2b)$, a cofinite gain $\eta>0$ gives $N(z+q)\ge N(z)+a\eta$ since $N(z)\ge a$. With $N(e-q)<\min\{a/2,a\eta/2\}$, each chosen child satisfies $N(z+e)\ge N(z)+a\eta/2$. Repetition again contradicts the same upper bound $b$. This is a separate way to accumulate the gain.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Parameter choices for the raw-child transfer
