At the safety radius $t_0=a/(2b)$, the raw-child argument above turns a cofinite gain into growth of a root-path norm. The following statement records three choices of that gain and their uniform error bounds.

<a id="stmt-stab-sign-obstruction"></a>
<div id="stab:sign:obstruction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17**. *In $B_\infty$, the root paths $h_v=d_{[o,v]}$ and the coordinates $e_v^*$ have norm one, and the children $(e_{v^\frown j}^*)_j$ of each fixed vertex are weakly null. If $a\|z\|\le N(z)\le b\|z\|$, with $0<a\le b<\infty$, then $\overline\delta_N(a/(2b))=0$.*

*More precisely, put $t_0=a/(2b)$, with $d,\varepsilon>0$ below. Each of the following assumptions would permit a finite-codimensional subspace and a child whose approximation error and growth factor satisfy the corresponding row: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \text{assumed positive lower bound}&
 \text{error less than}&\text{growth factor}\\\hline
 0<d<\overline\delta_N(t_0)&\min(a/2,ad/2)&1+d/2\rule{0pt}{13pt}\\
 2\varepsilon<\overline\delta_N(t_0)&
       \min(a/2,a\varepsilon/2)&1+\varepsilon/2\\
 d=\overline\delta_N(t_0)>0&\min(a/2,ad/4)&1+d/4
\end{array}$$ Here the error is $N(e_v^*-f)$ for the chosen child and its approximant $f$ in the finite-codimensional subspace. Growth is measured relative to the $N$-norm of the parent root-path vector.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-sign-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-sign-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* The path norms, weak nullity against the whole dual and vanishing one-sided modulus are Proposition <a href="#binf:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:obstruction">16</a>. For the table, choose respectively a cofinite gain $\gamma=d$, $\gamma=\varepsilon$ or $\gamma=d/2$. The uniform raw-child error bound $N(e_v^*-f)<\min(a/2,a\gamma/2)$ from <a href="#binf:raw-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="binf:raw-error">[binf:raw-error]</a> gives growth at least $1+\gamma/2$. These are exactly the three rows. In the last row the selected gain $d/2$ lies strictly below the named modulus, as required by the supremum over finite-codimensional subspaces. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-binf-supremum-obstruction"></a>
<div id="binf:supremum-obstruction" class="remark">

*Remark 18* (Taking the supremum of the path norms). For the infinite tree there is a second contradiction that uses no iteration along a branch. Set $$M=\sup_v N(d_{[o,v]})\in[a,b],\qquad t_0=\frac{a}{2M}.$$ Suppose $0<\gamma<\overline\delta_N(t_0)$. For each path vector $z=d_{[o,v]}$, choose a cofinite subspace $F$ giving gain $\gamma$ at $z/N(z)$. The child increments $e_{v^\frown n}^*$ are weakly null, so choose $q_n\in F$ with $N(q_n-e_{v^\frown n}^*)\to0$. Eventually $N(q_n)\ge a/2\ge t_0N(z)$. Convexity along the ray through $q_n$ therefore gives $N(z+q_n)\ge(1+\gamma)N(z)$. Passing to the lower limit and using the vanishing approximation error yields $$M\ge\liminf_n N(z+e_{v^\frown n}^*)\ge(1+\gamma)N(z).$$ Taking the supremum over all $v$ gives $M\ge(1+\gamma)M$, a contradiction. No path is required to attain $M$. This argument uses the fact that every vertex of the infinite tree has children; the finite-height forest is covered by the finite-iteration argument above.

</div>

<a id="clip:section"></a>
### Clipping cumulative segment sums

We now perturb a primal head test while keeping the squared cost quadratic in the perturbation size. The construction works on $T_\infty$ and on the finite-height forest. In this section $\mathcal T$ denotes either tree system, $J(\mathcal T)$ its segment completion, and $Z$ its closed coordinate span in $J(\mathcal T)^*$. Thus $Z=B_\infty$ in the first case and $Z=X$ in the second. We retain $\rho$ for the test norm and $\|\cdot\|$ for the coefficient norm. All tests constructed below are finitely supported.

<a id="clip:budgets"></a>
Fix a finite ancestral head $H$. For a gate $g$ let $U_g$ be its exterior cone, and give its parent the artificial cumulative value zero when that parent is absent from the component. For $v=Q_Hv$ define $$V_g(w)=v([g,w]),\qquad
 m_g=\max_{w\in U_g}|V_g(w)|,\qquad
 q_g=\rho(v\mathbf1_{U_g}).$$ These maxima exist: finite support gives only finitely many distinct cumulative values. We have $$\label{clip:budgets}
 \sum_gm_g^2\le\rho(v)^2=\sum_gq_g^2.$$ The equality follows from the exterior square-sum identity. For the inequality, choose one maximizing root prefix in each nonzero cone; these prefixes are disjoint.

<a id="clip:group-budgets"></a>
A third useful grouping collects cones with the same last head vertex. For $p\in H$, let $C_p$ consist of all exterior vertices whose last ancestor in $H$ is $p$. Either of $$\label{clip:group-budgets}
 b_p=\rho(v\mathbf1_{C_p}),\qquad
 M_p=\max_{w\in C_p}|v((p,w])|$$ may be used as its budget, with $M_p=0$ when $C_p$ is empty. Both satisfy $\sum_pb_p^2\le\rho(v)^2$ and $\sum_pM_p^2\le\rho(v)^2$. The first follows by joining nearly maximizing families in the disjoint regions; for the second choose one maximizing prefix in each region. A region may contain several cones, but a segment meeting it stays in one cone, and disjoint crossing segments have distinct last head vertices. These facts, rather than an assumed orthogonality within a region, justify the grouping. For a component disjoint from $H$, there is no last head vertex. Keep each such cone as a separate group, use its prefix maximum or full cone norm, and choose an arbitrary fixed inward sign. It has no head crossing and contributes only to exterior squares. This convention also handles $H=\varnothing$.

<a id="stmt-clip-crossing"></a>
<div id="clip:crossing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (An inward crossing estimate). *Let $f=P_Hf$ and $v=Q_Hv$ be finite tests. Use either the cone budgets $m_g$ or $q_g$, or the last-head budgets in <a href="#clip:group-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:group-budgets">[clip:group-budgets]</a>; write $c_j$ for the chosen budget of a cone or region. Fix $s>0$. At each attached group choose the shortest head suffix ending at its attachment whose $f$-sum has magnitude at least $sc_j$, if one exists and $c_j>0$. Let $\sigma_j$ be the opposite sign of that sum, and otherwise choose either sign.*

*Suppose a finite exterior test $w$ satisfies $\rho(w)\le\rho(v)$, and every root prefix in group $j$ has $w$-sum in $\sigma_j[0,c_j]$. Then <a id="clip:crossing-bound"></a>$$\label{clip:crossing-bound}
 \rho(f+sw)^2\le\rho(f)^2+3s^2\rho(v)^2.$$ The same conclusion holds with a strictly greater suffix threshold, provided the small-crossing case includes equality. With a non-strict suffix threshold, equality may also be assigned to the small-crossing case, leaving only strictly larger terms to be split.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-clip-crossing"><summary>Proof of stmt-clip-crossing</summary>

*Proof.* Take a disjoint segment family. Write $A_i$ and $B_i$ for its head $f$-sum and exterior $w$-sum. Then $\sum_iB_i^2\le\rho(v)^2$. A positive product $A_iB_i$ occurs only at a crossing, and different crossings use different cone indices (or different last head vertices in the grouped construction). If $|A_i|<sc_j$, its cross term is bounded by $2s^2c_j^2$. Their total is at most $2s^2\rho(v)^2$.

<a id="clip:split-gain"></a>
For a remaining positive product, the head qualifies for the suffix selection. It contains the selected suffix, whose sum $C_i$ has the opposite sign to $A_i$ and magnitude at least $sc_j$. The suffix is proper, so splitting it off leaves two nonempty head segments. The increase in their squared $f$-sums is $$\label{clip:split-gain}
 (A_i-C_i)^2+C_i^2-A_i^2
 =2C_i^2-2A_iC_i\ge2s|A_i|c_j\ge2sA_iB_i.$$ These cuts are simultaneous cuts in distinct original segments. Their pieces and the other nonempty heads are a valid disjoint test family for $f$. Thus the original head energy plus all large positive cross terms is at most $\rho(f)^2$. Drop nonpositive cross terms, add the small-crossing budget $2s^2\rho(v)^2$, and add the pure exterior budget $s^2\rho(v)^2$. This proves the result. For a strict threshold, use $|A_i|\le sc_j$ in the small case and $|A_i|>sc_j$ in the large case; the proof is unchanged. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-clip-two-clips"></a>
<div id="clip:two-clips" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Two cumulative clips). *For every $s>0$ and finite tests $f=P_Hf$, $v=Q_Hv$, there are $h_1,h_2$ supported on $\operatorname{supp}v$ such that <a id="clip:difference-split"></a>$$\label{clip:difference-split}
 v=h_1-h_2,\qquad \rho(h_j)\le\rho(v),\qquad
 \rho(f+sh_j)^2\le\rho(f)^2+3s^2\rho(v)^2\quad(j=1,2).$$ Equivalently, $v=k_1+k_2$ with $k_1=h_1$, $k_2=-h_2$, and the two admissible perturbations are $f+sk_1$ and $f-sk_2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-clip-two-clips"><summary>Proof of stmt-clip-two-clips</summary>

*Proof.* Fix any one of the budgets above and choose its signs by Lemma <a href="#clip:crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:crossing">19</a>. For each group let $\Phi_j(r)=\sigma_j\max\{\sigma_j r,0\}$. On an exterior cone in that group, set the cumulative potentials of $h_1,h_2$ equal to $\Phi_j(V_g(w))$ and $\Phi_j(-V_g(w))$, respectively, and take successive differences down the edges from initial value zero. Since $\Phi_j(r)-\Phi_j(-r)=r$, the difference of the two tests is $v$. If $v(w)=0$, its cumulative values at $w$ and its parent are equal, so both new increments vanish. Thus no new support appears, even along infinite descendant paths.

On any segment in a cone, its $h_j$-sum is a difference of two values of a single $1$-Lipschitz map. Its magnitude is therefore at most the absolute $v$-sum on that same segment. The same assertion holds for a crossing after deleting its zero head portion. In a last-head group, both endpoints use the same grouping index, including the artificial zero at the head predecessor. Hence $\rho(h_j)\le\rho(v)$. Root prefixes have the chosen sign and the chosen budget bounds their magnitudes. Lemma <a href="#clip:crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:crossing">19</a> completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="clip:shortening"></a>
The large-crossing calculation has a second form which retains only one of the two split pieces. If $A_iC_i<0$, the preceding head piece has sum $A_i-C_i$, and $$\label{clip:shortening}
 (A_i-C_i)^2\ge A_i^2+2s|A_i|c_j
                 \ge A_i^2+2sA_iB_i.$$ Keeping this piece for each large crossing, and keeping each other head unchanged, is again a disjoint head family. This proves the homogeneous sum-decomposition estimate in Lemma <a href="#clip:two-clips" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:two-clips">20</a> by shortening rather than by retaining both pieces. The missing suffix is discarded only from the test family, not from the vector being constructed.

<a id="clip:testing"></a>
#### What the two tests recover

<a id="clip:paired-testing"></a>
For $x=P_Hx$ and $y\in\ker P_H$, the mixed head–tail pairings vanish. If $\rho(f),\rho(v)\le1$, $x(f)=\|x\|$, and $y(v)\ge\theta\|y\|$ for $0<\theta<1$, the two clips yield both <a id="clip:max-testing"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \max_\pm\|x\pm ty\|&\ge
 \frac{\|x\|+st\theta\|y\|/2}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}},
 \label{clip:max-testing}\\
 \frac{\|x+ty\|+\|x-ty\|}{2}&\ge
 \frac{\|x\|+st\theta\|y\|/2}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}.
 \label{clip:paired-testing}
\end{aligned}$$ The first uses one clip with $|y(h_j)|\ge\theta\|y\|/2$ and chooses the corresponding endpoint sign. The second tests $x+ty$ on $f+sh_1$ and $x-ty$ on $f+sh_2$, and uses $y(h_1)-y(h_2)=y(v)$ before averaging. These are distinct testing procedures, although their displayed right sides agree. Both norms are separately at least $\|x\|$ by testing on $f$.

<a id="clip:general-head-tail"></a>
Finite tail vectors have exact norming tests after a finite ancestral projection and then $Q_H$. For completed tails, finite test density allows $\theta\uparrow1$; alternatively approximate the tail in norm using Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a>. Thus, for every $s,t>0$, $$\label{clip:general-head-tail}
 \max_\pm\|x\pm ty\|\ge
 \frac{\|x\|+st\|y\|/2}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}.$$ This preserves arbitrary head norms and arbitrary tail magnitudes. In particular, if $\|x-P_Hx\|<\varepsilon$ at a unit center and $y\in\ker P_H$, $\|y\|\ge1$, contractivity gives both endpoint bounds $\|x\pm ty\|\ge1-\varepsilon$, while <a href="#clip:general-head-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:general-head-tail">[clip:general-head-tail]</a> applied to $P_Hx$ gives $$\max_\pm\|x\pm ty\|\ge
 \frac{1-\varepsilon+st/2}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}-\varepsilon.$$

The paired argument also gives the same lower bound for the average.

Use the averaged modulus in <a href="#eq:averaged-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-modulus">[eq:averaged-modulus]</a>, with $Z$ as the chosen coordinate space. A uniform estimate proved for finite unit centers passes to general centers by normalized finite approximation. If its excess is $E(t)$, choosing an approximation within $E(t)/2$ retains $E(t)/2$. If approximation errors are allowed to tend to zero after choosing the cofinite subspace, the full $E(t)$ remains. The supremum over subspaces is taken before the infimum over centers. For a bound first proved for unit tails, the symmetric norm average is an even convex function of the tail radius, hence nondecreasing at nonnegative radii; this gives the required $\|y\|\ge1$ version.

<a id="stmt-clip-paired-modulus"></a>
<div id="clip:paired-modulus" class="corollary">

**Corollary 21** (The averaged clipping estimate). *For the infinite-height coordinate predual $B_\infty$ and the finite-height forest space $X$, $$\widehat\delta_Z(t)\ge\sqrt{1+t^2/12}-1\qquad(t>0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-clip-paired-modulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-clip-paired-modulus</summary>

*Proof.* Use the paired test procedure <a href="#clip:paired-testing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:paired-testing">[clip:paired-testing]</a>, let the tail test approach its norm, and put $s=t/6$. At a finite unit center the average is at least $$\frac{1+st/2}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}=\sqrt{1+t^2/12}.$$ Arbitrarily close normalized approximation of the center preserves this bound. The construction applies to untouched forest components because they have no crossing terms. It needs no bound on the component height. ◻

</details>

<a id="dir:primal:add:homogeneous"></a>
#### Homogeneous consequences of the clipped tests

We record two consequences of the common clipped construction that will be useful for comparing midpoint bounds. Here $X$ is the finite-height forest dual, and $H$ is a finite ancestral head.

<a id="stmt-dir-primal-add-head-tail-lens"></a>
<div id="dir:primal:add:head-tail-lens" class="proposition">

<a id="dir:primal:add:optimized"></a>
**Proposition 22**. *If $x=P_Hx$ and $y=Q_Hy$, then $$\label{dir:primal:add:optimized}
 \max_{\pm}\|x\pm y\|\ge\sqrt{\|x\|^2+\|y\|^2/12}.$$ If $0<\sigma\le1$ and $w,Z_1,Z_2,\ldots\in X$ satisfy $\|w\pm Z_j\|\le1$ and $\|Z_j-Z_l\|\ge2\sigma$ for $j\ne l$, then $\|w\|\le1-\sigma^2/24$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dir-primal-add-head-tail-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-dir-primal-add-head-tail-lens</summary>

*Proof.* In <a href="#clip:general-head-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:general-head-tail">[clip:general-head-tail]</a>, put $t=1$ and $s=\|y\|/(6\|x\|)$ when both vectors are nonzero. This gives <a href="#dir:primal:add:optimized" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:primal:add:optimized">[dir:primal:add:optimized]</a>. If $y=0$ the assertion is equality; if $x=0$, it follows from $\|y\|\ge\|y\|/\sqrt{12}$.

For the second assertion, given $\varepsilon>0$, choose $H$ with $\|w-P_Hw\|\le\varepsilon$. The bounded sequence $P_HZ_j$ has two distinct terms for which $h=(Z_j-Z_l)/2$ satisfies $\|P_Hh\|\le\varepsilon$. Then $\|h\|\ge\sigma$ and $\|w\pm h\|\le1$ by convexity. Formula <a href="#clip:general-head-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:general-head-tail">[clip:general-head-tail]</a> at $P_Hw,Q_Hh$, again with $t=1$, gives $$(1+2\varepsilon)\sqrt{1+3s^2}
 \ge\|w\|-\varepsilon+\tfrac{s}{2}(\sigma-\varepsilon).$$ Let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, put $s=\sigma/6$, and use $\sqrt{1+3s^2}\le1+3s^2/2$. This retains the stated $1/24$ loss. ◻

</details>

<a id="clip:selection"></a>
#### Three ways to retain a useful sign

We return to both choices of $Z$ for the three selection procedures below.

The two global clips retain half the original pairing in a single endpoint test. Sometimes the construction selects signs before it chooses that endpoint. The resulting extra loss records a different selection mechanism.

<a id="stmt-clip-selection-prop"></a>
<div id="clip:selection-prop" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23** (Selection before testing). *Suppose $x=P_Hx$, $\|x\|=1$, $y\in\ker P_H$, and $v=Q_Hv$, $\rho(v)\le1$, and $y(v)>\|y\|/2$. For every $s>0$, each of the following constructions produces an exterior test $w$ with $$|y(w)|>\|y\|/8,\qquad \rho(w)\le1,
 \qquad \rho(f+sw)^2\le1+3s^2,$$ where $f=P_Hf$ norms $x$ and the inward signs are chosen with full cone budgets $q_g$.*

1.  *Take the positive and negative cumulative parts globally, choose one by its total pairing, then retain one of the two groups of cones having the same prescribed inward sign.*

2.  *Form all four combinations of a cumulative positive/negative part and a prescribed inward-sign group, and select one by its pairing.*

3.  *Choose a cumulative part separately in each cone, orient it inward, then retain the cones on which the resulting pairings have one common sign.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-clip-selection-prop"><summary>Proof of stmt-clip-selection-prop</summary>

*Proof.* The exterior-test requirement loses no norming accuracy: replacing any finite test $v$ by $Q_Hv$ preserves $y(v)$ and cannot increase $\rho(v)$. Let $v^+,v^-$ be the successive differences of $\max(V_g,0)$ and $\max(-V_g,0)$ on each cone. Both have nonnegative root prefixes, $v=v^+-v^-$, and each segment sum is dominated in absolute value by the corresponding $v$-sum.

For the first construction choose $q\in\{v^+,v^-\}$ with $|y(q)|>\|y\|/4$. Partition its cones by $\sigma_g=1$ and $\sigma_g=-1$, writing $q=q_++q_-$. One part has pairing of absolute value greater than $\|y\|/8$. For that sign $\sigma$ put $w=\sigma q_\sigma$. This last sign makes its root prefixes inward; the eventual endpoint sign is chosen from the sign of $y(w)$. These two choices must not be confused.

For the second construction form $w_{\sigma,+}=\sigma v^+\mathbf1_{\{\sigma_g=\sigma\}}$ and $w_{\sigma,-}=\sigma v^-\mathbf1_{\{\sigma_g=\sigma\}}$ for $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}$. Then $v=\sum_\sigma\sigma(w_{\sigma,+}-w_{\sigma,-})$. The triangle inequality for their four pairings gives the claimed $1/8$ bound for one of them. Each of the four was fixed before the endpoint was selected.

For the third construction write $v_g=v\mathbf1_{U_g}$. In each nonzero cone select $q_g'$ from the two cumulative parts so that $|y(q_g')|\ge|y(v_g)|/2$. Reverse the selected part if necessary to make its root prefixes inward. Call the oriented test $r_g$. Then $$\sum_g|y(r_g)|\ge\tfrac12\sum_g|y(v_g)|
                    \ge\tfrac12y(v)>\|y\|/4.$$ Keep either the positive-pairing cones or the negative-pairing cones, whichever has larger total absolute pairing, and sum their $r_g$. This loses another factor two. The selected half in each cone can differ; this procedure is not the choice of one of two fixed global clips.

In all three constructions, masking cones and reversing complete cone pieces preserves the same-segment absolute-sum domination. Thus $\rho(w)\le\rho(v)$ and all prefix signs are inward. Lemma <a href="#clip:crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:crossing">19</a> proves the test-cost bound. ◻

</details>

For each selection procedure the selected endpoint satisfies <a id="clip:eighth-ratio"></a>$$\label{clip:eighth-ratio}
 \max_\pm\|x\pm ty\|\ge
 \frac{1+st\|y\|/8}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}},$$ and the other endpoint has norm at least one. If $\|y\|\ge1$, then for every fixed $t>0$ the ratio exceeds one by a uniform amount when $s>0$ is sufficiently small. The previously described center approximation then gives a uniform positive averaged gain. This route spends a second factor two to impose the selected sign; the paired procedure uses both test evaluations instead.

<a id="dir:primal:add:selection"></a>
#### Selection order and whole-crossing replacement

In this subsection we specialize to the finite-height forest dual $X$. The constructions in Proposition <a href="#clip:selection-prop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:selection-prop">23</a> allow two further choices worth distinguishing. The first chooses its prefix budget only after selecting a global clipped part. The second pays for a whole crossing square with a single shortened head segment.

<a id="dir:primal:add:global"></a>
###### Choosing the budget after a global clip.

Let $0<t\le1$, $\|x\|=1$, and put $\eta=t^2/1000$. Choose a finite ancestral $H$ with $\|Q_Hx\|<\eta$, and a head test $f$ with $\rho(f)\le1$, $x(f)>1-2\eta$. For $z\in\ker P_H$ with $\|z\|\ge t$, choose a finite unit-ball tail test $v$ with $z(v)>3t/4$. Take positive and negative cumulative parts as in Proposition <a href="#clip:selection-prop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:selection-prop">23</a>. One global part $q$ has $|z(q)|>3t/8$; all its root prefixes are nonnegative. Now assign to each cone the prefix maximum of $q$, rather than that of $v$, $$M_g=\max_{u\in U_g}q([g,u]),\qquad \sum_gM_g^2\le1.$$ With $s=t/32$, choose the inward sign from the shortest head suffix of magnitude at least $sM_g$, taking a default sign when $M_g=0$, there is no parent, or there is no qualifying suffix. One of the two sign classes of cones retains absolute pairing greater than $3t/16$. Multiply that class by its common inward sign, and call it $w$. Then $\rho(w)\le1$. Lemma <a href="#clip:crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:crossing">19</a>, applied with baseline $q$ and its own prefix budgets, gives $\rho(f+sw)^2\le1+3s^2$. Since $|x(w)|<\eta$ and $z(f)=0$, <a id="dir:primal:add:global-gain"></a>$$\label{dir:primal:add:global-gain}
 \max_{\pm}\|x\pm z\|
 \ge\frac{1-2\eta-s\eta+3st/16}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}
 \ge1+t^2/1000.$$ Indeed, after replacing the denominator by $1+3s^2/2$, the numerator minus $(1+t^2/1000)(1+3s^2/2)$, divided by $t^2$, is $$\frac3{512}-\frac3{1000}-\frac t{32000}
 -\frac3{2048}-\frac{3t^2}{2048000}
 \ge\frac{2789}{2048000}>0.$$ Thus this order of selection keeps both losses $3t/8$ and $3t/16$.

<a id="dir:primal:add:whole-crossing"></a>
###### One head piece for a whole crossing.

Take a finite head test $f$ with $\rho(f)\le1$, a unit $y\in\ker P_H$, and a finite tail test $v$ with $\rho(v)\le1$, $y(v)>1/2$. The third construction in the proof of Proposition <a href="#clip:selection-prop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="clip:selection-prop">23</a> uses only $f$ and these tests, before any center is evaluated. With full cone budgets $q_g$ and threshold $s q_g$, it produces $w$ with $\rho(w)\le1$, $|y(w)|>1/8$, and inward root prefixes of magnitude at most $q_g$.

Consider a crossing segment with head $P$, and put $A=f(P)$, $B=w([g,u])$. If its cone is unselected or $B=0$, keep the head square. Otherwise $q_g>0$. If $|A|<s q_g$, its whole square is at most $A^2+3s^2q_g^2$. If $|A|\ge s q_g$, the selected suffix $I\subseteq P$, with sum $C=f(I)$, exists. When $A$ and $C$ have the same sign, the inward tail cancels and $|A+sB|\le|A|$. With opposite signs, $I$ is proper and $$|f(P\setminus I)|=|A|+|C|\ge|A+sB|.$$ In that case replace the whole head test by its preceding piece. All retained head pieces are disjoint. Small crossings cost at most $3s^2\sum_gq_g^2$, and wholly exterior segments cost at most $s^2$. We have proved, directly by whole-crossing replacement, <a id="dir:primal:add:four-cost"></a>$$\label{dir:primal:add:four-cost}
 \rho(f+sw)^2\le1+4s^2.$$ This differs from paying the cross term and tail square separately in <a href="#clip:shortening" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:shortening">[clip:shortening]</a>.

For an actual unit center $x$, take $s=t/64$ and $\eta=\min\{1/2,ts/[32(1+s)]\}$, for any $t>0$. Choose $f$ finite with $\rho(f)\le1$, $x(f)>1-\eta$, and a finite coordinate approximation $x_0$ within $\eta$ of $x$. Choose $H$ to contain both supports and their ancestors. For each unit tail $y$, the selected $w$ above satisfies $|x(w)|<\eta$. Hence <a id="dir:primal:add:four-gain"></a>$$\label{dir:primal:add:four-gain}
 \max_{\pm}\|x\pm ty\|
 \ge\frac{1-(1+s)\eta+ts/8}{\sqrt{1+4s^2}}
 \ge\frac{1+3ts/32}{1+ts/32}>1.$$ The last step uses $2s^2=ts/32$.

<a id="clip:approximate-center"></a>
#### Direct estimates at an approximate center

<a id="clip:approx-selected"></a>
<a id="clip:finite-transfer"></a>
Here $Z$ again denotes either coordinate model. We record the error calculation because it permits the tests to be chosen at the actual center, and it distinguishes paired testing from selection of one endpoint. Let $\|x\|=1$, let $x_0=P_Hx_0$ with $\|x-x_0\|\le\varepsilon$, and choose a head test $f$ with $\rho(f)\le1$. Suppose $$x(f)\ge1-\kappa\varepsilon,
 \qquad y\in\ker P_H,\qquad \|y\|\ge1.$$ Every exterior unit test $w$ satisfies $|x(w)|\le\varepsilon$. If a selected inward test has $|y(w)|\ge\theta$, then $$\label{clip:approx-selected}
 \max_\pm\|x\pm ty\|\ge
 \frac{1-\kappa\varepsilon-s\varepsilon+st\theta}
      {\sqrt{1+3s^2}},
 \qquad \min_\pm\|x\pm ty\|\ge1-\kappa\varepsilon.$$ These follow by evaluating on $f+sw$ with the chosen endpoint sign, and on $f$ itself. If $v=h_1-h_2$ and $y(v)\ge\theta$, testing both endpoints instead gives <a id="clip:approx-paired"></a>$$\label{clip:approx-paired}
 \frac{\|x+ty\|+\|x-ty\|}{2}
 \ge\frac{1-\kappa\varepsilon-s\varepsilon+st\theta/2}
           {\sqrt{1+3s^2}}.$$ Here the tail pairings combine exactly to $y(v)$, while the average of the two center errors is bounded below by $-s\varepsilon$. If one instead works first at $x_0$, with $x_0(f)\ge1-2\varepsilon$, and then transfers the norm estimate back to $x$, the corresponding paired bound is $$\label{clip:finite-transfer}
 \frac{\|x+ty\|+\|x-ty\|}{2}
 \ge\frac{1-2\varepsilon+st\theta/2}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}
       -\varepsilon.$$

<a id="clip:ordered-accuracy"></a>
###### Choosing the test size before its accuracy.

The formulas also prove positivity without choosing a numerical test size in advance. With a half-norming tail and one global clip, first choose $0<s<1$ so that $(1+st/4)/\sqrt{1+3s^2}>1$. Then choose the center and head-test accuracy $\varepsilon$ small enough that $$\frac12\left[
 \frac{1-\varepsilon-s\varepsilon+st/4}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}
      +1-\varepsilon\right]>1.$$ The head normer and its finite approximation are chosen to this accuracy, and the cofinite subspace annihilates the resulting head. The choices depend on $t$, not on the unit tail. Their order separates the quadratic test cost from the approximation error.

<a id="clip:norming-kernel"></a>
###### Using a norming-functional kernel.

Let $\ell\in Z^*$ norm the actual center $x$, and use $F=\ker P_H\cap\ker\ell$. Then both endpoints have norm at least one, without a center error in that conclusion. A tail test with pairing above $3/4$ supplies one global clip with pairing above $3/8$. Choosing a head test with $x(f)\ge1-3\varepsilon$, formula <a href="#clip:approx-selected" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:approx-selected">[clip:approx-selected]</a> gives $$\max_\pm\|x\pm ty\|\ge
 \frac{1-3\varepsilon-s\varepsilon+3st/8}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}.$$ Averaging this bound with one exploits information from the added kernel that the finite-coordinate conditions alone do not supply.

<a id="clip:direct-paired"></a>
###### Testing the two endpoints directly.

For a half-norming tail, the difference decomposition and two head-plus-tail tests instead give $$\frac{\|x+ty\|+\|x-ty\|}{2}
 \ge\frac{1-3\varepsilon-s\varepsilon+st/4}{\sqrt{1+3s^2}}.$$ Here the tail gain is the pairing of the original tail test, divided by two; the center errors are estimated before averaging. Thus this procedure controls the sum directly. Formula <a href="#clip:finite-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:finite-transfer">[clip:finite-transfer]</a> records the other order: test a finite approximant first, then transfer both endpoint norms to the actual center.

<a id="clip:constant-four"></a>
###### Separating crossing and wholly exterior budgets.

For full-cone budgets, keep the sum decomposition $v=k_1+k_2$. One may bound a crossing by its retained or split head square plus $3s^2q_g^2$: $2s^2q_g^2$ pays the small cross term and $s^2q_g^2$ pays its tail square. Sum over crossings and then pay at most another $s^2$ for all wholly exterior segments. This gives $$\rho(f+sk_1)^2,\ \rho(f-sk_2)^2\le1+4s^2.$$ With a half-norming tail and a finite-center test of value at least $1-2\eta$, paired testing followed by center transfer therefore gives $$\frac{\|x+ty\|+\|x-ty\|}{2}
 \ge\frac{1-2\eta+st/4}{\sqrt{1+4s^2}}-\eta.$$ First choose $s>0$ so that the ratio at $\eta=0$ exceeds one, then choose $\eta$ from this positive gap. This retains the separate crossing and exterior accounting, including its constant $4$.

<a id="sec:primal-additions"></a>
### Frontier shifts and separated families

<a id="clip:frontier"></a>
#### A selected frontier shift

Cumulative clipping changes the increments only where the original test is supported. The next construction instead places a compensating value at each relevant gate. Its two possible perturbations recover the original tail through their difference, while the selected-endpoint argument keeps only half of the resulting maximum excess.

<a id="stmt-clip-frontier-prop"></a>
<div id="clip:frontier-prop" class="proposition">

<a id="clip:frontier-tests"></a>
<a id="clip:frontier-modulus"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Frontier shift and selected endpoint). *For finite $f=P_Hf$ and $v=Q_Hv$ with $\rho(f),\rho(v)\le1$, and every $s>0$, there is a finitely supported gate vector $a$ such that $$\label{clip:frontier-tests}
 \rho(a)\le1,\qquad \rho(a\pm v)\le2,\qquad
 \rho\bigl(f+s(a\pm v)\bigr)^2\le1+12s^2.$$ For either $Z=B_\infty$ or the finite-height forest space, this selected-endpoint method gives $$\label{clip:frontier-modulus}
 \widehat\delta_Z(t)\ge
 \frac{\sqrt{1+t^2/48}-1}{4}\qquad(0<t<1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-clip-frontier-prop"><summary>Proof of stmt-clip-frontier-prop</summary>

*Proof.* Put $q_g=\rho(v\mathbf1_{U_g})$, so $\sum_gq_g^2\le1$. At each attached gate with $q_g>0$, choose the shortest head suffix of magnitude at least $2sq_g$, if one exists, and let $\tau_g$ be its sign. Otherwise use $\tau_g=1$. Set $a(g)=-\tau_gq_g$ at the gates and zero elsewhere. The gates form an antichain, so $\rho(a)^2=\sum_gq_g^2\le1$. For $w=a\pm v$, every root prefix at $g$ has sign $-\tau_g$ or is zero, and magnitude at most $2q_g$. Also $\rho(w)\le2$.

In a disjoint testing family write the head and tail sums as $A_i,B_i$. The pure tail contribution $s^2\sum_iB_i^2$ is at most $4s^2$. A positive cross term with $|A_i|<2sq_g$ costs at most $8s^2q_g^2$. Their total is at most $8s^2$. For a larger positive term the selected suffix has sum $C_i$ opposite to $A_i$ and magnitude at least $2sq_g$. Splitting it off gains $$(A_i-C_i)^2+C_i^2-A_i^2
 \ge4s|A_i|q_g\ge2sA_iB_i.$$ The simultaneous cuts are disjoint, so all these larger terms are paid by the head norm. This proves <a href="#clip:frontier-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:frontier-tests">[clip:frontier-tests]</a>. Unattached roots contribute only to the pure tail budget.

For a finite unit center choose its head normer $f$ and a half-norming exterior test $v$, so $|y(v)|\ge1/2$ for $\|y\|\ge1$. One of $a+v,a-v$ has pairing of absolute value at least $|y(v)|$, because their difference is $2v$. Selecting that test and then the endpoint sign gives $$\max_\pm\|x\pm ty\|\ge
 \frac{1+st/2}{\sqrt{1+12s^2}}.$$ Take $s=t/24$. The ratio equals $\sqrt{1+t^2/48}$; testing on $f$ makes the other endpoint at least one. Finally approximate an arbitrary unit center within one quarter of this ratio’s excess. The average retains precisely the lower bound in <a href="#clip:frontier-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:frontier-modulus">[clip:frontier-modulus]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="dir:primal:add:root-shifts"></a>
#### An independent suffix threshold for root shifts

A root shift can be chosen with a suffix threshold independent of the size by which the shifted test is later multiplied. This retains a rational estimate and also permits prefix-maximum root weights.

<a id="stmt-dir-primal-add-independent-threshold"></a>
<div id="dir:primal:add:independent-threshold" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25**. *On either the finite-height forest or the infinite tree, let $f=P_Hf$, $v=Q_Hv$ be finite tests with $\rho(f),\rho(v)\le1$. Let finitely many $a_g\ge0$ satisfy $\sum_ga_g^2\le1$ and $|v([g,u])|\le a_g$ on every root prefix. Fix $\alpha>0$. For $a_g>0$, choose the shortest attached head suffix of magnitude at least $\alpha a_g$, if one exists, and let $\tau_g$ be its sign; otherwise put $\tau_g=1$. Strict thresholds may be used instead. Set $$a=-\sum_g\tau_ga_ge_g,\qquad h_+=a+v,\qquad h_-=a-v.$$ For $h\in\{a,h_+,h_-\}$ and $0<c\le\alpha/2$, <a id="dir:primal:add:independent-cost"></a>$$\label{dir:primal:add:independent-cost}
 \rho(h)\le2,\qquad
 \rho(f+ch)^2\le1+4c\alpha+4c^2.$$ Both the full cone norms $q_g$ and the prefix maxima $m_g$ may be used for $a_g$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dir-primal-add-independent-threshold"><summary>Proof of stmt-dir-primal-add-independent-threshold</summary>

*Proof.* Since the gates form an antichain, $\rho(a)^2=\sum_ga_g^2\le1$. Each root prefix of $h$ has sign $-\tau_g$ or is zero and magnitude at most $2a_g$, and $\rho(h)\le2$. Zero weights give zero cross terms; unattached roots have no head crossing.

For a disjoint test family write $A_i,B_i$ for head and tail sums and $L=\sum_iA_i^2\le1$. Small positive products, with $|A_i|\le\alpha a_g$, have total at most $2\alpha$. For every other positive product, the simultaneous suffix cuts in <a href="#clip:split-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:split-gain">[clip:split-gain]</a> release at least $2|A_i|\alpha a_g$. Their total release is at most $1-L$, whereas $|B_i|\le2a_g$. Thus the large positive products total at most $(1-L)/\alpha$. Since $\sum_iB_i^2\le4$, $$\sum_i(A_i+cB_i)^2
 \le L+2c\bigl(2\alpha+(1-L)/\alpha\bigr)+4c^2
 \le1+4c\alpha+4c^2.$$ This uses precisely $2c\le\alpha$. Strict thresholds use the same small class, including equality. Formula <a href="#clip:budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="clip:budgets">[clip:budgets]</a> verifies both proposed choices of weights. ◻

</details>

The next two endpoint estimates concern the finite-height forest dual $X$.

<a id="dir:primal:add:rational"></a>
###### The rational gap.

At a finite unit center take its head normer $f$, and a finite tail normer $v$ with $\rho(v)=1$ and $y(v)>1/2$, for unit $y\in\ker P_H$. Use $a_g=q_g$, so $\sum_gq_g^2=1$. The difference $h_+-h_-=2v$ gives one $|y(h)|>1/2$. The mixed head–tail pairings vanish, and $$\max_{\pm}\|x\pm ty\|
 \ge\frac{1+ct/2}{\sqrt{1+4c\alpha+4c^2}}
 \ge\frac{1+ct/2}{1+2c\alpha+2c^2}.$$ Choose $\alpha=t/16$, $c=t/32$, for any $t>0$. The ratio equals $1+d_0(t)$, where $$d_0(t)=\frac{5t^2}{512+3t^2}.$$ Approximation of any unit center by a finite unit center within $d_0(t)/2$ retains gain $d_0(t)/2$, with the same finite-coordinate annihilator.

<a id="dir:primal:add:root-actual"></a>
###### Prefix weights at the actual center.

Fix $t>0$, $0<c<t/6$, and put $L_c=\sqrt{1+12c^2}$, $b=1+ct-L_c>0$. Choose $$0<\eta<\min\{1,b/[2(1+ct+2c)]\},\qquad
 \gamma=b/(2L_c).$$ For any unit $x$, choose a finite ancestral $H$ with $\|Q_Hx\|<\eta$ and a head test $f$ with $\rho(f)\le1$, $x(f)\ge1-\eta$. For a unit $y\in\ker P_H$, choose a finite tail test $v$ with $\rho(v)\le1$, $y(v)>1-\eta$. In Lemma <a href="#dir:primal:add:independent-threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:primal:add:independent-threshold">25</a>, use prefix weights $a_g=m_g$, strict threshold $\alpha=2c$, and the deepest qualifying starting vertex, which is the shortest qualifying suffix. One $h\in\{h_+,h_-\}$ has $|y(h)|>1-\eta$, while $|x(h)|<2\eta$. Consequently <a id="dir:primal:add:root-actual-gain"></a>$$\label{dir:primal:add:root-actual-gain}
 \max_{\pm}\|x\pm ty\|
 \ge\frac{1-\eta+c((1-\eta)t-2\eta)}{\sqrt{1+12c^2}}
 >1+\gamma.$$ Indeed $L_c\le1+6c^2<1+ct$, and the numerator is $L_c+b-\eta(1+ct+2c)>L_c+b/2$.

<a id="dir:primal:add:fixed-bound"></a>
#### Two tests for a bounded separated family

The final variant uses a tail witness selected from an entire separated family. Its member need not annihilate the head. Testing the two endpoints with different primal vectors cancels that unknown head evaluation.

<a id="stmt-dir-primal-add-separated"></a>
<div id="dir:primal:add:separated" class="proposition">

**Proposition 26**. *For each $D\ge1$, there exists $c_D>0$ such that, if $x,z_i\in X$, $1\le\|x\|\le D$, $\|z_i\|\le2D$, and $\|z_i-z_j\|\ge1$ for distinct indices in an infinite sequence, then some $i$ satisfies $\max_{\pm}\|x\pm z_i\|>\|x\|+c_D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dir-primal-add-separated"><summary>Proof of stmt-dir-primal-add-separated</summary>

*Proof.* First choose $c>0$, depending only on $D$, with $D(\sqrt{1+16c^2}-1)<c/16$, and then choose $0<\eta<c/[64(1+c)]$. Choose a head $H$, a head test $f$, and a center tail error so that $\rho(f)\le1$, $x(f)>\|x\|-\eta$, and $\|Q_Hx\|<\eta$. Boundedness in the finite-dimensional head gives distinct indices with $\|P_H(z_i-z_j)\|<1/4$, hence their tail difference has norm greater than $3/4$. A finite tail test $v$, $\rho(v)\le1$, has $|(z_i-z_j)(v)|>1/2$. Rename one index so $|z_i(v)|>1/4$.

<a id="dir:primal:add:sixteen"></a>
Use prefix weights $a_g=m_g$ and strict threshold $2c m_g$ in Lemma <a href="#dir:primal:add:independent-threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:primal:add:independent-threshold">25</a>. The two perturbations $h_0=a$, $h_1=a+v$ differ by $v$, have norms at most one and two, and have the same inward prefix signs with magnitudes at most $2m_g$. One choice $h$ has $|z_i(h)|>1/8$. We retain the following direct whole-crossing estimate: $$\label{dir:primal:add:sixteen}
 \rho(f+ch)^2\le1+16c^2.$$ For a crossing write its entire sum as $A+B$, where $B$ already includes the factor $c$, so $|B|\le2cm_g$. If $|A|\le2cm_g$, its square is at most $A^2+12c^2m_g^2$. If $|A|>2cm_g$ and $AB\le0$, it is at most $A^2$. Otherwise the chosen suffix has sum $C$ opposite to $A$, with $|C|>2cm_g\ge|B|$, and $$(A-C)^2+C^2
 =A^2+2|A||C|+2C^2\ge(A+B)^2.$$ The simultaneous head cuts remain disjoint. Crossings and wholly head segments therefore cost at most $1+12c^2$; wholly tail segments add at most $c^2\rho(h)^2\le4c^2$. This proves <a href="#dir:primal:add:sixteen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:primal:add:sixteen">[dir:primal:add:sixteen]</a>.

Put $M=\max_{\pm}\|x\pm z_i\|$, and choose $\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}$ so that $\epsilon z_i(h)=|z_i(h)|$. Test $x+\epsilon z_i$ on $f+ch$, and $x-\epsilon z_i$ on $f$, and add. The head evaluations of $z_i$ cancel. With $L=\sqrt{1+16c^2}$, this gives <a id="dir:primal:add:two-test"></a>$$\label{dir:primal:add:two-test}
 M(1+L)\ge2(\|x\|-\eta)+c(1/8-2\eta).$$ Here $x(h)>-2\eta$. It follows that $$(1+L)(M-\|x\|)
 >c/16-2\eta(1+c)>c/32.$$ Thus $c_D=c/[32(1+L)]$ works. The parameter choice depends on the fixed bound $D$; no unit-tail hypothesis on the selected $z_i$ was used. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:radial"></a>
### A radial deficit and separate endpoint laws

After normalizing the lens radius to one, this argument uses the norm-one head-test sums $p(S_i)$ as replacement coefficients; the joint-budget proof used $\|x\|p(S_i)$. Estimating the coefficient change and squared-test slack separately gives a radial-deficit bound. The same calculation then controls each endpoint law.

<a id="stmt-thm-tail"></a>
<div id="thm:tail" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:atomic-tail"></a>
**Proposition 27** (A radial-deficit estimate). *For a finite ancestral $H$, $x=P_Hx$, and arbitrary $y\in X$ with $\|x\pm y\|\le R$, one has $$\label{eq:atomic-tail}
 \|Q_Hy\|\le(\sqrt2+\sqrt6)\sqrt{R(R-\|x\|)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-tail</summary>

*Proof.* If $R=0$, both vectors vanish. If $x=0$, contractivity of $Q_H$ gives $\|Q_Hy\|\le R$, which implies the theorem. In the remaining case, scale to $R=1$ and put $d=1-\|x\|$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a>, choose a norming test $p=P_Hp$ with $\rho(p)=1$ and $x(p)=\|x\|$.

<a id="eq:mixture-error"></a>
Fix a finite ancestral $K\supset H$. Represent each of $P_K(x+y)$ and $P_K(x-y)$ as a finite convex combination of atoms, as supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a>. On a finite probability space choose a uniform sign $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}$ and conditionally choose a represented atom $A=\sum_i a_i d_{S_i}$ from the corresponding combination. Then $$\mathbb EA=x,\qquad\mathbb E(\sigma A)=P_Ky.$$ For each outcome set $b_i=p(S_i)$, $A^\circ=\sum_i b_i d_{S_i}$, and $L=1-\sum_i b_i^2$. Replacing the original coefficients by these segment sums has the joint error estimate $$L+\sum_i(a_i-b_i)^2
 =1+\sum_i a_i^2-2A(p)\le2(1-A(p)).$$ Taking expectations and applying <a href="#eq:atom-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:atom-coefficients">[eq:atom-coefficients]</a> gives $$\label{eq:mixture-error}
 \mathbb EL\le2d,\qquad
 \mathbb E\|A-A^\circ\|\le\sqrt{2d}.$$

We have paid for coefficient replacement. It remains to control the tail of the replaced signed mean. At a gate $g$, let $b(g)$ be the crossing sum of the represented family, or zero. Segments entirely outside $H$ have replaced coefficient zero. Thus $A^\circ(e_g)=b(g)$ and its restriction to $J(U_g)$ has norm at most $|b(g)|$. Define finite gate vectors $$h(g)=\mathbb Eb(g),\qquad m(g)=\mathbb E|b(g)|.$$ Because $x(e_g)=0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cones">3</a> and <a href="#eq:mixture-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixture-error">[eq:mixture-error]</a> give $\|h\|_2\le\sqrt{2d}$. Take two independent copies of the atom data. Lemma <a href="#lem:opposite-crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:opposite-crossing">9</a> and independence yield $$\begin{split}
 \|m\|_2^2-\|h\|_2^2
 &=\mathbb E\sum_g\bigl(|b(g)b'(g)|-b(g)b'(g)\bigr)\\
 &\le\mathbb E(L+L')\le4d.
 \end{split}$$ Consequently $\|m\|_2\le\sqrt{6d}$. By the cone identity <a href="#eq:cone-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cone-decomposition">[eq:cone-decomposition]</a>, $$\|Q_H\mathbb E(\sigma A^\circ)\|\le\|m\|_2\le\sqrt{6d}.$$ Its distance to $Q_HP_Ky$ is at most $\sqrt{2d}$ by <a href="#eq:mixture-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixture-error">[eq:mixture-error]</a>. Let $K$ increase through an ancestral exhaustion. Lemma <a href="#lem:forest-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forest-structure">2</a> gives $P_Ky\to y$, proving <a href="#eq:atomic-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:atomic-tail">[eq:atomic-tail]</a> for $R=1$. Scaling gives the stated theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### A maximum bound from the radial estimate

The radial estimate also gives a bound for the maximum-based modulus defined in <a href="#eq:max-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:max-modulus">[eq:max-modulus]</a>, with a weaker constant than the joint-budget estimate. We retain this calculation to record the effect of the alternative normalization.

<a id="stmt-cor-max-modulus"></a>
<div id="cor:max-modulus" class="corollary">

**Corollary 28**. *For the specified dual norm on $X$, $$\overline\theta_X(t)\ge\frac{\sqrt{1+t^2/4}-1}{2}\qquad(t>0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-max-modulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-max-modulus</summary>

*Proof.* For a finite-head unit vector $u=P_Hu$ and unit $y\in\ker P_H$, put $R=\max\{\|u+ty\|,\|u-ty\|\}$. Convexity gives $R\ge1$. Since $(\sqrt2+\sqrt6)^2<16$, Proposition <a href="#thm:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tail">27</a> implies $t^2\le16R(R-1)$. Solving this quadratic inequality gives the asserted lower bound for $R-1$.

For an arbitrary unit $x$, choose an ancestral head $H$ with $\|Q_Hx\|<\eta<1$. Then $u=P_Hx/\|P_Hx\|$ is well defined and $\|x-u\|<2\eta$. The preceding bound for $R-1$, diminished by $2\eta$, therefore holds for all unit $y\in\ker P_H$ with center $x$. Take the infimum over these $y$, the supremum over finite-codimensional subspaces, and then let $\eta\downarrow0$. The bound is independent of $x$, so the final infimum proves the corollary. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Keeping the endpoint laws separate

The proof of Proposition <a href="#thm:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tail">27</a> estimates a signed mixture of the two atom laws. It does not require a small replacement error for either conditional law separately. The next proposition supplies that representation-level information.

<a id="stmt-prop-endpoint"></a>
<div id="prop:endpoint" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:endpoint-coefficients"></a>
<a id="eq:endpoint-gates"></a>
**Proposition 29**. *Let $0\le d<1$, let $H$ be finite and ancestral, and suppose $x=P_Hx$, $\|x\|\ge1-d$, and $\|x\pm y\|\le1$. Fix a head norming test $p$ with $\rho(p)=1$ and $x(p)=\|x\|$, and a finite ancestral set $K\supset H$. For each $\tau\in\{-1,1\}$ choose any finite represented atom law with mean $P_K(x+\tau y)$, and denote its expectation by $\mathbb E_\tau$. For an atom $A=\sum_i a_i d_{S_i}$, put $b_i=p(S_i)$, $A^\circ=\sum_i b_i d_{S_i}$, and $L=1-\sum_i b_i^2$. Then $$\label{eq:endpoint-coefficients}
 \mathbb E_\tau\!\left[L+\sum_i(a_i-b_i)^2\right]\le4d,
 \qquad \mathbb E_\tau\|A-A^\circ\|\le2\sqrt d.$$ If $b(g)$ is the replaced crossing sum at gate $g$, put $m_\tau(g)=\mathbb E_\tau|b(g)|$ and $m=(m_1+m_{-1})/2$. These nonnegative vectors satisfy $$\label{eq:endpoint-gates}
 m_\tau\le2m\text{ coordinatewise},\qquad\|m\|_2\le\sqrt{6d}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-endpoint"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-endpoint</summary>

*Proof.* The test exists by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a>, since $\|x\|\ge1-d>0$. Both endpoint evaluations on $p$ are at most one, so $|y(p)|\le1-\|x\|\le d$ and $(x\pm y)(p)\ge1-2d$. The coefficient identity from the proof of Proposition <a href="#thm:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tail">27</a> gives $$\mathbb E_\tau\!\left[L+\sum_i(a_i-b_i)^2\right]
 \le2\bigl(1-(x+\tau y)(p)\bigr)\le4d.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:atom-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:atom-coefficients">[eq:atom-coefficients]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz give the second bound in <a href="#eq:endpoint-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-coefficients">[eq:endpoint-coefficients]</a>.

Combine the laws with equal probabilities. Their mean is $x$, whose pairing with $p$ is at least $1-d$. The coefficient and opposite-crossing calculation in the proof of Proposition <a href="#thm:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tail">27</a> therefore gives $\|m\|_2\le\sqrt{6d}$. The coordinate inequality in <a href="#eq:endpoint-gates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-gates">[eq:endpoint-gates]</a> follows from $m=(m_1+m_{-1})/2$.

These separate-law bounds also give a tail estimate directly. The cone identity implies $$\|Q_H\mathbb E_\tau A^\circ\|\le\|m_\tau\|_2\le2\sqrt{6d},
 \qquad Q_H\mathbb E_\tau A=\tau Q_HP_Ky.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:endpoint-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-coefficients">[eq:endpoint-coefficients]</a> gives $\|Q_HP_Ky\|\le2\sqrt d+2\sqrt{6d}$. Ancestral exhaustion yields $$\|Q_Hy\|\le(2+2\sqrt6)\sqrt d.$$ The larger constant records the cost of retaining the two conditional laws separately; Proposition <a href="#thm:tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tail">27</a> uses their signed mixture. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:joint-consequences"></a>
### Further consequences of the joint budget

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### Separate endpoint recovery and strict gauge representations

The signed mixture in Theorem <a href="#mol:prop:joint-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mol:prop:joint-budget">1</a> can be replaced by the recovery of one conditional endpoint. This pays for the fact that a conditional absolute mass can be twice the mixed mass. The next statement keeps that loss explicit.

<a id="stmt-mol-prop-conditional-joint"></a>
<div id="mol:prop:conditional-joint" class="proposition">

<a id="mol:eq:conditional-joint"></a>
<a id="mol:eq:conditional-joint-tail"></a>
<a id="mol:eq:strict-gauge-tail"></a>
**Proposition 30**. *Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#mol:prop:joint-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mol:prop:joint-budget">1</a>, assume in addition that $P_Hy=0$ and $r=\|x\|>0$, and put $\Delta=R^2-r^2$. Fix a head norming test $p=P_Hp$ with $\rho(p)=1$ and $x(p)=r$. For every finite ancestral $K\supset H$ and each sign $\tau\in\{-1,1\}$, choose any finite law of represented vectors $$A=\sum_i a_i d_{S_i},\qquad \sum_i a_i^2\le R^2,
 \qquad \mathbb E_\tau A=P_K(x+\tau y),$$ where each represented family consists of disjoint segments. Put $$b_i=r p(S_i),\qquad A^\circ=\sum_i b_i d_{S_i},\qquad
 V=\sum_i(a_i-b_i)^2,\qquad L=r^2-\sum_i b_i^2.$$ These laws exist by the finite atomic-ball formula. For every such choice, the replacement satisfies $$\label{mol:eq:conditional-joint}
 \mathbb E_\tau(V+L)\le\Delta,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_\tau\|Q_H(A-A^\circ)\|\le\sqrt\Delta.$$ At each gate $g$, let $b(g)$ be the replaced crossing coefficient, or zero when there is no crossing segment. The mixed absolute crossing vector $m(g)=\bigl(\mathbb E_1|b(g)|+\mathbb E_{-1}|b(g)|\bigr)/2$ satisfies $\|m\|_2\le\sqrt{2\Delta}$. Consequently a separate endpoint argument, followed by ancestral exhaustion, yields $$\label{mol:eq:conditional-joint-tail}
 \|y\|\le(1+2\sqrt2)\sqrt\Delta.$$ If the same two nonnegative errors are bounded separately, without retaining their sum, the corresponding calculation gives $$\label{mol:eq:strict-gauge-tail}
 \|y\|\le(1+2\sqrt3)\sqrt\Delta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mol-prop-conditional-joint"><summary>Proof of stmt-mol-prop-conditional-joint</summary>

*Proof.* The head support of $p$ and $P_Hy=0$ imply $P_K(x+\tau y)(p)=r$ for both signs. Expanding exactly as in <a href="#mol:eq:joint-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:joint-identity">[mol:eq:joint-identity]</a> gives the first assertion of <a href="#mol:eq:conditional-joint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:conditional-joint">[mol:eq:conditional-joint]</a>, and Cauchy–Schwarz gives the second. For the equal mixture, put $\eta=\mathbb EV$ and $\theta=\mathbb EL$. Then $\eta+\theta\le\Delta$, so $\eta+2\theta\le2\Delta$. Thus <a href="#mol:eq:joint-absolute-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:joint-absolute-mass">[mol:eq:joint-absolute-mass]</a> bounds $\|m\|_2$ as asserted. Writing $m_\tau(g)=\mathbb E_\tau|b(g)|$, positivity gives $m_\tau\le2m$ coordinatewise. Hence $$\|Q_H\mathbb E_\tau A^\circ\|
 \le\|m_\tau\|_2\le2\sqrt{2\Delta}.$$ The original conditional tail is $\tau P_Ky$. Adding its replacement error gives $\|P_Ky\|\le(1+2\sqrt2)\sqrt\Delta$ for every finite ancestral $K\supset H$. Along an ancestral exhaustion, $P_Ky\to y$ in norm, proving <a href="#mol:eq:conditional-joint-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:conditional-joint-tail">[mol:eq:conditional-joint-tail]</a> for the completed vector.

Alternatively, use only $\eta\le\Delta$ and $\theta\le\Delta$. Then $\eta+2\theta\le3\Delta$, and the identical endpoint recovery proves <a href="#mol:eq:strict-gauge-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:strict-gauge-tail">[mol:eq:strict-gauge-tail]</a>. This calculation is useful when starting from the gauge definition. First let $x$ and $y$ have finite coordinate support. For every $\lambda>\max\{\|x+y\|,\|x-y\|\}$ there are finite convex representations with coefficient radii at most $\lambda$. Carrying out the calculation with $\lambda$ gives $$\|y\|\le(1+2\sqrt3)\sqrt{\lambda^2-r^2}.$$ Letting $\lambda\downarrow R$ gives <a href="#mol:eq:strict-gauge-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mol:eq:strict-gauge-tail">[mol:eq:strict-gauge-tail]</a>, without assuming that the infimum in the gauge definition is attained. For a completed tail $y$, first apply this finite-coordinate argument to $P_Ky$, where $K\supset H$ is finite and ancestral. Contractivity bounds both projected endpoint norms by $R$, and $P_Kx=x$. The resulting estimate for $P_Ky$ passes to $y$ along an ancestral exhaustion, as in the preceding proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### A radial deficit budget

A different normalization puts both endpoints in the unit ball and replaces their coefficients by the norm-one head-test sums. In the notation of the original cancellation proof, let $d=1-\|x\|$. The joint estimate is $\eta+\theta\le2d$, with $\eta=\mathbb E\sum_i(a_i-p(S_i))^2$ and $\theta=\mathbb E(1-\sum_i p(S_i)^2)$. Consequently <a id="mol:eq:radial-budget"></a>$$\label{mol:eq:radial-budget}
 \|m\|_2^2\le\eta+2\theta\le4d,
 \qquad
 \|Q_Hy\|\le(2+\sqrt2)\sqrt d.$$ The second inequality follows by adding the mixed replacement error $\sqrt\eta\le\sqrt{2d}$ to $\|m\|_2\le2\sqrt d$. This retains the radial-budget route and its constants; it uses neither individual conditional error bounds nor equality of the two endpoint head projections. After scaling, it reads $$\|Q_Hy\|\le(2+\sqrt2)\sqrt{R(R-\|x\|)}.$$ The coefficient $2+\sqrt2<4$ gives the convenient consequence $\|y\|^2\le16R(R-\|x\|)$ for a zero-head displacement.

<a id="presentation:constructions"></a>
### Atomic gauges and finite-coordinate polars

The forest space also admits two direct constructions. Identify a coefficient vector $x\in c_{00}(\mathcal F)$ with $\sum_v x(v)e_v^*$, and let $\mathcal A$ be the atoms defined above, including zero. Define $$a(x)=\inf\{\lambda>0:x\in\lambda\operatorname{conv}\mathcal A\},
 \qquad
 q(x)=\sup_{\substack{u\in c_{00}(\mathcal F)\\\rho(u)\le1}}
       \left|\sum_v x(v)u(v)\right|.$$ Let $X_a$ and $X_q$ be the respective completions.

<a id="stmt-presentation-identity"></a>
<div id="presentation:identity" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31**. *The coordinate identity satisfies $a(x)=q(x)=\|x\|$ on $c_{00}(\mathcal F)$ and extends to surjective linear isometries $$X_a=X_q=X=J^*.$$ The finite unit balls and completed atomic ball are therefore exactly those of Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a> and Corollary <a href="#cor:closed-atomic-ball" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:closed-atomic-ball">8</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-presentation-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-presentation-identity</summary>

<a id="presentation:gauge-bounds"></a>
*Proof.* The convex hull of $\mathcal A$ is symmetric and absorbing. Disjointness bounds every atom’s coordinates by one, while singleton segments make $x/\|x\|_2$ an atom for $x\ne0$. Hence its gauge is a norm and $$\label{presentation:gauge-bounds}
 \|x\|_\infty\le a(x)\le\|x\|_2.$$ For every $\lambda>a(x)$ there is a finite convex representation $$x=\sum_{k=1}^N\theta_k A_k,\qquad
 \theta_k\ge0,\quad\sum_k\theta_k=1,
 \qquad
 A_k=\sum_i c_{ki}d_{S_{ki}},\quad
 \sum_i c_{ki}^2\le\lambda^2.$$ Indeed, the defining infimum gives such a representation at some radius less than $\lambda$; adjoining the zero atom permits any larger radius. No attainment of the gauge infimum is used here.

If every segment meets $E\subset\mathcal F$ in a segment or the empty set, then $P_E$ preserves atoms: intersect their disjoint segment families with $E$ and discard empty terms. Thus $P_E$ is contractive for $a$ and extends to $X_a$. This applies to ancestral sets, descendant subtrees, and unions of incomparable descendant subtrees. Along a finite ancestral exhaustion, $P_Hx\to x$ in $X_a$, because these projections are contractive and eventually fix each finite coordinate vector.

Density of $c_{00}(\mathcal F)$ in $J$ gives $q(x)=\|x\|$; tests approaching the unit ball may be rescaled to have norm at most one. Every atom belongs to $B_X$, so $\|x\|\le a(x)$. Conversely, place the support of a nonzero $x$ in a finite ancestral set $H$. Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a> expresses $x/\|x\|$ as a finite convex combination of atoms supported in $H$, giving $a(x)\le\|x\|$. The zero vector is immediate. This proves equality of the three norms. Coordinate density in Lemma <a href="#lem:forest-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forest-structure">2</a> makes both completion maps onto $X$.

In particular the component duality already proved in that lemma applies to the finite polar construction without a change of norm: $q_h=q|_{c_{00}(T_h)}$ is the norm inherited from $J_h^*$, and $$q(x)^2=\sum_h q_h(x|_{T_h})^2
 \qquad(x\in c_{00}(\mathcal F)).$$ On each component the inherited bounds are $$\frac{\|x\|_2}{\sqrt{h+1}}\le q_h(x)\le\|x\|_2
 \qquad(x\in c_{00}(T_h)).$$ Thus the polar completion has the asserted component decomposition and is reflexive. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-presentation-rescaled-closed-ball"></a>
<div id="presentation:rescaled-closed-ball" class="remark">

*Remark 32* (Rescaled finite approximants). There is also a direct closed-ball argument from the strict gauge infimum. If $a(x)\le1$ in the completion, choose finite $x_n$ with $a(x_n-x)<1/n$. Then $a(x_n)\le1+1/n<1+2/n$, so the strict infimum and balance of the convex hull give $x_n/(1+2/n)\in\operatorname{conv}\mathcal A$. These vectors converge to $x$. Conversely, every atom has norm at most one, so the closed convex hull is contained in the completed unit ball. The same argument applies to the infinite-tree gauge of Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a>.

</div>

The atomic gauge also gives a direct proof of the subtree formula.

<a id="stmt-presentation-subtrees"></a>
<div id="presentation:subtrees" class="proposition">

**Proposition 33**. *Let $(r_j)$ be a finite or countable family of incomparable vertices, and put $U_j=\{v:r_j\preceq v\}$. For $x\in X_a$ supported in $\bigcup_jU_j$, $$a(x)^2=\sum_j a(P_{U_j}x)^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-presentation-subtrees"><summary>Proof of stmt-presentation-subtrees</summary>

*Proof.* First take a finite family and $x\in c_{00}(\mathcal F)$. A segment meets at most one $U_j$. Consequently the squared coefficient norms of an atom’s restrictions sum to at most one. The seminorm $z\mapsto(\sum_j a(P_{U_j}z)^2)^{1/2}$ is at most one on every atom, so it is at most $a(z)$ by convexity and passage through radii $\lambda>a(z)$.

For the reverse inequality, choose $\lambda_j>a(P_{U_j}x)$. Write each $P_{U_j}x$ as a finite convex average $\sum_{k_j}\theta_{j,k_j}A_{j,k_j}$ of disjoint-segment sums with coefficient norm at most $\lambda_j$. Project these representations onto $U_j$, preserving that bound. For each tuple $(k_1,\ldots,k_m)$, the sum $\sum_j A_{j,k_j}$ still uses disjoint segments and has coefficient norm at most $(\sum_j\lambda_j^2)^{1/2}$. Give this sum the product weight $\prod_j\theta_{j,k_j}$. These weights are nonnegative and sum to one, and their average is $\sum_jP_{U_j}x=x$. Therefore $$a(x)\le\left(\sum_j\lambda_j^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Let each $\lambda_j$ decrease to $a(P_{U_j}x)$, including when this value is zero. This proves the finite-coordinate identity.

Projection onto the union of the subtrees preserves atoms. Apply it to finite-coordinate approximants of $x$ to obtain the finite-family identity in the completion. For a countable family, the finite-subtree truncations converge: a finite-coordinate approximant in the union meets only finitely many subtrees, and projection onto the remaining subtrees has norm at most one. The finite-family formula passes to the limit and proves the asserted sum.

Taking the component roots gives the atomic construction’s outer $\ell_2$ sum directly. Project atomic representations onto $T_h$. Every resulting atom has Euclidean norm at most $\sqrt{h+1}$, since its segments are disjoint and each has at most $h+1$ vertices. Convexity and the strict-radius limit therefore give $\|x\|_2\le\sqrt{h+1}\,a(x)$; the reverse Hilbert bound is <a href="#presentation:gauge-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="presentation:gauge-bounds">[presentation:gauge-bounds]</a>. The components are thus Hilbert-isomorphic, and the $\ell_2$-sum reflexivity argument of Lemma <a href="#lem:forest-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forest-structure">2</a> applies to this direct construction. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-presentation-weak-null-coordinates"></a>
<div id="presentation:weak-null-coordinates" class="remark">

*Remark 34*. The gauge gives a direct whole-dual proof of coordinate weak nullity. For $L\in X_a^*$, <a href="#presentation:gauge-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="presentation:gauge-bounds">[presentation:gauge-bounds]</a> yields $$|L(x)|\le\|L\|a(x)\le\|L\|\|x\|_2
 \qquad(x\in c_{00}(\mathcal F)).$$ Thus $L$ extends continuously to $\ell_2(\mathcal F)$ and $(L(e_v^*))_{v\in\mathcal F}$ is square summable. Its values tend to zero along every sequence of distinct vertices. In particular, the successive child increments are weakly null in $X_a$.

</div>

<a id="stmt-presentation-infinite-height"></a>
<div id="presentation:infinite-height" class="remark">

*Remark 35*. The full-dual identification uses the finite component heights. On $T_\infty=\mathbb N^{<\omega}$, let $J_\infty$ be the completion under the same segment norm, and let $B_\infty$ be the norm-closed coordinate span in $J_\infty^*$, as in Section <a href="#binf:definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:definition">3</a>. Then $$B_\infty\subsetneq J_\infty^*.$$ Indeed, fix an infinite branch $\beta$. The functional $f_\beta(u)=\sum_{v\in\beta}u(v)$ on finite vectors has norm one: its value is a finite segment sum, and evaluation on any coordinate unit vector of the branch attains one. It therefore extends to $J_\infty$. If $y$ is a finite coordinate functional, choose a vertex $v\in\beta$ beyond its support. Since $\rho(e_v)=1$, $$\|f_\beta-y\|\ge|(f_\beta-y)(e_v)|=1.$$ The same lower bound holds on the closed coordinate span $B_\infty$. The zero functional gives the reverse bound, so $\operatorname{dist}(f_\beta,B_\infty)=1$. Thus completing the finite coordinate polar norm yields the proper coordinate subspace here.

</div>

<a id="stab:toolkit"></a>
### A finite-coordinate toolkit

The estimates in this section apply to either coordinate model already constructed. We write $Z$ for the chosen model: the finite-height forest space $X$ of Proposition <a href="#presentation:identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="presentation:identity">31</a>, or the infinite-height coordinate predual $B_\infty$ of Section <a href="#binf:definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:definition">3</a>. In each case $J$ is the corresponding primal segment completion and $\rho$ is its norm. The finite atomic hulls and norming head tests are supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a> in the forest and Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a> in the infinite-height model.

For a finite ancestral set $H$, let $P_H$ be its coordinate projection and put $Q_H=I-P_H$. These projections are contractive in either model. Write $G_H$ for the minimal vertices of the complement of $H$. Thus $G_H$ includes the roots of forest components missing $H$; if $H$ is empty, it consists of all component roots. Its vertices are incomparable, and their descendant subtrees partition the complement. A crossing segment meets both $H$ and its complement and has a unique first exterior vertex, its gate. A root of a component missing $H$ carries no crossing coefficient.

<a id="stab:sign:coefficients"></a>
We use $d_S=\sum_{v\in S}e_v^*$ for a segment functional. The coefficient estimate holds in both models: $$\label{stab:sign:coefficients}
 \left\|\sum_i a_i d_{S_i}\right\|
 \le\left(\sum_i a_i^2\right)^{1/2}
 \qquad(S_i\text{ pairwise disjoint}).$$ Indeed, Cauchy–Schwarz bounds evaluation on $v\in J$ by the coefficient norm times $\rho(v)$. An atom includes its chosen representation $$A=\sum_i c_i d_{S_i},\qquad S_i\text{ pairwise disjoint},
 \qquad \sum_i c_i^2\le1.$$ A functional may have several such representations; all subsequent statements concern the chosen one.

<a id="stab:cone-bounds"></a>
We will use the following common form of the cone estimates from the forest construction and Lemma <a href="#binf:cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:cones">6</a>. For a finite antichain $G$ and vectors $u_g\in Z$ supported in its descendant subtrees, $$\label{stab:cone-bounds}
 \left(\sum_{g\in G}|u_g(e_g)|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le\left\|\sum_{g\in G}u_g\right\|
 \le\left(\sum_{g\in G}\|u_g\|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Tests supported on $G$ have $\rho$-norm equal to their Euclidean norm, which proves the lower bound and the contraction of restriction to an antichain. For the upper bound, combine finite disjoint segment tests from the separate subtrees to obtain $\sum_g\rho(P_{T_g}v)^2\le\rho(v)^2$, and apply Cauchy–Schwarz. Choosing almost maximizing families suffices. For countable $G$, finite partial sums give the same estimates whenever $\sum_g\|u_g\|^2<\infty$; the upper bound makes those sums Cauchy. If an existing vector is decomposed into its cone restrictions, its finite cone sums converge by coordinate approximation, so the finite estimates pass to that decomposition as well.

<a id="stab:coef:boundary"></a>
#### The exterior term and two boundary comparisons

Fix $p=P_Hp\in J$ with $\rho(p)\le1$. For a represented atom put $$\begin{gathered}
 s_i=p(S_i),\qquad L(A)=1-A(p),\qquad D(A)=1-\sum_i s_i^2,\\
 E(A)=\sum_i(c_i-s_i)^2,\qquad A^\circ=\sum_i s_i d_{S_i}.
 \end{gathered}$$ The evaluation deficit $L(A)$, squared-test slack $D(A)$, and coefficient error $E(A)$ are nonnegative; also $A^\circ$ is an atom. For each gate $g$, let $c_g(A),s_g(A)$ be the coefficient and head sum of the crossing segment through $g\in G_H$, or zero if there is none. There is at most one such segment in a disjoint family. Write $c(A)=(c_g(A))_g$ and $s(A)=(s_g(A))_g$, and define $$O(A)=\sum_{S_i\cap H=\varnothing}c_i d_{S_i},\qquad
 C(A)=\sum_{S_i\text{ crossing}}c_i d_{S_i\setminus H}.$$ Thus $Q_HA=O(A)+C(A)$. The letters $O$ and $C$ denote the wholly exterior part and the crossing part, respectively.

<a id="stmt-stab-coef-pointwise"></a>
<div id="stab:coef:pointwise" class="lemma">

<a id="stab:deficit-identity"></a>
<a id="stab:coef:exterior"></a>
<a id="stab:coef:gate-coordinates"></a>
**Lemma 36**. *For every represented atom, the exact identity is $$\label{stab:deficit-identity}
 2L(A)=1-\sum_i c_i^2+D(A)+E(A).$$ In particular, <a id="stab:coef:budget"></a>$$\label{stab:coef:budget}
 D(A)+\sum_i(c_i-s_i)^2
 =1+\sum_i c_i^2-2A(p)\le2L(A).$$ The full coefficient replacement satisfies <a id="stab:toolkit:replacement"></a>$$\label{stab:toolkit:replacement}
 \max\{\|A-A^\circ\|,\|Q_H(A-A^\circ)\|\}
 \le\sqrt{E(A)}\le\sqrt{2L(A)}.$$ Consequently, $$\label{stab:coef:exterior}
 \|O(A)\|\le\sqrt{2L(A)},\qquad
 \|c(A)-s(A)\|_2\le\sqrt{2L(A)}.$$ At the gates one has $$\label{stab:coef:gate-coordinates}
 A(e_g)=c_g(A)+O(A)(e_g),\qquad
 \|(O(A)(e_g))_g\|_2\le\|O(A)\|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-coef-pointwise"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-coef-pointwise</summary>

*Proof.* The identity is an expansion of the square; its right-hand inequality uses $\sum_i c_i^2\le1$. Applying <a href="#stab:sign:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:coefficients">[stab:sign:coefficients]</a> to the coefficients $c_i-s_i$ and using contractivity of $Q_H$ proves the replacement bound. For the exterior segments $s_i=0$, so the coefficient norm bound for disjoint segment functionals gives the first inequality in <a href="#stab:coef:exterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:exterior">[stab:coef:exterior]</a>. The crossing indices form a subset of the full coefficient array, giving the second. A crossing segment contains its gate, and all other contributions there come from $O(A)$, proving the coordinate identity. On any finite array supported on the gate antichain, $\rho$ equals the Euclidean norm: a segment meets that antichain at most once, and singleton tests give the reverse inequality. Dual testing proves the final contraction, also for an infinite gate set by taking finite subsets. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stab-coef-head-conflict"></a>
<div id="stab:coef:head-conflict" class="lemma">

<a id="stab:coef:head-conflict-eq"></a>
**Lemma 37**. *Two represented atoms $A,B$, tested against the same $p$, satisfy $$\label{stab:coef:head-conflict-eq}
 2\sum_{g:s_g(A)s_g(B)<0}|s_g(A)s_g(B)|\le D(A)+D(B).$$ There are two direct proofs: one discards a suffix of the longer head, and the other splits the longer segment into two pieces.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-coef-head-conflict"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-coef-head-conflict</summary>

*Proof by truncation.* At an opposite-sign gate, both head segments end at the parent of that gate. Their starting vertices lie on the same ancestor chain, so the heads are nested. They are unequal because equal heads have equal sums on $p$. Suppose $A$ has the longer head, with sum $s$, and the shorter head of $B$ has sum $s'$. Replace the segment of $A$ by its prefix ending just before the shorter head starts. This prefix is nonempty, and its sum is $s-s'$. Its squared sum increases by $$(s-s')^2-s^2=(s')^2-2ss'\ge2|ss'|.$$ Perform these truncations at every opposite-sign gate where $A$ has the longer head. Each original segment is used at most once, since it has only one gate. The new segments are subsets of the old ones, so they remain disjoint. Their total squared test sum is at most one, and hence the total increase is at most $D(A)$. The other nesting direction has budget $D(B)$. Adding proves the assertion.

This comparison can also be indexed first by the last interior vertex: at most one crossing segment of an atom uses any given such vertex, and the same nesting proof applies. Before using a cancellation of coordinates, however, one must reindex by the *first exterior* vertex. Different children of one interior vertex are different coordinates. Applying the same comparison after this reindexing gives <a href="#stab:coef:head-conflict-eq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:head-conflict-eq">[stab:coef:head-conflict-eq]</a> in precisely the form needed below. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-coef-head-conflict-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-coef-head-conflict</summary>

*Proof by splitting.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:opposite-crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:opposite-crossing">9</a> to the two underlying segment families. In its notation, $L_{\mathcal S}=D(A)$, $L_{\mathcal S'}=D(B)$, and the crossing sums are $s_g(A),s_g(B)$. Its simultaneous two-piece cuts retain the longer segment’s original endpoint and give exactly <a href="#stab:coef:head-conflict-eq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:head-conflict-eq">[stab:coef:head-conflict-eq]</a>. The proof uses only nesting of finite paths and the bound on their squared $p$-sums, so it applies unchanged in either coordinate model. Gates that are untouched component roots have zero crossing sums and contribute nothing. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stab-coef-original-products"></a>
<div id="stab:coef:original-products" class="lemma">

**Lemma 38**. *For the original crossing coefficients, <a id="stab:coef:original-products-eq"></a>$$\label{stab:coef:original-products-eq}
 \sum_g(-c_g(A)c_g(B))_+
 \le L(A)+L(B)+\sqrt{2L(A)}+\sqrt{2L(B)},$$ where $r_+=\max(r,0)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-coef-original-products"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-coef-original-products</summary>

*Proof.* The map $r\mapsto(-r)_+$ is one-Lipschitz. The difference between the product sums formed with $c$ and with $s$ is therefore bounded by $$\sum_g|c_g(A)-s_g(A)|\,|c_g(B)|
 +\sum_g|s_g(A)|\,|c_g(B)-s_g(B)|.$$ The crossing arrays $c(B)$ and $s(A)$ have Euclidean norms at most one. Cauchy–Schwarz and Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:pointwise" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:pointwise">36</a> bound this error by $\sqrt{2L(A)}+\sqrt{2L(B)}$. Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:head-conflict" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:head-conflict">37</a> bounds the remaining sum by $(D(A)+D(B))/2\le L(A)+L(B)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stab:coef:means"></a>
#### From scalar means to a tail norm

Only finite probability spaces occur in the next two lemmas. This allows every expectation, product measure, and maximizing segment to be chosen before any passage to the completion.

<a id="stmt-stab-coef-gate-bound"></a>
<div id="stab:coef:gate-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="stab:coef:mean-head"></a>
<a id="stab:coef:absolute-head"></a>
<a id="stab:coef:via-head"></a>
<a id="stab:coef:via-products"></a>
**Lemma 39**. *Let $A$ have a finite law of represented atoms and suppose $\mathbb E L(A)\le k$, where $k\ge0$. Write $$\beta=\|\mathbb E c(A)\|_2,\qquad
 M_c=(\mathbb E|c_g(A)|)_g,\qquad
 M_s=(\mathbb E|s_g(A)|)_g.$$ Then $$\begin{aligned}
 \|\mathbb E s(A)\|_2&\le\beta+\sqrt{2k},
       \label{stab:coef:mean-head}\\
 \|M_s\|_2^2&\le(\beta+\sqrt{2k})^2+4k,
       \label{stab:coef:absolute-head}\\
 \|M_c\|_2&\le\sqrt{(\beta+\sqrt{2k})^2+4k}+\sqrt{2k},
       \label{stab:coef:via-head}\\
 \|M_c\|_2^2&\le\beta^2+4k+4\sqrt{2k}.
       \label{stab:coef:via-products}
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality is obtained directly from the original products, whereas the preceding one first controls the signs of the head sums.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-coef-gate-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-coef-gate-bound</summary>

<a id="stab:signed-unsigned"></a>
*Proof.* The mean of $\|c-s\|_2$ is at most $\sqrt{2k}$, which proves <a href="#stab:coef:mean-head" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:mean-head">[stab:coef:mean-head]</a>. Put $a_g=\mathbb E(s_g)_+$ and $b_g=\mathbb E(-s_g)_+$. For independent copies $A,B$, the expected opposite-sign product sum is $2\sum_g a_gb_g$. Thus Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:head-conflict" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:head-conflict">37</a> and $\mathbb E D\le2k$ give $$4\sum_g a_gb_g\le2\mathbb E D\le4k.$$ Since $M_s=a+b$ and $\mathbb E s=a-b$, the same expansion gives $$\label{stab:signed-unsigned}
 \|M_s\|_2^2-\|\mathbb E s\|_2^2
 =2\mathbb E\sum_{g:s_g(A)s_g(B)<0}|s_g(A)s_g(B)|
 \le2\mathbb E D(A)\le4k.$$ Together with <a href="#stab:coef:mean-head" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:mean-head">[stab:coef:mean-head]</a>, this proves <a href="#stab:coef:absolute-head" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:absolute-head">[stab:coef:absolute-head]</a>. The coordinate inequality $||c_g|-|s_g||\le|c_g-s_g|$ gives $\|M_c-M_s\|_2\le\sqrt{2k}$, proving <a href="#stab:coef:via-head" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:via-head">[stab:coef:via-head]</a>.

For the other route put $u_g=\mathbb E(c_g)_+$ and $w_g=\mathbb E(-c_g)_+$. Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:original-products" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:original-products">38</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz in the probability space give $$2\sum_g u_gw_g
 =\mathbb E\sum_g(-c_g(A)c_g(B))_+
 \le2k+2\sqrt{2k}.$$ Now $M_c=u+w$ and $\mathbb E c=u-w$, so $\|M_c\|_2^2=\beta^2+4\sum_g u_gw_g$ gives <a href="#stab:coef:via-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:via-products">[stab:coef:via-products]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stab-sign-rooted"></a>
<div id="stab:sign:rooted" class="lemma">

<a id="stab:sign:absolute-masses"></a>
<a id="stab:sign:rooted-equality"></a>
**Lemma 40** (Rooted mixtures and tail assembly). *<span id="stab:coef:tail-assembly" label="stab:coef:tail-assembly"></span>Let $G$ be a finite antichain and, for each $g\in G$, let $$f_g=\sum_j a_{g,j}d_{[g,w_{g,j}]},\qquad
 M_g=\sum_j|a_{g,j}|,$$ where every sum is finite. Then $$\label{stab:sign:absolute-masses}
 \left\|\sum_g f_g\right\|\le\|(M_g)_g\|_2.$$ If the nonzero coefficients have one common sign for each fixed $g$, then $$\label{stab:sign:rooted-equality}
 \left\|\sum_g f_g\right\|
 =\left(\sum_g|f_g(e_g)|^2\right)^{1/2}.$$ Consequently, adding another rooted mixture with the same sign at each $g$ cannot decrease the norm.*

<a id="stab:coef:tail-assembly-eq"></a>
<a id="stab:coef:conditional-assembly"></a>
*In particular, for a finite law of represented atoms, put $M_c=(\mathbb E|c_g(A)|)_g$. For any real random variable $\theta$ with $|\theta|\le1$, $$\label{stab:coef:tail-assembly-eq}
 \|\mathbb E(\theta C(A))\|\le\|M_c\|_2.$$ If the law is an equal mixture of two laws with expectations $\mathbb E_+,\mathbb E_-$, then $$\label{stab:coef:conditional-assembly}
 \|\mathbb E_\pm C(A)\|
 \le\|(\mathbb E_\pm|c_g(A)|)_g\|_2\le2\|M_c\|_2.$$ The same estimates pass to norm limits of finite rooted mixtures when their absolute-mass vectors converge in $\ell_2$. In particular, countably many roots are permitted when their masses are square summable and the series at each root is absolutely summable.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-sign-rooted"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-sign-rooted</summary>

*Proof by convex products.* Ignore roots with $M_g=0$. At each remaining root, select $j$ with probability $|a_{g,j}|/M_g$ and choose the signed segment $\operatorname{sgn}(a_{g,j})d_{[g,w_{g,j}]}$. Make these choices independently at the finitely many roots. Put $r=\|(M_g)_g\|_2$. If $r=0$, all mixtures vanish. Otherwise multiply the selected segment at $g$ by $M_g/r$. The selected segments lie in incomparable subtrees and are disjoint, and their squared coefficients sum to one. Every resulting functional is therefore an atom. Its expectation is $(\sum_g f_g)/r$, which proves <a href="#stab:sign:absolute-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:absolute-masses">[stab:sign:absolute-masses]</a>.

For the crossing expectation in <a href="#stab:coef:tail-assembly-eq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:tail-assembly-eq">[stab:coef:tail-assembly-eq]</a>, the contribution at $g$ divided by $M_c(g)>0$ is a convex combination of signed segments starting at $g$, with zero allowed to account for $|\theta|<1$.
