Fix $\eta>0$. Choose two finite laws on represented atoms, with expectations $E_+,E_-$, such that $\|E_\pm m-z^\pm\|\le\eta$. For each atom put $$b_i=a(S_i),\qquad
 m_a=\sum_i b_i d_{S_i},\qquad
 D(m)=1-\sum_i b_i^2.$$ The coefficient identity gives $$D(m)+\sum_i(c_i-b_i)^2
 =1+\sum_i c_i^2-2m(a)\le2(1-m(a)).$$ Since each endpoint has the same evaluation on $a$, each law separately satisfies $$\label{molx:separate-deficits}
 E_\pm D(m)\le2(d+\eta),\qquad
 E_\pm\sum_i(c_i-b_i)^2\le2(d+\eta).$$ Consequently, for $e=\eta+\sqrt{2(d+\eta)}$, $$\label{molx:separate-error}
 \|z^\pm-E_\pm m_a\|\le e.$$ The estimates for separate laws here use $y(a)=0$; they do not follow from a bound on their mixture alone.

Only segments crossing from $A$ into its complement contribute to $Q_Am_a$. In each $U_r$ there is at most one such segment in a represented atom, because it must pass through $r$. Let $b_r(m)$ be its coefficient, or zero if it is absent. The restriction of $m_a$ to $J(U_r)$ has norm at most $|b_r(m)|$ and value $b_r(m)$ on the unit vector at $r$. Segments wholly outside $A$, including those in components disjoint from $A$, have corrected coefficient zero; their original coefficients have already been included in <a href="#molx:separate-deficits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molx:separate-deficits">[molx:separate-deficits]</a>.

<a id="molx:cross-conflict"></a>
The truncation comparison in Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:head-conflict" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:head-conflict">37</a>, applied to the test $a$ and the two represented families, gives $$\label{molx:cross-conflict}
 2\sum_{r:\,b_r(m)b_r(m')<0}|b_r(m)b_r(m')|
       \le D(m)+D(m').$$ The important choice here is the law of each sample: we compare one sample from each endpoint, instead of two samples from their mixture.

<a id="molx:min-mass"></a>
Now take $m$ from the plus law and $m'$ independently from the minus law. Set $$L_r^\pm=E_\pm|b_r(m)|,\qquad S_r^\pm=E_\pm b_r(m).$$ All these arrays have finite support. Independence and <a href="#molx:separate-deficits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molx:separate-deficits">[molx:separate-deficits]</a>–<a href="#molx:cross-conflict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molx:cross-conflict">[molx:cross-conflict]</a> yield <a id="molx:cross-law-product"></a>$$\label{molx:cross-law-product}
 \sum_r(L_r^+L_r^- -S_r^+S_r^-)\le4(d+\eta).$$ The endpoint sum $z^++z^-=2x$ vanishes outside $A$. By <a href="#molx:separate-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molx:separate-error">[molx:separate-error]</a> and the contractive root-coordinate map, $\|S^++S^-\|_2\le2e$. Therefore $$\sum_rS_r^+S_r^-=\tfrac14
  \bigl(\|S^++S^-\|_2^2-\|S^+-S^-\|_2^2\bigr)\le e^2,$$ and hence $$\label{molx:min-mass}
 \sum_r\min(L_r^+,L_r^-)^2
 \le\sum_rL_r^+L_r^-\le4(d+\eta)+e^2.$$

Let $y_r=y|_{J(U_r)}$ and let $e_r^\pm$ be the norm of the restriction of $z^\pm-E_\pm m_a$ to that subtree. Equation <a href="#eq:cone-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cone-decomposition">[eq:cone-decomposition]</a> and <a href="#molx:separate-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molx:separate-error">[molx:separate-error]</a> give $\|(e_r^\pm)_r\|_2\le e$. The restrictions of $z^\pm$ are $\pm y_r$, so $$\|y_r\|\le\min(L_r^+,L_r^-)+e_r^++e_r^-.$$ Combining <a href="#eq:cone-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cone-decomposition">[eq:cone-decomposition]</a>, the Euclidean triangle inequality, and <a href="#molx:min-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molx:min-mass">[molx:min-mass]</a>, we obtain the complete error estimate <a id="molx:minimum-budget"></a>$$\label{molx:minimum-budget}
 \|y\|\le\sqrt{4(d+\eta)+e^2}+2e,
 \qquad e=\eta+\sqrt{2(d+\eta)}.$$ Let $\eta$ decrease to zero. This gives <a href="#molx:minimum-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molx:minimum-conclusion">[molx:minimum-conclusion]</a>, since $\sqrt6+2\sqrt2<6$. ◻

</details>

<a id="molc:oriented-section"></a>
#### An ordered sign comparison

An ordered sign comparison gives a tail estimate in terms of the head deficit. We work in the infinite-height space $B_\infty$ of Remark <a href="#presentation:infinite-height" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="presentation:infinite-height">35</a>, on $T=T_\infty$, and write $J(a)=\rho(a)$ for its segment test norm. Finite coefficient arrays are identified with coordinate functionals. We use the contractive projections and norming head tests from Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a>, the exact finite atomic representations from Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a>, and the exterior cone estimates from Lemma <a href="#binf:cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:cones">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-molc-oriented-lens"></a>
<div id="molc:oriented-lens" class="proposition">

**Proposition 63**. *Let $A$ be a nonempty finite ancestral set and let $x,y\in B_\infty$ with $x=P_Ax$ and $P_Ay=0$. For every $\varepsilon\ge0$, $$\max\{\|x+y\|,\|x-y\|\}\le1,
 \qquad \|x\|\ge1-\varepsilon
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|y\|\le (\sqrt2+\sqrt{10})\sqrt\varepsilon.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-molc-oriented-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-molc-oriented-lens</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose that $y$ is finitely supported and choose a finite ancestral set $B\supseteq A$ containing its support. Choose a test $a$ supported in $A$ such that $J(a)=1$ and $\langle x,a\rangle=\|x\|$. Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a> supplies such a test when $x\ne0$; when $x=0$, any $J$-unit test supported in the nonempty set $A$ has the required equality.

<a id="molc:joint-budget"></a>
<a id="molc:replacement"></a>
Choose a uniform sign $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}$ and, conditionally on that sign, a finitely supported law of represented molecules $m=\sum_i\lambda_i\mathbf1_{S_i}$, with $S_i\subset B$ and $\sum_i\lambda_i^2\le1$, as supplied by Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a>, such that $$\mathbb E(m\mid\sigma)=x+\sigma y.$$ The representation includes the segment family and its coefficients. Thus $\mathbb Em=x$ and $\mathbb E(\sigma m)=y$. For each outcome define $$u_i=a(S_i),\qquad D=1-\sum_i u_i^2,\qquad
 H=\sum_i(\lambda_i-u_i)^2,\qquad
 \widetilde m=\sum_i u_i\mathbf1_{S_i}.$$ Both $D$ and $H$ are nonnegative, and $$\label{molc:joint-budget}
 \mathbb E(D+H)
 =\mathbb E\left(1+\sum_i\lambda_i^2-2\langle m,a\rangle\right)
 \le 2(1-\|x\|)\le2\varepsilon.$$ The nonempty suffixes $S_i\setminus A$ remain disjoint segments. Therefore, with $Q=I-P_A$, $$\label{molc:replacement}
 \|Q(m-\widetilde m)\|\le\sqrt H,
 \qquad
 \|y-\mathbb E(\sigma Q\widetilde m)\|
 \le\mathbb E\sqrt H\le\sqrt{2\varepsilon}.$$

Let $R$ consist of the minimal nodes of $B\setminus A$. These nodes are incomparable, and their descendant subtrees intersected with $B$ partition $B\setminus A$. For each $r\in R$, let $c_r$ be the coefficient $u_i$ of the segment crossing from $A$ through $r$, or zero if there is no crossing. A segment entirely outside $A$ has $u_i=0$, and each segment meets at most one member of $R$. Consequently $(Q\widetilde m)(r)=c_r$ and $$\sum_{r\in R}|c_r-m(r)|^2\le H.$$ Since $\mathbb Em=x$ vanishes on $R$, Jensen’s inequality gives <a id="molc:frontier-mean"></a>$$\label{molc:frontier-mean}
 \sum_{r\in R}(\mathbb Ec_r)^2\le\mathbb EH\le2\varepsilon.$$

<a id="molc:ordered-conflict"></a>
Compare two represented molecules, using primes for the second. Write $c^+=\max(c,0)$ and $c^-=\max(-c,0)$ coordinatewise. Retain only conflicts with $c_r>0>c'_r$ in the nonnegative sum of Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:head-conflict" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:head-conflict">37</a>. Its truncation proof, applied to these families and the test $a$, gives $$\label{molc:ordered-conflict}
 2\sum_{r\in R}c_r^+(c'_r)^-\le D+D'.$$ Thus the ordered estimate uses only one of the two opposite-sign orientations controlled by the common boundary comparison. Take two independent copies of the mixture law. Independence and <a href="#molc:ordered-conflict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molc:ordered-conflict">[molc:ordered-conflict]</a> imply $$\sum_{r\in R}(\mathbb Ec_r^+)(\mathbb Ec_r^-)
 \le\mathbb ED.$$ Thus the unsigned means satisfy <a id="molc:ten-deficit"></a>$$\label{molc:ten-deficit}
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{r\in R}(\mathbb E|c_r|)^2
 &=\sum_{r\in R}(\mathbb Ec_r)^2
     +4\sum_{r\in R}(\mathbb Ec_r^+)(\mathbb Ec_r^-)\\
 &\le 2\varepsilon+4\mathbb ED\le10\varepsilon.
 \end{aligned}$$ The coefficient $10$ results from retaining just one ordered conflict and then using the separate bounds on $\mathbb EH$ and $\mathbb ED$ in <a href="#molc:joint-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molc:joint-budget">[molc:joint-budget]</a>. Using both orientations in the common boundary comparison gives a smaller unsigned-mass bound; the present calculation records the cost of the ordered choice.

Only crossing segments contribute to $Q\widetilde m$. For a finitely supported test $b$, put $$L_r(b)=\max\{|b([r,s])|:s\in B,\ r\preceq s\}.$$ The segments attaining these finitely many maxima lie in disjoint subtrees, so $\sum_r L_r(b)^2\le J(b)^2$. Hence $$|\langle\mathbb E(\sigma Q\widetilde m),b\rangle|
 \le\sum_r\mathbb E|c_r|\,L_r(b)
 \le\sqrt{10\varepsilon}\,J(b).$$ Taking the supremum over $J(b)\le1$ and using <a href="#molc:replacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molc:replacement">[molc:replacement]</a> proves the finite-support estimate. If $R$ is empty, all these tail sums are zero and the same conclusion holds.

For general $y\in B_\infty$, apply the finite estimate to $x\pm P_By=P_B(x\pm y)$ along the exhaustion in Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a>. These pairs remain in the unit ball, and $P_By\to y$ in norm, so the same bound passes to the limit. ◻

</details>

<a id="molc:original-center-budget"></a>
###### The original-center budget.

Put $K=\sqrt2+\sqrt{10}$. Given $0<t<1$, choose $0<d<1$ with $K\sqrt{3d(1+2d)}<t$. For $x\in S_{B_\infty}$, choose a nonempty finite ancestral set $A$ with $\|x-P_Ax\|\le d$, and choose a norm-one functional $x^*$ with $x^*(x)=1$. Set $F=\ker P_A\cap\ker x^*$. If $y\in F$, $\|y\|\ge1$, and both $\|x\pm ty\|\le1+d$, then $$\|P_Ax\pm ty\|\le1+2d,\qquad \|P_Ax\|\ge1-d.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#molc:oriented-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="molc:oriented-lens">63</a> after dividing both vectors by $1+2d$, with deficit $3d/(1+2d)$. It gives $$t\|y\|\le K\sqrt{3d(1+2d)}<t,$$ a contradiction. Both original endpoint norms are at least $1$ because $x^*(x\pm ty)=1$, so their average exceeds $1+d/2$. This proves the averaged asymptotic modulus bound $d/2$ with the stated choice of $d$.

<a id="molc:half-tail-budget"></a>
###### The splitting variant and a half-tail budget.

<a id="molc:finite-ordered-lens"></a>
The splitting proof in Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:head-conflict" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:head-conflict">37</a> gives the same ordered inequality <a href="#molc:ordered-conflict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molc:ordered-conflict">[molc:ordered-conflict]</a>, since its squared-test gain is at least twice the absolute product of the two head sums at each opposite-sign gate. Using that proof with the same single orientation therefore retains the bound $10d$ above. In a finite ancestral set, let $L$ denote the dual norm of $J$, let the head $x$ have $L(x)=1$, and suppose $L(x\pm z)\le1+\eta$ with $z$ vanishing on the head. Applying this version with $d=1-1/(1+\eta)$ gives $$\label{molc:finite-ordered-lens}
 L(z)\le(1+\eta)(\sqrt{2d}+\sqrt{10d}).$$ For $0<t<1$, choose $\eta>0$ so that the right side is less than $t/2$. Approximate an arbitrary unit center within $\eta/4$ by a finite unit center on a head $A$. For any completed tail $y\in\ker P_A$ of norm at least $1$, choose a finite ancestral $B\supseteq A$ with $\|P_By\|>1/2$. If both norms at the finite center with perturbation $ty$ were at most $1+\eta$, projection into $B$ and <a href="#molc:finite-ordered-lens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molc:finite-ordered-lens">[molc:finite-ordered-lens]</a> would give $t\|P_By\|<t/2$. Both norms at the finite center are at least $1$. Passing back to the original center therefore gives an average greater than $1+\eta/4$.

<a id="mold:weaker-trimming"></a>
#### A weaker trimming budget

<a id="mold:weaker-trimming-bound"></a>
We work in the infinite-height coordinate predual $B_\infty$, with its atomic gauge presentation from Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a>. Let $D$ be a nonempty finite ancestral set, $x=P_Dx$, $\|x\|=1$, and $z\in\ker P_D\subset B_\infty$. We use $Q_D=I-P_D$. This calculation keeps only the bounds $\|Q_D\|\le2$ and a squared-test gain bounded below by the absolute product of the two opposite head sums per conflict, although the earlier lemmas give $\|Q_D\|\le1$ and at least twice that gain. We record the resulting larger error budget from these weaker inputs. Suppose $$0<h<1,\qquad \max\{\|x+z\|,\|x-z\|\}\le1+h.$$ Then $$\label{mold:weaker-trimming-bound}
 \|z\|\le(1+h)\bigl(2\sqrt{10h}+2\sqrt{2h}\bigr).$$ For the proof, first take $z$ finitely supported and choose a head-supported norming functional $f$ for $x$, using Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a>. Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a> represents $(x\pm z)/(1+h)$ by finite probability distributions of segment atoms $m=\sum_i a_i d_{S_i}$. Put $b_i=f(d_{S_i})$, $\widetilde m=\sum_i b_i d_{S_i}$, $L=\sum_i(a_i-b_i)^2$, and $\Delta=1-\sum_i b_i^2$. Since $L+\Delta=1+\sum_i a_i^2-2f(m)$ and $\mathbb E_\pm f(m)=1/(1+h)$, each conditional distribution satisfies $$\mathbb E_\pm L\le2h,\qquad \mathbb E_\pm\Delta\le2h.$$ Let $B_r=\widetilde m(e_r)$ at the roots immediately outside $D$, and use $\mathbb E$ for the equal mixture of the two distributions. Each segment meets at most one such root. Since $\mathbb E m=x/(1+h)$, $$\sum_r(\mathbb E B_r)^2\le\mathbb E L\le2h.$$ For independent outcomes, trimming the longer of two opposite-signed heads preserves disjointness and increases its squared evaluation by at least $|B_rB'_r|$. Retaining this bound on the gain gives $$\sum_{B_rB'_r<0}|B_rB'_r|\le\Delta+\Delta'.$$ The modifications can be simultaneous: different exterior roots use distinct segments of either list. Independence now gives $$\sum_r(\mathbb E|B_r|)^2
 =\sum_r(\mathbb E B_r)^2+
       2\mathbb E\sum_{B_rB'_r<0}|B_rB'_r|
 \le2h+8h=10h.$$ For either conditional distribution, the corrected tail has norm at most $\| (\mathbb E_\pm |B_r|)_r\|_2\le2\sqrt{10h}$. Indeed, in each exterior component its mean is a positive scalar times a convex combination of signed segment indicators; products of these finite combinations produce disjoint segment atoms. Recovering the uncorrected conditional tail costs at most $\|Q_D\|\mathbb E_\pm\sqrt L\le2\sqrt{2h}$, proving <a href="#mold:weaker-trimming-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mold:weaker-trimming-bound">[mold:weaker-trimming-bound]</a>. Approximation in $\ker P_D$, with an arbitrarily small increase of $h$, proves the completed-tail assertion; the required density is supplied by Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a>.

For $0<t<1$, choose $h\in(0,1)$ so that the right side of <a href="#mold:weaker-trimming-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mold:weaker-trimming-bound">[mold:weaker-trimming-bound]</a> is less than $t$. At a finite unit head, every displacement in the tail of norm at least $t$ makes one endpoint norm exceed $1+h$, while both endpoint norms are at least one. A finite unit approximation to an arbitrary unit center with error less than $h/4$ therefore gives $\widehat\delta_{B_\infty}(t)\ge h/4$.

<a id="pm-h-78"></a>
### Signs determined by the target tail

Work in the finite-height forest space $X=J^*$. For a finite ancestor-closed head $H$, put $P=P_H$ and $Q=I-P$; its gates $g$ are the minimal excluded nodes, and $T_g$ is the full descendant cone rooted at $g$. We use the represented atoms, head normers, and completed cone formula from Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a>, Corollary <a href="#cor:closed-atomic-ball" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:closed-atomic-ball">8</a>, and Proposition <a href="#presentation:subtrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="presentation:subtrees">33</a>.

<a id="molb:sec:target-signs"></a>
#### Signs chosen from the target tail

<a id="stmt-molb-prop-target-signs"></a>
<div id="molb:prop:target-signs" class="proposition">

<a id="molb:eq:target-sign-bound"></a>
<a id="molb:eq:target-separated"></a>
**Proposition 64**. *Suppose $Px=x$, $Qy=y$, and $\|x\pm y\|\le1$. Then $$\label{molb:eq:target-sign-bound}
 \|y\|\le(2\sqrt2+2\sqrt6)\sqrt{1-\|x\|}
                         \le8\sqrt{1-\|x\|}.$$ Consequently, if $\|p\pm u_j\|\le1$ and $\|u_i-u_j\|\ge\varepsilon>0$ for all distinct $i,j$ in an infinite sequence, then $$\label{molb:eq:target-separated}
 \|p\|\le1-\varepsilon^2/256.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-molb-prop-target-signs"><summary>Proof of stmt-molb-prop-target-signs</summary>

*Proof.* The case $x=0$ in <a href="#molb:eq:target-sign-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molb:eq:target-sign-bound">[molb:eq:target-sign-bound]</a> follows from $\|y\|\le1$. Otherwise choose a primal unit vector $z$ supported on $H$ with $x(z)=\|x\|$. Set $$\Delta=1-\|x\|,\qquad h_g=\|y|_{T_g}\|,\qquad
 R^2=\sum_gh_g^2=\|y\|^2,\qquad\theta=1/4.$$ If $h_g>0$ and $g$ has parent $p_g\in H$, inspect the terminal head intervals $[q,p_g]$ with absolute $z$-sum at least $\theta h_g$. When there are any, choose the shortest such interval and let $d_g\in\{-1,1\}$ be the sign of its sum. In all other cases put $d_g=1$. The signs depend on $z,y,H$, and will be the same for both endpoint approximations.

<a id="molb:eq:target-charge"></a>
For an atom $A=\sum_Ia_I\mathbf1_I$, put $b_I=z(I)$, $\widetilde A=\sum_Ib_I\mathbf1_I$, and $L_A=1-\sum_Ib_I^2$. Let $b_g$ be its crossing coefficient, zero if there is no crossing. We claim $$\label{molb:eq:target-charge}
 \sum_gh_g|b_g|
 \le\sum_gh_gd_gb_g+2\theta R^2+L_A/\theta.$$ Call a gate bad if $h_g>0$, $d_gb_g<0$, and $|b_g|\ge\theta h_g$. Its crossing head qualifies for the selection above; it strictly contains the selected shortest suffix, since the signs disagree. Delete that suffix and the tail from the crossing, retaining its nonempty initial part. The squared-test gain is at least $2|b_g|\theta h_g$. All bad gates use distinct segments, so the deletions preserve a disjoint test family and $$2\theta\sum_{g\text{ bad}}h_g|b_g|\le L_A.$$ At remaining gates with $d_gb_g<0$, the sum of $h_g|b_g|$ is at most $\theta\sum_gh_g^2=\theta R^2$. The identity $|b_g|=d_gb_g+2|b_g|\mathbf1_{\{d_gb_g<0\}}$ proves <a href="#molb:eq:target-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molb:eq:target-charge">[molb:eq:target-charge]</a>.

Fix $\rho>0$, and approximate each endpoint $x+\sigma y$, $\sigma=\pm1$, separately within $\rho$ by a finite atom mean. For its expectation, the usual coefficient identity gives $$\mathbb EL_A\le2(\Delta+\rho),\qquad
 \|\mathbb E\widetilde A-(x+\sigma y)\|
       \le\rho+\sqrt{2(\Delta+\rho)}=:E_\rho.$$ Here each law has this bound because $y(z)=0$. If $r_g=\|[\mathbb E\widetilde A-(x+\sigma y)]|_{T_g}\|$, then $\|(r_g)\|_2\le E_\rho$. Thus $$h_g\le\mathbb E|b_g|+r_g,
 \qquad |\mathbb Eb_g-\sigma y(g)|\le r_g.$$ Multiply by $h_g$, sum, and apply <a href="#molb:eq:target-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molb:eq:target-charge">[molb:eq:target-charge]</a> to obtain $$R^2\le\sigma\sum_gh_gd_gy(g)+2RE_\rho
                     +2\theta R^2+2(\Delta+\rho)/\theta.$$ All sums converge: $(h_g)\in\ell_2$, $|y(g)|\le h_g$, and the error-cone norms are square summable. Both signs yield this inequality with the same $d_g$. Select the sign for which the first term is nonpositive and let $\rho\downarrow0$. With $\theta=1/4$, $$\tfrac12 R^2\le2R\sqrt{2\Delta}+8\Delta.$$ Solving this quadratic proves <a href="#molb:eq:target-sign-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molb:eq:target-sign-bound">[molb:eq:target-sign-bound]</a>, including $\Delta=0$.

For the separated-sequence consequence, fix $\rho>0$ and choose a finite ancestor-closed $H$ with $\|Qp\|\le\rho$. The head projections of the bounded sequence have a pair with $\|P(u_i-u_j)\|\le2\rho$. Let $v=(u_i-u_j)/2$. Convexity gives $\|p\pm v\|\le1$, while $\|Pv\|\le\rho$ and $\|v\|\ge\varepsilon/2$. Therefore $$\|Pp\pm Qv\|\le1+2\rho,\qquad
 \|Pp\|\ge\|p\|-\rho,\qquad
 \|Qv\|\ge\varepsilon/2-\rho.$$ Apply <a href="#molb:eq:target-sign-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molb:eq:target-sign-bound">[molb:eq:target-sign-bound]</a> with its constant $8$ after division by $1+2\rho$, then let $\rho\downarrow0$. This gives $\varepsilon/2\le8\sqrt{1-\|p\|}$, which is <a href="#molb:eq:target-separated" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molb:eq:target-separated">[molb:eq:target-separated]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stab:sign:section"></a>
### Choosing signs at the boundary

The preceding tail estimates control cancellation by comparing signed and absolute means. We now retain more of the individual segment representations. Two sign choices will be useful. The first assigns one sign to every possible last vertex in the head; the second assigns one sign to every first vertex outside it. Both produce tail vectors whose norms can be recovered from their boundary coordinates.

The preferred-parent argument applies to either coordinate model $Z$. For the subsequent exit-sign and trimming arguments we use the infinite-height coordinate predual $B_\infty$ from Section <a href="#binf:definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:definition">3</a>. Finite representations and head tests come from the model’s finite-hull theorem; the coordinate projections are contractive and finite coordinate vectors are norm dense. Write $H$ for a finite ancestral set. Its gates $G_H$ are the first vertices outside $H$, including roots of untouched forest components. A segment crossing the boundary has an exterior portion $[g,w]$ with $g\in G_H$; the parent of $g$ is its last head vertex.

The shared coefficient estimate <a href="#stab:sign:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:coefficients">[stab:sign:coefficients]</a>, rooted-mass bound <a href="#stab:sign:absolute-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:absolute-masses">[stab:sign:absolute-masses]</a> and same-sign norm formula <a href="#stab:sign:rooted-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:rooted-equality">[stab:sign:rooted-equality]</a> will turn crossing coefficients into tail norms. We also use the two subtree estimates <a href="#stab:cone-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:cone-bounds">[stab:cone-bounds]</a>; the lower estimate reads the root coordinates, and the upper estimate combines the norms in incomparable subtrees.

<a id="pm-h-81"></a>
#### One preferred sign at each last head vertex

Grouping exits by their parent permits different child subtrees to share one sign. The loss is controlled by the conflict between two nested head segments ending at that parent.

<a id="stmt-stab-sign-parent-lens"></a>
<div id="stab:sign:parent-lens" class="proposition">

**Proposition 65**. *Let $H$ be finite and ancestral, $x=P_Hx$ with $\|x\|=1$, and $u\in\ker P_H$. For every $L>1$, <a id="stab:sign:parent-lens-bound"></a>$$\label{stab:sign:parent-lens-bound}
 \max\{\|x+u\|,\|x-u\|\}<L
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|u\|\le
 3\left(\sqrt{L^2-1}
            +2\sqrt{\frac{1-L^{-2}}2}\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-sign-parent-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-sign-parent-lens</summary>

<a id="stab:sign:parent-error"></a>
*Proof.* First suppose $u$ has finite support. The finite-hull theorem for $Z$ gives two finite laws of represented vectors $A=\sum_i c_i d_{S_i}$ with disjoint segments and $\sum_i c_i^2\le L^2$, such that $\mathbb E_+A=x+u$ and $\mathbb E_-A=x-u$. In $B_\infty$ these laws also follow directly from the strict gauge representation, since both endpoint norms are strictly less than $L$. Choose a head test $p=P_Hp$ with $\rho(p)=1$ and $x(p)=1$. For each represented outcome set $$s_i=p(S_i),\qquad D=1-\sum_i s_i^2.$$ Every such disjoint family is a test for $\rho$, so $D\ge0$. Both conditional expectations of $\sum_i c_is_i$ equal one. Expansion and Cauchy–Schwarz give, separately for both laws, $$\label{stab:sign:parent-error}
 \mathbb E_\pm\sum_i(c_i-s_i)^2\le L^2-1,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_\pm D\le1-L^{-2}.$$ For the second inequality, use $1\le L(\mathbb E_\pm\sum_i s_i^2)^{1/2}$.

Pool the laws with probabilities $1/2$, and write $\mathbb E$ for their mixture. At a vertex $v\in H$ there is at most one segment of an outcome that crosses with last head vertex $v$: two such segments would both contain $v$. Let $C_v$ be its $s_i$ value, or zero if it is absent. Define $$p_v=\mathbb E(C_v)_+,\qquad n_v=\mathbb E(-C_v)_+.$$ Take an independent second outcome, denoted by primes. If $C_vC'_v<0$, their head portions are distinct nested segments ending at $v$. Split the longer immediately before the shorter starts. If its old sum is $C_v$, the new sums are $C_v-C'_v,C'_v$; the gain in squared sums is $$(C_v-C'_v)^2+(C'_v)^2-C_v^2
       =2(C'_v)^2-2C_vC'_v\ge2|C_vC'_v|.$$ Make every such split in the outcome having the longer head. Different $v$ require different original segments, so these splits preserve disjointness in both outcomes. Their total gain is at most $D+D'$. Independence and <a href="#stab:sign:parent-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:parent-error">[stab:sign:parent-error]</a> yield <a id="stab:sign:parent-conflicts"></a>$$\label{stab:sign:parent-conflicts}
 2\sum_v p_vn_v\le1-L^{-2}.$$

Choose sign $+$ at $v$ if $p_v\ge n_v$ and sign $-$ otherwise. The minority masses $m_v=\min(p_v,n_v)$ satisfy <a id="stab:sign:parent-minority"></a>$$\label{stab:sign:parent-minority}
 \sum_vm_v^2\le\frac{1-L^{-2}}2.$$ In $Q_HA$, replace each coefficient $c_i$ by $s_i$. In the expectation of either law this changes the vector by at most $\sqrt{L^2-1}$, by <a href="#stab:sign:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:coefficients">[stab:sign:coefficients]</a>, contractivity of $Q_H$, and <a href="#stab:sign:parent-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:parent-error">[stab:sign:parent-error]</a>. Segments wholly outside $H$ disappear because $s_i=0$. Split the remaining expected crossing terms into $G_\pm+B_\pm$, retaining in $G_\pm$ the preferred parent sign and putting the other terms in $B_\pm$.

For children $g\in G_H$ of a fixed $v$, the sum of absolute coefficients in $B_\pm$ is at most $2m_v$: a nonnegative conditional expectation is at most twice its mixture expectation. Put $b_g=|B_\pm(e_g)|$. Formula <a href="#stab:sign:rooted-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:rooted-equality">[stab:sign:rooted-equality]</a>, followed by $\sum_g b_g^2\le(\sum_g b_g)^2$ within each parent group, gives $$\|B_\pm\|\le2\left(\sum_vm_v^2\right)^{1/2}
       \le2\sqrt{(1-L^{-2})/2}.$$ Thus $\pm u=G_\pm+E_\pm$, where $$\|E_\pm\|\le b_L,
 \qquad b_L=\sqrt{L^2-1}+2\sqrt{(1-L^{-2})/2}.$$ The two $G$ vectors have the same sign at each exit vertex. Lemma <a href="#stab:sign:rooted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:sign:rooted">40</a> therefore gives $\|G_+\|\le\|G_++G_-\|\le2b_L$, and hence $\|u\|\le3b_L$.

For a completed tail $u$, approximate it by $Q_Hu_j$ with finitely supported $u_j$. The strict endpoint hypothesis persists for all sufficiently large $j$. Passing to the limit proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

We use the averaged modulus $\widehat\delta_Z$ from <a href="#eq:averaged-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-modulus">[eq:averaged-modulus]</a>.

<a id="stmt-stab-sign-parent-modulus"></a>
<div id="stab:sign:parent-modulus" class="corollary">

**Corollary 66**. *Let $b_L=\sqrt{L^2-1}+2\sqrt{(1-L^{-2})/2}$ for $L>1$. If $0<t<1$ and $L$ is chosen so that $3b_L<t$, then $$\widehat\delta_Z(t)\ge (L-1)/8>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-sign-parent-modulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-sign-parent-modulus</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\delta=(L-1)/8$. Given a unit $x$, choose a finite-head unit $x_0$ with $\|x-x_0\|<\delta$, supported on an ancestral $H$. If $y\in\ker P_H$, $\|y\|\ge1$, and the average of $\|x\pm ty\|$ were at most $1+\delta$, the average at $x_0$ would be less than $1+2\delta$. Each endpoint at $x_0$ has norm at least one by $P_H$-contractivity. Their maximum would therefore be less than $1+4\delta<L$. Proposition <a href="#stab:sign:parent-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:sign:parent-lens">65</a> would give $t\le\|ty\|\le3b_L<t$, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stab-sign-forest-parent-lens"></a>
<div id="stab:sign:forest-parent-lens" class="corollary">

<a id="stab:sign:forest-parent-bound"></a>
**Corollary 67** (A forest lens from preferred parent signs). *In the forest space $X$, let $h=P_Hh$, $P_Hu=0$, and $h+u,h-u\in B_X$. Then $$\label{stab:sign:forest-parent-bound}
 \|u\|\le3(\sqrt2+2)\sqrt{1-\|h\|}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-sign-forest-parent-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-sign-forest-parent-lens</summary>

*Proof.* If $h=0$, then $\|u\|\le1$ proves the assertion. Otherwise fix a norming head test $p$, so $\rho(p)=1$ and $h(p)=\|h\|$. For $\epsilon>0$, approximate $h\pm u$ within $\epsilon$ by finite convex combinations $V_\pm=\mathbb E_\pm A$ of atoms. Put $d_\epsilon=1-\|h\|+\epsilon$ and mix the two laws equally. The individual evaluation deficits obey $\mathbb E_\pm L(A)\le d_\epsilon$. Therefore Lemma <a href="#stab:coef:pointwise" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:coef:pointwise">36</a> gives the prelimit budgets <a id="stab:sign:parent-approx-budget"></a>$$\label{stab:sign:parent-approx-budget}
 \mathbb E_\pm E(A)\le2d_\epsilon,\qquad
 \mathbb E_\pm D(A)\le2d_\epsilon.$$

Group only the crossings by their last head vertex $v$, and let $p_v,n_v$ be their positive and negative mean head-sum masses in the mixture. The simultaneous parent-indexed splitting in the proof of Proposition <a href="#stab:sign:parent-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:sign:parent-lens">65</a> gives $\sum_vp_vn_v\le d_\epsilon$. This step compares crossings that may leave through different children of the same parent. The proof applies in each forest component separately; untouched roots have no crossing term.

<a id="stab:sign:parent-approx-remainder"></a>
Choose the same preferred parent sign for both laws. Each law’s wrong-sign mass at $v$ is at most $2\min(p_v,n_v)$, so the sum of its squared parent masses is at most $4d_\epsilon$. Splitting each parent mass among its child gates cannot increase that square sum. Thus the discarded replaced tail has norm at most $2\sqrt{d_\epsilon}$ by <a href="#stab:sign:rooted-equality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:rooted-equality">[stab:sign:rooted-equality]</a>. Replacement itself costs at most $\sqrt{2d_\epsilon}$ under each law. If $M_\pm$ are the retained replaced tails, contractivity and the approximation errors give $$\label{stab:sign:parent-approx-remainder}
 \|\pm u-M_\pm\|
 \le\epsilon+(\sqrt2+2)\sqrt{d_\epsilon}.$$ The signs of $M_+$ and $M_-$ agree at every gate. Hence their rooted norm formula gives $\|M_+\|\le\|M_++M_-\|$, at most twice the right side of <a href="#stab:sign:parent-approx-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:parent-approx-remainder">[stab:sign:parent-approx-remainder]</a>. Adding the remaining error and letting $\epsilon\downarrow0$ proves <a href="#stab:sign:forest-parent-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:forest-parent-bound">[stab:sign:forest-parent-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-82"></a>
#### Majority signs at the first exit

From this point through the trimming argument we work in $B_\infty$, and take the finite ancestral heads to contain its root.

There is a second way to remove the minority contributions. We keep the original atom coefficients, assigning signs at individual exit vertices. The cost now separates into a wholly exterior part and a wrong-sign crossing part. This leads to a direct quadratic bound for the averaged modulus.

<a id="stmt-stab-sign-exit-lens"></a>
<div id="stab:sign:exit-lens" class="proposition">

**Proposition 68**. *Let $x=P_Hx$, $\|x\|=1$, and $y\in\ker P_H$. Put $C_{\mathrm{ex}}=2+3\sqrt2$. For every $\delta>0$, with $\eta=\delta/(1+\delta)$, <a id="stab:sign:exit-lens-bound"></a>$$\label{stab:sign:exit-lens-bound}
 \max\{\|x+y\|,\|x-y\|\}\le1+\delta
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \frac{\|y\|}{1+\delta}\le3C_{\mathrm{ex}}\sqrt\eta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-sign-exit-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-sign-exit-lens</summary>

<a id="stab:sign:exit-error"></a>
*Proof.* First suppose $y$ is supported on $K\setminus H$ for a finite ancestral $K\supset H$. By Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a>, represent $(x\pm y)/(1+\delta)$ as finite expectations $\mathbb E_\pm A$ of atoms $A=\sum_i c_id_{S_i}$ supported in $K$, where $\sum_i c_i^2\le1$. Choose a norming head test $p$ for $x$, set $s_i=p(S_i)$, and put $D_A=1-\sum_i s_i^2$. The shared identity <a href="#stab:deficit-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:deficit-identity">[stab:deficit-identity]</a> gives $$D_A+\sum_i(c_i-s_i)^2\le2(1-A(p)).$$ Both terms on the left are nonnegative. Since $\mathbb E_\pm A(p)=1/(1+\delta)$, both conditional laws and their equal mixture satisfy $$\label{stab:sign:exit-error}
 \mathbb E_\pm\sum_i(c_i-s_i)^2\le2\eta,
 \qquad \mathbb E_\pm D_A\le2\eta.$$

<a id="stab:sign:exit-minority"></a>
For a crossing at $g\in G_H\cap K$, denote its coefficients by $c_g=c_i$ and $s_g=s_i$, using zero for an absent crossing. Take independent outcomes from the mixture. The full opposite-sign comparison <a href="#stab:coef:head-conflict-eq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:coef:head-conflict-eq">[stab:coef:head-conflict-eq]</a>, applied to their head sums, gives $$2\sum_g(-s_gs'_g)_+\le D_A+D_{A'}.$$ Its expectation is $4\sum_g\mathbb E(s_g)_+\,\mathbb E(-s_g)_+$ on the left and at most $4\eta$ on the right. Consequently <a id="stab:sign:exit-conflicts"></a>$$\label{stab:sign:exit-conflicts}
 \sum_g\mathbb E(s_g)_+\,\mathbb E(-s_g)_+\le\eta.$$ Choose $\sigma_g\in\{-1,1\}$ to be a majority sign for $s_g$. The Euclidean norm of the vector of expected wrong-sign $s_g$ masses is at most $\sqrt\eta$, because $\min(P,Q)^2\le PQ$. Moreover, Jensen’s inequality in Euclidean space and <a href="#stab:sign:exit-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:exit-error">[stab:sign:exit-error]</a> imply $$\left\|(\mathbb E|c_g-s_g|)_g\right\|_2
       \le\sqrt{2\eta}.$$ Using $(-\sigma_gc_g)_+\le(-\sigma_gs_g)_++|c_g-s_g|$, we obtain $$\label{stab:sign:exit-minority}
 \left\|(\mathbb E(-\sigma_gc_g)_+)_g\right\|_2
       \le(1+\sqrt2)\sqrt\eta.$$ For either conditional law the bound is twice this large.

<a id="stab:sign:exit-discard"></a>
We now recover the tail vectors from these masses. Wholly exterior segments have $s_i=0$. Their expected vector in either law has norm at most $\sqrt{2\eta}$ by <a href="#stab:sign:exit-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:exit-error">[stab:sign:exit-error]</a> and <a href="#stab:sign:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:coefficients">[stab:sign:coefficients]</a>. A crossing at $g$ has exterior segment $L_g=[g,w]$. Its retained and discarded conditional means are $$g_{\pm,g}=\sigma_g\mathbb E_\pm
          [ (\sigma_g c_g)_+d_{L_g}],\qquad
 h_{\pm,g}=-\sigma_g\mathbb E_\pm
          [(-\sigma_g c_g)_+d_{L_g}],$$ with zero terms for absent crossings. Since $\|d_{L_g}\|=1$, <a href="#stab:cone-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:cone-bounds">[stab:cone-bounds]</a> and <a href="#stab:sign:exit-minority" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:exit-minority">[stab:sign:exit-minority]</a> bound $\|\sum_gh_{\pm,g}\|$ by $2(1+\sqrt2)\sqrt\eta$. Thus, with $g_\pm=\sum_gg_{\pm,g}$, $$\label{stab:sign:exit-discard}
 \pm\frac{y}{1+\delta}=g_\pm+r_\pm,
 \qquad \|r_\pm\|\le C_{\mathrm{ex}}\sqrt\eta.$$ To verify the required comparison explicitly, put $C_{\pm,g}=\mathbb E_\pm(\sigma_gc_g)_+$. Then $\|g_{\pm,g}\|\le C_{\pm,g}$ and $g_{\pm,g}(e_g)=\sigma_gC_{\pm,g}$. The upper and lower estimates in <a href="#stab:cone-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:cone-bounds">[stab:cone-bounds]</a> give $$\|g_+\|\le\|(C_{+,g})\|_2
 \le\|(C_{+,g}+C_{-,g})\|_2
 \le\|g_++g_-\|\le2C_{\mathrm{ex}}\sqrt\eta.$$ Adding the remainder in <a href="#stab:sign:exit-discard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:exit-discard">[stab:sign:exit-discard]</a> proves the claim for finite support.

For a general completed tail, let $K$ increase through a finite ancestral exhaustion containing $H$. Contractivity gives $\|x\pm P_Ky\|\le1+\delta$, and $P_Ky\to y$ in norm by coordinate-span density. The finite-support estimate passes to the limit with the same $\delta$. This uses exact finite closed-ball representations, rather than a strict-radius approximation. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stab-sign-exit-modulus"></a>
<div id="stab:sign:exit-modulus" class="corollary">

**Corollary 69**. *For $0<t<1$ and $C_{\mathrm{ex}}=2+3\sqrt2$, $$\widehat\delta_{B_\infty}(t)\ge\frac{t^2}{576C_{\mathrm{ex}}^2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-sign-exit-modulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-sign-exit-modulus</summary>

*Proof.* Set $\delta_t=(t/(12C_{\mathrm{ex}}))^2<1$. For a unit finite-head $x_0$ and $y\in\ker P_H$ with $\|y\|\ge1$, the two inequalities $\|x_0\pm ty\|\le1+\delta_t$ would imply $$\|ty\|\le3C_{\mathrm{ex}}\sqrt{\delta_t(1+\delta_t)}
       \le6C_{\mathrm{ex}}\sqrt{\delta_t}=t/2,$$ which is impossible. Each endpoint has norm at least one, so their average exceeds $1+\delta_t/2$. For an arbitrary unit $x$, choose a finite-head unit $x_0$ with $\|x-x_0\|<\delta_t/4$. The resulting average at $x$ exceeds $1+\delta_t/4$, uniformly on this tail subspace. Formula <a href="#eq:averaged-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-modulus">[eq:averaged-modulus]</a> gives the conclusion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stab:trim:section"></a>
### Trimming by maximal splitting gain

The next argument keeps the absolute crossing masses of the original coefficients. A crossing is discarded if its coefficient is too small, if it is poorly aligned with the head test, or if its head allows a large increase by splitting. The remaining crossings have one sign in each exit component. The point requiring care is the discarded mass: a small probability alone does not control its Euclidean norm.

<a id="stmt-stab-trim-vanishing-lens"></a>
<div id="stab:trim:vanishing-lens" class="proposition">

**Proposition 70**. *There are no sequences of finite ancestral sets $H_n$, finite-head unit vectors $x_n=P_{H_n}x_n$, and finitely supported $z_n\in\ker P_{H_n}$ such that $$\max\{\|x_n+z_n\|,\|x_n-z_n\|\}\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad \liminf_n\|z_n\|>0.$$ The same assertion holds with $z_n$ in the completed tail spaces.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-trim-vanishing-lens"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-trim-vanishing-lens</summary>

<a id="stab:trim:error"></a>
*Proof.* We first prove the finite-support assertion. Choose radii $R_n>\max\{\|x_n+z_n\|,\|x_n-z_n\|\}$ with $R_n\to1$. The gauge representation supplies two finite laws of atoms with means $(x_n\pm z_n)/R_n$. Pool them equally. Suppress the index $n$ and choose a norming head test $p$ for $x$. For an outcome $A=\sum_i c_id_{S_i}$, put $s_i=p(S_i)$, $$E_A=\sum_i(c_i-s_i)^2,\qquad D_A=1-\sum_i s_i^2.$$ Since $\mathbb E_\pm A(p)=1/R$, the same calculation as in <a href="#stab:sign:exit-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:exit-error">[stab:sign:exit-error]</a> gives $$\label{stab:trim:error}
 \mathbb E_\pm E_A\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \mathbb E_\pm D_A\longrightarrow0.$$ All expectations in what follows are finite, even though the tree has infinitely many children.

<a id="stab:trim:gain-budget"></a>
For a crossing at $g$, let $c_g,s_g$ be its coefficient and head sum. Its head is a suffix $[v,\operatorname{parent}(g)]$ of the ancestral path. Define $d_g\ge0$ as the maximum gain in the sum of squared $p$-sums obtainable by splitting this head at one place, leaving the exterior portion attached to the later piece. Include the option of no split, with gain zero. The head is finite, so this maximum exists. Define all three quantities to be zero if there is no crossing at $g$. Maximizing splits for different crossings act on distinct disjoint segments. Therefore $$\label{stab:trim:gain-budget}
 \sum_gd_g\le D_A.$$

<a id="stab:trim:exterior"></a>
Let $W$ be the part of $A$ consisting of wholly exterior segments. These have $s_i=0$, so $$\label{stab:trim:exterior}
 \|\mathbb E_\pm W\|\le(\mathbb E_\pm E_A)^{1/2}=o(1).$$ Also the Euclidean norm of the expected root-coordinate vector $(\mathbb EW(e_g))_g$ is $o(1)$. Indeed, exit vertices are incomparable, so a wholly exterior segment meets at most one of them; disjointness then bounds the root-coordinate norm by the Euclidean coefficient norm of the exterior segments.

Put $m_g=\mathbb E|c_g|$. We will show $\|(m_g)\|_2=o(1)$. Indices with $m_g=0$ can be omitted. Discard an observation at $g$ if any one of the following holds: <a id="stab:trim:tests"></a>$$\label{stab:trim:tests}
 |c_g|<m_g/4,\qquad
 |c_g-s_g|>m_g/8,\qquad
 \sqrt{d_g}\ge m_g/8.$$ The first condition removes an expected absolute mass at most $m_g/4$. For the union $B_g$ of the last two conditions, put $p_g=\mathbb P(B_g)$ and $b_g=\mathbb E[|c_g|1_{B_g}]$. Markov’s inequality, <a href="#stab:trim:error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:trim:error">[stab:trim:error]</a>, and <a href="#stab:trim:gain-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:trim:gain-budget">[stab:trim:gain-budget]</a> imply <a id="stab:trim:bad-probability"></a>$$\label{stab:trim:bad-probability}
 \sum_gm_g^2p_g
 \le64\mathbb E\sum_g\big((c_g-s_g)^2+d_g\big)=o(1).$$ Write the last quantity as $\varepsilon_n\to0$.

To bound actual mass removal, fix $\eta>0$ and separate the components according to $p_g>\eta$ or $p_g\le\eta$. On the first set, $b_g\le m_g$, hence $$\sum_{p_g>\eta}b_g^2\le\varepsilon_n/\eta.$$ On the second set, Cauchy–Schwarz gives $b_g^2\le p_g\mathbb E[c_g^21_{B_g}]$; therefore <a id="stab:trim:bad-mass"></a>$$\label{stab:trim:bad-mass}
 \sum_{p_g\le\eta}b_g^2
       \le\eta\mathbb E\sum_gc_g^2\le\eta,
 \qquad
 \|b\|_2^2\le\varepsilon_n/\eta+\eta.$$ Choose $\eta=\eta_n\to0$ slowly enough that $\varepsilon_n/\eta_n\to0$ (for example $\eta_n=\sqrt{\varepsilon_n}+1/n$). Thus $\|b\|_2=o(1)$.

Every surviving observation has $c_g$ and $s_g$ of the same sign, $|s_g|\ge m_g/8$, and $d_g<(m_g/8)^2$. If two survivors at one exit vertex had opposite signs, their head suffixes would be distinct and nested. Splitting the longer at the shorter would increase its squared sum by at least $2(m_g/8)^2$, contradicting the definition and bound of its maximal gain. Hence the survivors have one common sign at each $g$.

<a id="stab:trim:signed-mean"></a>
Let $a_g$ be the mass discarded by the first condition; thus $a_g\le m_g/4$. The total discarded mass is at most $a_g+b_g$, so the single-sign conclusion implies $$\label{stab:trim:signed-mean}
 |\mathbb Ec_g|\ge m_g-2(a_g+b_g)
                 \ge m_g/2-2b_g.$$ The mixture has mean $x/R$ and therefore zero exterior coordinates. At the exit roots its crossing part has values $\mathbb Ec_g$; the remaining values come from $\mathbb EW$. By <a href="#stab:trim:exterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:trim:exterior">[stab:trim:exterior]</a>, $\|(\mathbb Ec_g)_g\|_2=o(1)$. Equation <a href="#stab:trim:signed-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:trim:signed-mean">[stab:trim:signed-mean]</a> consequently yields <a id="stab:trim:mass-vanishes"></a>$$\label{stab:trim:mass-vanishes}
 \tfrac12\|m\|_2
       \le\|(\mathbb Ec_g)_g\|_2+2\|b\|_2=o(1).$$

The expected absolute crossing mass at each $g$ in either conditional law is at most $2m_g$. By <a href="#stab:sign:absolute-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:sign:absolute-masses">[stab:sign:absolute-masses]</a>, its expected crossing tail has norm at most $2\|m\|_2=o(1)$. Add the wholly exterior part from <a href="#stab:trim:exterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:trim:exterior">[stab:trim:exterior]</a>. Since the conditional tail means are $\pm z/R$, we obtain $\|z/R\|=o(1)$, the desired contradiction.

For completed tails, choose finitely supported approximations and apply $Q_{H_n}$, with errors tending to zero. Contractivity preserves the tail condition and the endpoint limits, while the lower norm bound persists. The finite-support contradiction applies. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stab-trim-modulus"></a>
<div id="stab:trim:modulus" class="corollary">

**Corollary 71**. *For the coordinate segment space, $\widehat\delta_{B_\infty}(t)>0$ for every $0<t<1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stab-trim-modulus"><summary>Proof of stmt-stab-trim-modulus</summary>

*Proof.* The modulus is nonnegative by convexity. If it vanished at $t$, there would be unit vectors $v_n$ for which the supremum over finite-codimensional subspaces in <a href="#eq:averaged-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-modulus">[eq:averaged-modulus]</a> tends to zero. Choose unit finite-head $x_n$ with $\|x_n-v_n\|<1/n$ and support contained in an ancestral $H_n$. Using $\ker P_{H_n}$ in that supremum, choose $z_n$ there with $\|z_n\|\ge t$ and $$\frac{\|x_n+z_n\|+\|x_n-z_n\|}{2}\longrightarrow1.$$ The choice of $z_n$ uses approximate infima; attainment is unnecessary. Both endpoint norms are at least one by projection, and therefore both converge to one. Approximation by finite tails gives, if desired, $\|z_n\|\ge t-o(1)$ with the same endpoint limit. This contradicts Proposition <a href="#stab:trim:vanishing-lens" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:trim:vanishing-lens">70</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:sequential-forest"></a>
### Sequential cancellation in the forest

Here $X=J^*$ is the finite-height forest space. The heads and the heights of their tree components may vary along a sequence. The argument therefore gives a uniform near-unit lens conclusion without selecting a numerical rate.

<a id="stmt-mol-finite-head-pair"></a>
<div id="mol:finite-head-pair" class="lemma">

**Lemma 72**. *Let $P$ be a bounded finite-rank projection on a Banach space and let $(u_j)$ be an infinite bounded sequence. For every $\varepsilon>0$ there are distinct $i,j$ with $\|P(u_i-u_j)\|<\varepsilon$. If in addition $x\pm u_j\in R B$ and $\|u_i-u_j\|\ge\theta$ for all distinct indices, then $v=(u_i-u_j)/2$ satisfies <a id="mol:half-difference"></a>$$\label{mol:half-difference}
 x\pm v\in R B,\qquad \|Pv\|<\varepsilon/2,
 \qquad \|v\|\ge\theta/2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mol-finite-head-pair"><summary>Proof of stmt-mol-finite-head-pair</summary>

*Proof.* The bounded set of images $Pu_j$ is totally bounded in the finite-dimensional range of $P$. Cover it by finitely many open balls of radius $\varepsilon/2$. Two images with distinct indices belong to the same ball. The norm bounds for their half-difference follow immediately, and $x+v=((x+u_i)+(x-u_j))/2$ and $x-v=((x-u_i)+(x+u_j))/2$ belong to $R B$ by convexity. ◻

</details>

<a id="mola:sequential-section"></a>
#### A sequential argument using the smaller squared crossing

The following argument uses only vanishing test deficits and the smaller of two squared crossing coefficients. It gives a qualitative estimate, with no choice of numerical rate built into the proof.

<a id="stmt-mola-sequential"></a>
<div id="mola:sequential" class="lemma">

**Lemma 73**. *For each positive integer $n$, let $H_n$ be a finite ancestor-closed subset of the finite-height forest, and let $x_n,y_n\in X$ satisfy $$P_{H_n}x_n=x_n,\qquad P_{H_n}y_n=0,\qquad
 \|x_n+y_n\|,\|x_n-y_n\|\le1.$$ If $\|x_n\|\longrightarrow1$, then $\|y_n\|\longrightarrow0$. The heads and the heights of the trees meeting them may vary with $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mola-sequential"><summary>Proof of stmt-mola-sequential</summary>

*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a>, choose $p_n=P_{H_n}p_n$ with $\rho(p_n)\le1$ and $x_n(p_n)=\|x_n\|$; take $p_n=0$ when $x_n=0$. Let $\eta_n>0$ tend to zero. Corollary <a href="#cor:closed-atomic-ball" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:closed-atomic-ball">8</a> provides approximations to $x_n+y_n$ and $x_n-y_n$ within $\eta_n$ by finite convex combinations of represented atoms $Z=\sum_i a_i d_{S_i}$, where the segments in each represented atom are pairwise disjoint and $\sum_i a_i^2\le1$. Sample the two combinations with equal probabilities, recorded by a sign $\sigma$. Thus $$\|\mathbb EZ-x_n\|\le\eta_n,\qquad
 \|\mathbb E(\sigma Z)-y_n\|\le\eta_n.$$ The probability space may depend on $n$, but it is finite for each $n$. Put $b_i=p_n(S_i)$, $\widehat Z=\sum_i b_i d_{S_i}$, $L=1-\sum_i b_i^2$, and $D=\sum_i(a_i-b_i)^2$. The defining segment test gives $L\ge0$, and expansion of the square gives $$L+D=1+\sum_i a_i^2-2Z(p_n)\le2(1-Z(p_n)).$$ Consequently, with $d_n=1-\|x_n\|+\eta_n\longrightarrow0$, $$\mathbb EL\le2d_n,\quad \mathbb ED\le2d_n,\quad
 \mathbb E\|Z-\widehat Z\|\le\sqrt{2d_n}.$$ Thus both corrected means approximate their targets with error $e_n=\eta_n+\sqrt{2d_n}\longrightarrow0$.

Let $G_n$ be the minimal nodes outside $H_n$. For a represented atom, write $A_r$ for the corrected coefficient of its crossing segment at $r\in G_n$, or zero if no such segment exists. There is at most one such segment at each $r$, and segments wholly outside $H_n$ have corrected coefficient zero. Hence $\sum_r A_r^2\le1$ pointwise. The gate-coordinate contraction and $x_n(e_r)=0$ imply $$\sum_r(\mathbb EA_r)^2\le e_n^2.$$ Only finitely many gates occur for each $n$.

Take an independent copy of the represented data, with crossing coefficients $B_r$ and deficit $L'$. Whenever $A_rB_r<0$, the two head portions end at the parent of $r$ and are properly nested. Split the longer head at the start of the shorter, in the family to which the longer head belongs. If the two sums are $a,b$, with $a$ corresponding to the longer head, the increase in the squared segment test is $$(a-b)^2+b^2-a^2=2b^2-2ab\ge\min\{a^2,b^2\}.$$ Splitting the actual segment into two retains its original endpoint; $p_n$ vanishes on its tail, so these are precisely the two new sums. Each segment crosses at only one gate. All assigned splits can therefore be performed simultaneously in each family and preserve disjointness. Their total increases are bounded by $L$ and $L'$. It follows that $$M_n:=\mathbb E\sum_{A_rB_r<0}\min\{A_r^2,B_r^2\}
 \le2\mathbb EL\longrightarrow0.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz on the product probability space and the finite set of gates now supplies the step from smaller squares to products: $$\begin{split}
 \mathbb E\sum_{A_rB_r<0}|A_rB_r|
 &\le\left(\mathbb E\sum_{A_rB_r<0}\min\{A_r^2,B_r^2\}\right)^{1/2}
       \left(\mathbb E\sum_{A_rB_r<0}\max\{A_r^2,B_r^2\}\right)^{1/2}\\
 &\le\sqrt{2M_n}\longrightarrow0.
 \end{split}$$ For $m_r=\mathbb E|A_r|$ and $h_r=\mathbb EA_r$, independence gives $$\sum_r m_r^2-\sum_r h_r^2
 =2\mathbb E\sum_{A_rB_r<0}|A_rB_r|\longrightarrow0.$$ Together with the signed-mean estimate this proves $\|(m_r)\|_2\to0$.

In the cone rooted at $r$, the corrected tail is a segment functional with coefficient $A_r$, or zero. Its signed expectation has norm at most $m_r$. Propositions <a href="#presentation:identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="presentation:identity">31</a> and <a href="#presentation:subtrees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="presentation:subtrees">33</a> therefore give $$\|Q_{H_n}\mathbb E(\sigma\widehat Z)\|\le\|(m_r)\|_2\longrightarrow0.$$ Restoring the error $e_n$ and using $Q_{H_n}y_n=y_n$ proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-mola-uniform-sequential"></a>
<div id="mola:uniform-sequential" class="corollary">

**Corollary 74**. *For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $\delta>0$ such that, for every finite ancestor-closed $H$ and all $x=P_Hx$, $y=Q_Hy$ with $\|x\pm y\|\le1$ and $\|x\|>1-\delta$, one has $\|y\|<\varepsilon$. The given norm is asymptotically midpoint uniformly convex, with the maximum of the two endpoint norms in the definition.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mola-uniform-sequential"><summary>Proof of stmt-mola-uniform-sequential</summary>

*Proof.* If the first statement failed, taking $\delta=1/n$ would produce a sequence contradicting Lemma <a href="#mola:sequential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mola:sequential">73</a>. This contradiction also proves that the choice is independent of the head and its height.

Fix $t>0$ and apply the first statement with $\varepsilon=t/2$. Choose $0<e<1$ so that $e/(1+e)<\delta$. If $u=P_Hu$ is a unit vector and $v=Q_Hv$ has norm $t$, the inequality $\max\{\|u+v\|,\|u-v\|\}\le1+e$ would allow its endpoints to be divided by $1+e$. The normalized center has norm greater than $1-\delta$, whereas the normalized displacement has norm $t/(1+e)>t/2$, a contradiction. Thus the maximum is greater than $1+e$ for every such $u,v$.

For any unit $x\in X$, choose a finite ancestor-closed $H$ such that $u=P_Hx/\|P_Hx\|$ is defined and $\|x-u\|<e/2$, using the contractive exhaustion in Proposition <a href="#presentation:identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="presentation:identity">31</a>. On the unit sphere of the finite-codimensional space $\ker P_H$, apply the preceding result to $v=ty$. It gives $$\max\{\|x+ty\|,\|x-ty\|\}>1+e/2.$$ Taking the infimum over that sphere, the supremum over finite-codimensional spaces, and the infimum over unit centers yields a midpoint modulus at least $e/2>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-mola-separated-sequential"></a>
<div id="mola:separated-sequential" class="corollary">

**Corollary 75**. *For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $\delta>0$ such that any infinite $\varepsilon$-separated family $(v_j)$ satisfying $x\pm v_j\in B_X$ has $\|x\|\le1-\delta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mola-separated-sequential"><summary>Proof of stmt-mola-separated-sequential</summary>

*Proof.* Otherwise choose such configurations indexed by $n$, with $\|x_n\|\to1$. Choose finite ancestor-closed $H_n$ with $\|Q_{H_n}x_n\|<1/n$. The sequence $(P_{H_n}v_j)$ is bounded in a finite-dimensional space. Hence two different indices $i,j$ have $\|P_{H_n}(v_i-v_j)/2\|<1/n$. Put $w_n=(v_i-v_j)/2$. Separation gives $\|w_n\|\ge\varepsilon/2$, and convexity of the two opposite endpoint pairs gives $x_n\pm w_n\in B_X$. The head center $a_n=P_{H_n}x_n$ and tail displacement $b_n=Q_{H_n}w_n$ satisfy $$\|a_n\pm b_n\|\le1+2/n,\quad
 \|a_n\|\ge\|x_n\|-1/n,\quad
 \|b_n\|\ge\varepsilon/2-1/n.$$ Dividing both by $1+2/n$ contradicts Lemma <a href="#mola:sequential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mola:sequential">73</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="molx:coordinate-null-section"></a>
#### Coordinatewise-null displacements

<a id="stmt-molx-coordinate-null"></a>
<div id="molx:coordinate-null" class="corollary">

**Corollary 76**. *Let $x\in X$ and let $(z_j)\subset X$ satisfy $x\pm z_j\in B_X$ and $z_j(e_v)\to0$ for every forest vertex $v$. Then, with $C=\sqrt2+2\sqrt6<7$, <a id="molx:coordinate-null-bound"></a>$$\label{molx:coordinate-null-bound}
 \limsup_{j\to\infty}\|z_j\|
 \le C\sqrt{1-\|x\|}\le7\sqrt{1-\|x\|}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-molx-coordinate-null"><summary>Proof of stmt-molx-coordinate-null</summary>

<a id="molx:coordinate-null-input"></a>
*Proof.* First recall the separate-endpoint bound for a finite ancestral head: if $a=P_Ha$, $P_Hb=0$, and $\|a\pm b\|\le R$, then $$\label{molx:coordinate-null-input}
 \|b\|\le C\sqrt{R(R-\|a\|)}.$$ For $a\ne0$ choose a norming head test by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-hull">7</a> and apply <a href="#stab:common-test-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stab:common-test-bound">[stab:common-test-bound]</a> with $d=1-\|a\|/R$. Both endpoints have the same test value $\|a\|$, since the test vanishes on $b$; thus the individual replacement cost is $\sqrt{2d}$ and the conditional crossing cost is $2\sqrt{6d}$. Scaling gives the displayed inequality. If $a=0$ or $R=0$, it follows directly from $\|b\|\le R$.

Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:forest-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forest-structure">2</a> supplies a finite ancestral $H$ with $\|Q_Hx\|\le\varepsilon$. Coordinatewise convergence on this fixed finite set implies $\|P_Hz_j\|\le\varepsilon$ for all sufficiently large $j$. For those indices, $$\|P_Hx\pm Q_Hz_j\|
 \le\|x\pm z_j\|+\|Q_Hx\|+\|P_Hz_j\|
 \le1+2\varepsilon.$$ Applying <a href="#molx:coordinate-null-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molx:coordinate-null-input">[molx:coordinate-null-input]</a> with $R=1+2\varepsilon$ gives <a id="molx:coordinate-null-approximation"></a>$$\label{molx:coordinate-null-approximation}
 \|z_j\|\le\varepsilon+
 C\sqrt{(1+2\varepsilon)(1+2\varepsilon-\|P_Hx\|)}.$$ Take the upper limit in $j$ with this head fixed. Since $\|P_Hx\|\ge\|x\|-\varepsilon$, the right side is at most $\varepsilon+C\sqrt{(1+2\varepsilon)(1-\|x\|+3\varepsilon)}$. Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ proves the result. The argument uses only convergence of the finitely many coordinates on each chosen head. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:qualitative-infinite"></a>
### Qualitative midpoint arguments in infinite height

We work in the coordinate predual $B_\infty$ of Section <a href="#binf:definition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:definition">3</a>, with the distinction from the full dual specified in Remark <a href="#presentation:infinite-height" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="presentation:infinite-height">35</a>. The averaged modulus is defined in <a href="#eq:averaged-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-modulus">[eq:averaged-modulus]</a>. Three different reductions lead to a vanishing-deficit contradiction: adding a supporting kernel, normalizing the displacement twice, and normalizing the two endpoints separately.

<a id="qual:conditional-recovery"></a>
The common recovery step is Theorem <a href="#stab:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:weighted">41</a>. To make its inputs explicit, let two finite laws of represented atoms have means $w_\pm$, and choose a head test $p=P_Hp$ with $\rho(p)\le1$. Put $$\delta_\pm=1-w_\pm(p),\qquad
 \delta=\frac{\delta_++\delta_-}{2},\qquad
 \eta=\left\|Q_H\frac{w_++w_-}{2}\right\|.$$ Use the toolkit’s squared-test slack $D$ and squared coefficient error $E$ for these laws and this test. The pointwise budget and Theorem <a href="#stab:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stab:weighted">41</a>, with weights $1/2$, give <a id="qual:conditional-budget"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_\pm(D+E)&\le2\delta_\pm,
       \label{qual:conditional-budget}\\
 \|Q_Hw_\pm\|&\le
 2\sqrt{(\eta+\sqrt{2\delta})^2+4\delta}
       +\sqrt{2\delta_\pm}.
       \label{qual:conditional-recovery}
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $\delta_\pm\to0$ and $\eta\to0$ force both conditional tails to vanish, even when the heads, tests, and finite laws vary. The first two reductions below give an exactly zero pooled tail. Separate endpoint normalization gives a residual tail whose norm must also be shown to tend to zero. The conditional budgets record what each normalization preserves in the atom representations.

<a id="mold:qualitative"></a>
#### A qualitative contradiction with a supporting kernel

Suppose that $\widehat\delta_{B_\infty}(t)=0$ for some $0<t<1$. For each positive integer $n$, choose a unit center $v\in B_\infty$ whose centerwise supremum in the definition of this modulus is less than $1/n$, and a finitely supported unit vector $x$ with $\|x-v\|<1/n$. Let $A$ be a finite initial set supporting $x$, and choose $f\in B_\infty^*$ with $\|f\|=f(v)=1$. In $\ker P_A\cap\ker f$, choose $y$ of norm at least one such that $$\frac{\|v+ty\|+\|v-ty\|}{2}<1+2/n.$$ The supporting-functional condition makes each endpoint norm at least one; hence both are less than $1+4/n$. Approximate $ty$ by a finitely supported $u\in\ker P_A$ within $\min\{t/2,1/n\}$, as allowed by Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a>. Then $$\|u\|\ge t/2,\qquad
 \max\{\|x+u\|,\|x-u\|\}\le1+6/n.$$

Choose $r\ge1$ dominating these endpoint norms, with $r\to1$, and use Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a> to represent $(x\pm u)/r$ exactly by finite convex combinations of atoms in a finite ancestral set containing their supports. Choose a head test $p=P_Ap$ with $\rho(p)=x(p)=1$. For the means $w_\pm=(x\pm u)/r$, $$\delta_\pm=1-1/r\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 Q_A\frac{w_++w_-}{2}=Q_A(x/r)=0.$$ In particular, each law has $\mathbb E_\pm(D+E)\le2(1-1/r)\to0$. Equation <a href="#qual:conditional-recovery" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="qual:conditional-recovery">[qual:conditional-recovery]</a> now gives $\|u\|/r=\|Q_Aw_+\|\to0$, contradicting $\|u\|\ge t/2$. Thus the averaged modulus is positive.

<a id="mold:qualitative-normalized"></a>
#### A qualitative route with two normalizations

A second reduction uses the radial monotonicity of the symmetric average. Suppose that $\widehat\delta_{B_\infty}(t)=0$ at a fixed $0<t<1$. Choose unit centers in $B_\infty$ whose centerwise suprema tend to zero; approximate each by a finitely supported unit vector $x$ with error tending to zero, and let $H$ be a finite initial set containing its support. In $\ker P_H$, the infimum provides a vector $y$ of norm at least one for which the endpoint average at $x$, with displacement $ty$, is $$\frac{\|x+ty\|+\|x-ty\|}{2}\le1+o(1).$$ For a fixed direction, the symmetric average $$s\longmapsto\frac{\|x+sy\|+\|x-sy\|}{2}$$ is even and convex, hence nondecreasing for $s\ge0$. Replacing $y$ by $y/\|y\|$ therefore does not increase that average. Next approximate this unit vector in $\ker P_H$ by finite vectors, using Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a>, and normalize the approximants again. Their errors can be made to tend to zero, so we obtain finite unit $y\in\ker P_H$ with the same $1+o(1)$ bound.

Both endpoint norms are at least one by $P_H$, and therefore both tend to one. Choose $r$ strictly larger than their maximum, with $r\to1$. The finite vectors $(x\pm ty)/r$ have norm strictly less than one, so the strict gauge assertion in Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a> gives exact finite convex representations. Choose a head test $p=P_Hp$ with $\rho(p)=x(p)=1$. Each mean $w_\pm=(x\pm ty)/r$ has evaluation $1/r$, so its own coefficient budget is $\mathbb E_\pm(D+E)\le2(1-1/r)\to0$. The pooled mean is $x/r$, whose tail vanishes. Thus $\delta_\pm=1-1/r\to0$ and $\eta=0$ in <a href="#qual:conditional-recovery" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="qual:conditional-recovery">[qual:conditional-recovery]</a>. It follows that $\|Q_Hw_\pm\|\to0$, whereas these norms equal $t/r\to t>0$. This contradiction proves positivity.

<a id="molc:sequential-section"></a>
#### A sequential argument with separate endpoint normalizations

We now prove the averaged midpoint property of $B_\infty$ qualitatively, normalizing the two endpoints separately.

We use the averaged modulus defined in <a href="#eq:averaged-modulus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-modulus">[eq:averaged-modulus]</a>, with the inner infimum taken over directions of norm at least one. Its inner expression is nonnegative by the triangle inequality.

<a id="stmt-molc-sequential-positive"></a>
<div id="molc:sequential-positive" class="proposition">

**Proposition 77**. *For each fixed $0<t<1$, the space $B_\infty$ satisfies $\widehat\delta_{B_\infty}(t)>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-molc-sequential-positive"><summary>Proof of stmt-molc-sequential-positive</summary>

<a id="molc:sequential-tail-bounded"></a>
*Proof.* Suppose instead that $\widehat\delta_{B_\infty}(t)=0$. There are unit vectors $x_n$ for which the supremum over finite-codimensional subspaces is less than $1/n$. Approximate $x_n$ within $1/n$ by a finitely supported unit vector $a_n$, and let $A_n$ be a finite nonempty initial set containing its support. Taking the subspace $\ker P_{A_n}$ in the definition, choose $y_n$ there with $\|y_n\|\ge1$ and $$\frac{\|x_n+ty_n\|+\|x_n-ty_n\|}{2}\le1+2/n.$$ By Lemma <a href="#binf:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:projections">4</a>, choose a finite vector $z_n\in\ker P_{A_n}$ within $1/n$ of $ty_n$. It follows that $$\|z_n\|\ge t-1/n,\qquad
 \frac{\|a_n+z_n\|+\|a_n-z_n\|}{2}\le1+4/n.$$ Put $r_n^\pm=\|a_n\pm z_n\|$. Projection onto the head shows that $r_n^\pm\ge1$, so both sequences tend to $1$. Moreover, $$\label{molc:sequential-tail-bounded}
 \|z_n\|\le\frac{r_n^++r_n^-}{2}\le1+4/n.$$ This boundedness is needed when the two normalizations are compared.

For each $n$, take a finite initial set $B_n\supseteq A_n$ containing both supports. Choose a head test $p_n=P_{A_n}p_n$ with $\rho(p_n)=a_n(p_n)=1$. Lemma <a href="#binf:finite-hull" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:finite-hull">5</a> gives two finite laws of represented molecules $G=\sum_i\lambda_i\mathbf1_{S_i}$ satisfying $$\mathbb E_{n,\pm}G=(a_n\pm z_n)/r_n^\pm.$$ Write $w_{n,\pm}$ for these means. Their deficits on $p_n$ are $\delta_{n,\pm}=1-1/r_n^\pm\to0$. With $D,E$ computed using $p_n$, each law retains the budget <a id="molc:sequential-deficits"></a>$$\label{molc:sequential-deficits}
 \mathbb E_{n,\pm}(D+E)
 \le2\left(1-(\mathbb E_{n,\pm}G)(p_n)\right)
 =2(1-1/r_n^\pm)\longrightarrow0.$$ Write $Q_n=I-P_{A_n}$ and let $\mathbb E_n$ denote the equally weighted mixture. Its tail is <a id="molc:unequal-normalization-tail"></a>$$\label{molc:unequal-normalization-tail}
 \mathbb E_n Q_nG
 =\frac12\left(\frac1{r_n^+}-\frac1{r_n^-}\right)z_n.$$ It need not vanish exactly. Its norm $\eta_n$ tends to zero because $r_n^\pm\to1$ and <a href="#molc:sequential-tail-bounded" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="molc:sequential-tail-bounded">[molc:sequential-tail-bounded]</a> bounds $\|z_n\|$. Apply <a href="#qual:conditional-recovery" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="qual:conditional-recovery">[qual:conditional-recovery]</a> with the head $A_n$, the test $p_n$, the deficits $\delta_{n,\pm}$, and this $\eta_n$. It gives $\|Q_nw_{n,\pm}\|\to0$. These norms are $\|z_n\|/r_n^\pm$, which cannot tend to zero because $\|z_n\|\ge t-1/n$ and $r_n^\pm\to1$. This contradiction proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

The obstruction to an equivalent asymptotically uniformly convex norm is Proposition <a href="#binf:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="binf:obstruction">16</a>, an instance of the general criterion in Proposition <a href="#foundation:bounded-tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="foundation:bounded-tree">14</a>.

      "caption": "Opposite $p$-sums at a gate $g$, with $p$ supported in $H$. The two heads on the left belong to different segment families; their traces are displaced to show their nesting on the same ancestral chain. Their tails are schematic and need not coincide. On the right, the longer segment is split before $a′$; the lower piece retains that segment’s original tail and has $p$-sum $b′$. The squared segment test increases by at least $2|bb′|$.",

(1.1,2.2) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=left:\\(v\\)] at (0,0.8) {};\n  \\node[vertex] at (1.1,0.8) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=left:\\(g\\)] at (0,0) {};\n  \\node[vertex] at (1.1,0) {};\n  \\node[anchor=west,text=blue!65!black] at (0.15,3.1) {\\(b\\)};\n  \\node[anchor=west,text=red!65!black] at (1.25,1.5) {\\(b'\\)};\n  \\node at (1.1,-1.3) {\\(bb'<0\\)};\n longer segment};\n    \\draw[gray!70,densely dotted] (-0.6,0.4)--(3.2,0.4);\n    \\draw[head] (0,3.8)--(0,2.7);\n    \\draw[second] (0,2.2)--(0,0.8);\n    \\draw[second,tail] (0,0.8)--(0,-0.7);\n    \\node[vertex,label=left:\\(a\\)] at (0,3.8) {};\n    \\node[vertex] at (0,2.7) {};\n    \\node[vertex,label=left:\\(a'\\)] at (0,2.2) {};\n    \\node[vertex,label=left:\\(v\\)] at (0,0.8) {};\n       "witness_description": "Two schematic line-segment diagrams labeled \"Two crossing heads\" (left) and \"Splitting the longer segment\" (right), connected by a horizontal right-pointing arrow labeled \"Split before $a'$\". Both diagrams share a common vertical coordinate frame with marked levels $a$, $a'$, $v$, and $g$ from top to bottom, and a horizontal dashed line through $g$ separating region $H$ (above) from $H^c$ (below). In the left diagram, a blue vertical line segment runs from $a$ down to $v$ labeled $b$, and a slightly displaced red vertical line segment runs from $a'$ through $v$ and $g$ into $H^c$ labeled $b'$; below is the inequality $bb' < 0$. In the right diagram, the longer segment is split at $a'$: an upper blue segment runs from $a$ to $a'$ labeled \"sum $b - b'$\", and a lower red segment runs from $a'$ through $v$ and $g$ into $H^c$ labeled \"sum $b'$\"; below is the label \"Gain $\\ge 2|bb'|$\"."
